These schedules are starting seeds. NPCs can break schedule for emergencies, quests, relationships, weather, crime, monster attacks, illness, guild orders, or player actions.

##### Starter Quest Time Availability

Starter quests should appear when the right NPC is available and the right need exists.

| Quest | Best Time | Night Version |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Bread, Water, and First Footsteps | Dawn through Evening | Leftover food, tavern meal, stolen food, or Mara's guarded kitchen. |
| Find Your Feet in Eldoria | Morning through Afternoon | Ask guards, thieves, tavern informants, or buy a worse map. |
| Green Edge Gathering | Morning through Afternoon | Riskier gathering, nocturnal herbs, stealth travel, more danger. |
| The First Honest Sale | Morning through Evening | Taverns, fences, late stalls, or black market if unlocked. |
| Slimes in the Tall Grass | Morning through Evening | Slimes harder to see, higher risk, possible ghost or poison slime rumors. |
| The Guild Row Walk | Morning through Afternoon | Closed doors, night guards, rumors, taverns, hidden guild whispers. |
| A Small Kindness | Any time | More urgent at night; danger, hunger, and crime rise. |
| The Mark That Wasn't There | Any time | Stronger chance during Night or Deep Night. |

Time should create different mood, not block all play.

##### Time-Based Inventory Consumption

NPC inventories should be consumed by routine.

Examples:

- Bessa consumes flour, water, honey, and firewood during Dawn baking.
- Mara consumes food, water, bowls, and firewood during meal service.
- Sister Ameline consumes herbs, bandages, candles, and clean water while treating patients.
- Pell consumes parchment and ink when making maps.
- Brindle consumes slime gel, scrap, and repair tools while fixing gear.
- Tollen consumes seeds, water, tools, and fence materials during farm work.
- Saffi consumes arrows, rope, markers, and field reports during trail work.

When inventory drops below threshold, the NPC can generate a request. When inventory is restored, the request closes.

##### Food Deterioration and Storage

Food should deteriorate if it is not properly stored. This gives survival, cooking, trade, taverns, bakeries, farms, hunters, meal halls, and magical storage real value.

Food freshness states:

| Freshness State | Meaning | Gameplay Effect |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fresh | Recently gathered, cooked, caught, baked, or preserved | Full hunger/thirst value, full market value. |
| Stale | Edible but lower quality | Reduced value, lower morale, weaker food buffs. |
| Spoiled | Unsafe or unpleasant | Can cause sickness, nausea, poison risk, or social penalties if served. |
| Rotten | Dangerous waste | Usually inedible; can become bait, compost, poison input, cleaner slime food, or disease source. |

Base deterioration should depend on food type.

| Food Type | Loose Inventory | Proper Container | Preserved or Cooled Storage | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Raw fish | Spoils after 1 in-game day | 2 in-game days | 5 in-game days | Valuable near water but risky to carry long. |
| Raw meat | Spoils after 1 in-game day | 2 in-game days | 5 in-game days | Can be dried, salted, smoked, cooked, or frozen. |
| Cooked meals | Stale after 1 in-game day, spoiled after 2 | 3 in-game days | 5 to 7 in-game days | Taverns and meal halls depend on turnover. |
| Bread | Stale after 2 in-game days, spoiled after 4 | 5 in-game days | 7 in-game days | Good starter food, but not permanent. |
| Wild fruit | Spoils after 2 in-game days | 4 in-game days | 7 in-game days | Can become preserves. |
| Wild vegetables | Spoils after 3 in-game days | 5 in-game days | 7 in-game days | Useful for meal halls and farms. |
| Herbs | Wilt after 3 in-game days | 5 in-game days | 10 in-game days if dried | Freshness affects alchemy and medicine quality. |
| Honey | Very slow deterioration | Very slow deterioration | Very slow deterioration | Natural preservative and trade good. |
| Jerky or dried food | 7 in-game days | 14 in-game days | 30 in-game days | Strong travel food. |
| Magically preserved food | Depends on enchantment | Depends on enchantment | Can last weeks or longer | Requires runes, Amera blessing, magic container, or special storage. |

Storage quality should matter.

Storage examples:

- Loose backpack: no protection.
- Basic pouch: slight protection from dirt and handling.
- Food pouch: better protection for small food.
- Chest: protects from handling but not heat or pests.
- Cool cellar: slows spoilage.
- Sealed jar: protects from air, pests, and water.
- Oil-sealed container: useful for preserved foods.
- Ice box or Amera-blessed container: strong cooling effect.
- Magical storage box: can greatly slow or pause deterioration if enchanted for preservation.

Spoiled food should not simply vanish. It can create new gameplay.

Spoiled food uses:

- Compost for farming.
- Bait for traps, fishing, slimes, insects, or monsters.
- Cleaner slime food.
- Poison or disease alchemy input.
- Evidence of neglect in taverns, orphanages, warehouses, or meal halls.
- Quest trigger for sickness, bad merchants, or food fraud.

NPCs should track food freshness. A baker, tavern owner, farmer, merchant, Church healer, or meal hall cook may generate quests when food spoils, storage fails, a cellar breaks, or a shipment arrives too late.

##### Resource Respawn and Restoration Timing

Resource respawn should use the in-game calendar.

Core timing rules:

| Resource or Structure | Respawn or Restoration Time | Real-Time Equivalent | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ore nodes | 3 in-game days | About 10 hours and 17 minutes real time | Covers surface veins, common ore nodes, and beginner mining routes. |
| Plants and herbs | 5 in-game days | About 17 hours and 9 minutes real time | Covers common herbs, grass fiber, wild vegetables, wild fruit, and gatherable plants. |
| Trees | 1 in-game week | 24 real hours | Covers chopped trees, managed groves, softwood, and small forests. |
| Terrain damage | 1 in-game week | 24 real hours | Covers standard holes, broken paths, small landslides, and reshaped beginner terrain. |
| Damaged buildings | 1 in-game week | 24 real hours | Covers routine repairs, civic rebuilding, and non-story structural damage. |

This timing represents more than nature. Huwan has NPC labor, civic maintenance, guild work, druidic aid, rune work, earth magic, repair magic, city contracts, and subtle world-mending magic.

Respawn and restoration sources:

- Natural regrowth
- Farmer and gatherer labor
- Contractor and builder work
- Druidic care
- Rune-stabilized land
- Earth magic
- Wood magic
- Church charity repair drives
- Merchant-funded repairs
- Noble-funded civic works
- Mages Guild emergency stabilization
- Local world-mending magic

Restoration should not erase major story consequences too quickly. If a building is destroyed by a major quest, dragon attack, faction war, player siege, divine curse, or server event, it can require special repairs, materials, money, magic, labor contracts, or story resolution before the normal weekly restoration applies.

The default restoration timer is for normal gameplay damage and harvested resources, not major plot scars.

##### Time-Based Quest Cooldowns

Quest cooldowns should be measured in in-game time.

| Quest Need | Suggested Cooldown |
| --- | --- |
| Meal food shortage | 1 in-game day if meals are consumed. |
| Water shortage | 1 in-game day during normal conditions, faster during heat or crisis. |
| Herb shortage | 1 to 3 in-game days depending on patient load. |
| Crafting material shortage | Until production consumes stock, usually 1 to 5 in-game days. |
| Ore shortage | Rechecks after 3 in-game days or when new ore shipments arrive. |
| Plant and herb node depletion | Rechecks after 5 in-game days. |
| Tree, terrain, or building restoration | Rechecks after 1 in-game week. |
| Tool repair | 3 to 7 in-game days unless theft, disaster, or heavy use occurs. |
| Map stock shortage | 1 to 7 in-game days depending on sales and battle fog demand. |
| Slime population problem | Rechecks every 1 to 3 in-game days. |
| Relationship interruption | Event-based, not purely time-based. |
| Dawnspire hint | Event-based with higher chance at Night, Deep Night, or after strange magic behavior. |

Because one real day equals one in-game week, a player who returns the next real day should find that the city has meaningfully moved forward.

##### Quest Urgency and Consequence Escalation

Quest urgency should be driven by server time, NPC needs, resource condition, danger level, and social promises.

Not every quest needs a hard timer, but every active problem should be allowed to change if the world moves on without the player.

Urgency levels:

| Urgency | Meaning | Player-Facing Behavior |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Open | No immediate pressure | Quest remains available while the need exists. |
| Timed | Has a known deadline | Quest log shows remaining time. |
| Urgent | Situation is worsening | HUD or quest log highlights the quest more strongly. |
| Emergency | Failure or major harm is close | Quest becomes high priority and may notify involved parties. |
| Resolving | Another player or NPC is handling it | Quest may close, shrink, or change objective. |
| Failed | The chance passed or the problem became something else | Quest moves to failed, expired, or transformed state. |

Deadline types:

- Soft deadline: Better rewards, trust, prices, or outcomes if completed quickly.
- Hard deadline: The quest fails, transforms, or is completed by someone else after the timer ends.
- Hidden deadline: The player does not know the exact time unless they have enough information.
- World deadline: The event continues whether or not the player accepted the quest.
- Promise deadline: The timer begins when the player agrees to help.

Consequence escalation examples:

| Problem | If Solved Quickly | If Delayed | If Ignored |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Food shortage | NPC trust rises, meal service continues | Food prices rise, NPC stress rises | Hunger spreads, debt rises, Dawnmere or criminals exploit need. |
| Medicine shortage | Patient improves, healer trusts player | Illness worsens, medicine prices rise | Sickness spreads, death risk rises, Church or factions intervene. |
| Slime crop problem | Crops saved, farmer pays fairly | Crop damage grows, slime population rises | Food supply drops, market prices rise, larger slime quest appears. |
| Broken tool | Work resumes quickly | Production slows | NPC loses income, debt or firing risk rises. |
| Stolen goods | Owner trusts player, evidence remains fresh | Trail gets colder, thief sells item | Quest becomes investigation, revenge, black market, or debt problem. |
| Damaged building | Cheap repair, small reward | Repair cost rises, services close | Building stays unusable until weekly repair, guild contract, or special quest. |
| Escort request | Caravan leaves safely | Caravan waits and loses money | Caravan leaves without player, gets attacked, or another group takes the job. |

Escalation should update:

- NPC inventory
- NPC wealth
- NPC schedule
- NPC memory
- Relationship values
- Market supply
- Market prices
- Guild boards
- Faction influence
- Local reputation
- Future quest options
- World event state

The quest log should clearly show urgency when the player knows it. If the player does not know the problem is urgent, the quest may appear normal until new information is discovered.

##### Party and Group Quest Sharing

If a player or NPC is in a party, group, squad, crew, caravan, guild team, or temporary work party, the whole group should be able to see each other's active shared quests.

Shared quest information should include:

- Quest name
- Quest giver
- Objective steps
- Current progress
- Time limit
- Urgency level
- Known rewards
- Known risks
- Required items
- Required destination
- Map marker or search area, if known
- Battle fog limitations
- Who accepted the quest
- Who is allowed to turn it in
- Reward split rules

This allows party members to complete quests on someone else's behalf.

Example:

- Player A accepts a request from Mara for vegetables.
- Player A joins a party.
- Player B sees the quest, gathers vegetables, and delivers them.
- Mara's inventory updates.
- Player B receives an agreed cut.
- Player A receives completion credit or partial credit depending on the party rules.
- Mara remembers who accepted the promise and who actually helped.

NPCs in parties or work groups should follow the same logic. If an NPC caravan guard, hired helper, companion, tamed creature handler, or guild worker is grouped with the player, their relevant tasks and urgent needs can appear to the group if trust and permissions allow it.

##### Party Quest Permissions

Not every quest should automatically expose every secret.

Quest sharing should depend on permission and quest type.

| Quest Type | Sharing Rule |
| --- | --- |
| Public guild quest | Visible to the party by default. |
| Basic resource request | Visible to the party by default. |
| Market delivery | Visible if party trade sharing is enabled. |
| Personal favor | Visible only if the player shares it or party trust is high. |
| Criminal contract | Hidden unless shared, party is criminal-linked, or Thieves Guild rules allow it. |
| Noble secret | Hidden unless shared or the party has clearance. |
| Romance or soul mate quest | Private by default. |
| Dawnspire or Mages Guild clue | Private or partially hidden unless magically shared. |
| Divine, curse, or relic quest | Sharing depends on lore, danger, and discovery state. |

Private quests can still show vague alerts to the group if the player chooses, such as "Party member has an urgent private objective" without revealing details.

##### Completing Quests on Another's Behalf

A party member can complete a quest on behalf of the original quest holder if:

- The quest allows delegation.
- The party member has access to the objective.
- The item, target, NPC, or location accepts party fulfillment.
- The original quest holder has not marked the quest private or personal-only.
- The quest giver accepts third-party help.

Some NPCs may care who actually completes the work.

Examples:

- Mara may appreciate anyone who brings food, but remember the person who promised it.
- A noble may refuse delivery from anyone except the named courier.
- The Thieves Guild may punish outsiders who complete a contract without permission.
- A Church confession task may be personal-only.
- A tamed creature bond task may require the actual bonded player.

##### Reward Cuts and Party Splits

When a party member completes a quest on someone else's behalf, they may take a cut of the reward.

Default reward logic:

- Solo completion: the completer receives the reward.
- Quest-holder delegation: the completer receives an agreed cut and the quest holder receives credit.
- Partial contribution: contributors receive shares based on delivered items, damage, healing, protection, crafting, or travel support.
- Full party involvement: rewards are split according to the party leader's discretion.

Party leader split options:

- Equal split
- Contribution-based split
- Need-based split
- Role-based split
- Manual split
- Winner takes main reward, party shares gold
- Quest holder takes reputation, party shares gold/items
- Guild rule split, if the quest is guild-controlled

The party leader may control the final split when the entire party is involved, but the game should record contribution and reputation consequences. A leader who repeatedly cheats party members may gain distrust, rivalry, hatred, debt, guild complaints, or party abandonment.

Reward types that can split:

- Gold
- Items
- Materials
- Market credit
- Guild credit
- Job XP
- Class XP
- Skill XP
- Reputation
- Relationship gain
- Map knowledge
- Quest chain credit

Some rewards should not split evenly.

Examples:

- A unique item may go to one player.
- A relationship gain may favor the player who spoke kindly.
- A reputation reward may favor the quest holder, completer, or visible hero.
- Skill XP should favor the player who performed the action.
- Hidden Dawnspire progress should usually remain personal unless a magical group event occurs.

##### Raid Battle Rewards

Raid battles should use a special reward system. A raid is larger than a normal party quest and may involve multiple parties, NPC allies, guild teams, caravans, city defenders, or whole server events.

Raid reward logic should track contribution instead of rewarding only attendance.

Raid reward factors:

| Reward Factor | Meaning | Possible Reward Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Last Hit | Player, NPC, party, or raid group that lands the final decisive blow. | Bonus loot roll, title progress, fame burst, trophy claim. |
| Most Damage | Highest total valid damage dealt to the boss or priority targets. | Damage award, rare material chance, combat reputation. |
| Most Healing | Highest useful healing, cleansing, or resurrection contribution. | Support award, Church or healer reputation, healing item bonus. |
| Most Protection | Highest useful shielding, blocking, damage prevention, or tanking contribution. | Defender award, armor material chance, knight or guard reputation. |
| Most Threat Held | Player or group that controlled boss attention without exploiting. | Tank award, endurance XP, defensive title progress. |
| Objective Contribution | Completing raid mechanics, disabling runes, saving NPCs, carrying relics, closing portals, breaking siege engines. | Objective award, unique quest credit, guild recognition. |
| Crowd Control | Useful stuns, slows, binds, dispels, interrupts, positioning, or add control. | Control award, rune or magic material chance. |
| Revives and Rescue | Reviving, stabilizing, dragging allies to safety, preventing wipe conditions. | Rescue award, relationship gain, support reputation. |
| Resource Support | Supplying arrows, potions, food, repairs, maps, siege tools, or crafting during the raid. | Logistics award, merchant or guild credit. |
| Leadership | Organizing groups, calling mechanics, assigning roles, and keeping the raid alive. | Leadership reputation, command XP, title progress. |
| Discovery | Finding the raid entrance, weakness, map, lore, or boss mechanic before or during the raid. | Scout award, map reward, lore unlock, exploration XP. |

Raid rewards should include a base participation reward plus contribution bonuses.

Base participation reward requires:

- Being present for a meaningful portion of the raid.
- Avoiding inactivity.
- Contributing to combat, support, objectives, logistics, scouting, healing, defense, or rescue.
- Not intentionally sabotaging the raid.

Possible raid rewards:

- Gold
- Rare materials
- Boss materials
- Monster cores
- Relics or relic fragments
- Unique equipment
- Crafting recipes
- Titles
- Guild credit
- Faction reputation
- World Popularity
- World Favor
- Raid currency or tokens
- Housing trophies
- Map reveals
- Lore entries
- Relationship gains with witnesses, guilds, towns, or rescued NPCs

Raid loot distribution modes:

| Mode | Rule |
| --- | --- |
| Contribution Roll | Higher contribution increases loot roll weight. |
| Role Awards | Damage, tank, healer, support, objective, and leadership awards are separate. |
| Leader Distribution | Raid leader assigns loot, with reputation consequences for unfairness. |
| Guild Rules | Guild-controlled raids follow guild loot contracts. |
| Need Before Greed | Players who can use an item receive priority. |
| Auction Split | Rare drops are auctioned and gold is split. |
| Trophy Claim | Last hit or key objective player may claim a visible trophy, not necessarily the best item. |
| Server Event Reward | Large world events may give all qualified participants a fixed reward plus top-contributor bonuses. |

Last hit should matter, but it should not be the only thing that matters. A player who lands the final blow may receive fame, a trophy, title progress, or a bonus roll, but the healer who kept everyone alive, the tank who held the boss, and the player who completed the key objective should still be rewarded.

##### Raid Loot Dice System

Raid rewards should use dice rolls so rewards feel uncertain, exciting, and fairer than a fixed checklist.

The game should avoid simple logic like:

- "Kill the dragon and get dragon boots."

Instead, raid rewards should feel more like:

- "The raid succeeded, the dragon survived, and the party rolled high enough to receive a rare dragon-scale boot pattern."
- "The dragon was befriended, so no corpse materials dropped, but the alliance reward table unlocked a better chance at dragon-blessed gear."
- "The dragon was tamed, so the dungeon economy opened, but immediate loot was lower because the dragon and dungeon remain alive."

Loot dice should consider both outcome and contribution.

Core raid roll layers:

| Roll Layer | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| Outcome Roll | Determines the reward table based on whether the boss was killed, defeated, sealed, befriended, tamed, escaped, spared, or enraged. |
| Raid Table Roll | Determines which reward category appears for the raid group. |
| Individual Contribution Roll | Determines each participant's personal bonus reward chance. |
| Role Bonus Roll | Gives extra chances for damage, healing, tanking, objectives, support, leadership, discovery, and rescue. |
| Contested Loot Roll | Resolves who receives a rare item if multiple qualified players want it. |
| Luck Roll | Applies personal Luck, blessings, curses, relic effects, guild contracts, and strange world events. |

Suggested dice language:

- Common raid checks can use d20-style rolls.
- Large reward tables can use percentile rolls.
- Contribution can add modifiers rather than guaranteeing rewards.
- Luck can improve odds, rerolls, critical success chance, or bad-luck protection.

Example reward roll:

| Roll Result | Reward Tier |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Bad luck, reduced reward, damaged materials, or only base participation reward. |
| 2 to 9 | Common reward. |
| 10 to 15 | Good reward. |
| 16 to 19 | Rare reward. |
| 20 | Critical reward, bonus table, title progress, unique material, or special drop chance. |

Percentile tables can support bigger raids.

Example percentile table:

| Roll Result | Reward Result |
| --- | --- |
| 1 to 45 | Common materials, gold, raid tokens, or basic boss parts. |
| 46 to 70 | Good materials, recipes, guild credit, or useful gear. |
| 71 to 88 | Rare materials, high-quality gear, boss-themed items, or strong recipes. |
| 89 to 97 | Very rare reward, unique pattern, relic fragment, dragon oath item, or special title. |
| 98 to 100 | Legendary table roll, server announcement item, or world-changing reward hook. |

Contribution modifiers:

| Contribution | Example Modifier |
| --- | --- |
| Meaningful participation | Qualifies for base roll. |
| High contribution | +1 to +3 on d20-style rolls or higher percentile weight. |
| Top role performer | Bonus role roll. |
| Last hit | Bonus trophy/fame roll or +1 to final loot roll. |
| Most damage | Damage bonus roll. |
| Most healing | Healing/support bonus roll. |
| Most protection | Defender bonus roll. |
| Key objective completed | Objective reward roll. |
| Low contribution | Reduced roll or base reward only. |
| Sabotage or exploit behavior | No roll, penalty, or criminal/guild consequence. |

Rarity should never be fully guaranteed unless the raid itself is designed as a specific story reward. Even then, the exact quality, variant, enchantment, or ownership may still be rolled.

Dragon raid outcome tables:

| Dragon Outcome | Reward Table Logic |
| --- | --- |
| Dragon killed | Corpse materials, dragon parts, dungeon collapse loot, high immediate material chance, dungeon ends. |
| Dragon defeated but not killed | Lower corpse material chance, higher mercy/reputation chance, possible scale, fang, claw, or oath-gift roll. |
| Dragon befriended | No corpse harvest, but alliance table opens: dragon-blessed gear, protected dungeon farming rights, knowledge, map, spell, or future aid. |
| Dragon tamed | Immediate loot may be lower, but dungeon control, dungeon economy access, dragon bond rewards, and long-term resource rights unlock. |
| Dragon escaped | Reduced loot, possible chase quest, scar trophy, partial materials, danger continues. |
| Dragon enraged | Poor loot, new raid danger, regional consequences, revenge event. |
| Dragon sealed | Seal reward table: relic fragments, rune knowledge, divine favor, but limited harvest. |

This means players can still hope for rare rewards even when the best story outcome is not killing the boss.

Example:

- The raid defeats an Ancient Dragon but chooses not to kill it.
- The raid rolls on the "Defeated but spared" table.
- Most Damage earns a bonus roll for dragon scale fragments.
- Most Healing earns a dragon mercy blessing roll.
- Last Hit earns a trophy/fame roll.
- The party rolls high on the raid table and unlocks Dragonhide Boots Pattern instead of looting finished boots.
- A crafter later needs dragon-blessed leather, rare thread, heat-treated scale, and a master leatherworking station to make the boots.

This keeps rewards exciting while making crafting, economy, and story outcome matter.

Bad-luck protection can exist without removing randomness.

Bad-luck protection options:

- Raid tokens after each qualified clear.
- Pity progress toward a chosen reward table.
- Increased odds after repeated failed rolls.
- Crafting fragments that eventually build a desired item.
- Guild credit redeemable for raid materials.
- Dragon favor points from non-kill outcomes.

The best version is not "everyone gets the boots." The best version is "everyone gained something, and someone rolled into a story."

##### Craftable and Black Market Loot Dice

Loot dice can exist as rare physical or magical items in Huwan. They allow skilled players, powerful guilds, and insanely rich buyers to improve their raid reward chances without fully guaranteeing the exact item they want.

Loot dice should be valuable because they touch chance itself.

Possible lore sources:

- Shaohr's greed and wager magic.
- Matrena's failed probability experiments.
- Luck-infused monster cores.
- Dungeon fate fragments.
- Dragon bone dice.
- Relic dust sealed into gemstone dice.
- Black market counterfeits.
- Thieves Guild-loaded chance dice.
- Guild-certified raid dice.

Loot dice can be:

- Crafted by highly skilled players.
- Bought by extremely wealthy players.
- Sold through black markets.
- Awarded through raid tokens.
- Earned from guild contracts.
- Stolen from nobles, vaults, casinos, thieves, or relic hunters.
- Forged as illegal counterfeits.
- Blessed, cursed, loaded, cracked, or unstable.

Loot dice should never simply say "guarantee Ultra Rare Gear." They should improve odds, add rerolls, unlock bonus tables, or increase reward quality chances.

Core rule:

- Loot dice improve probability.
- Loot dice do not erase contribution requirements.
- Loot dice do not bypass raid outcome tables.
- Loot dice do not create loot that the raid table cannot logically provide.
- Loot dice do not override server rules, anti-exploit checks, or story consequences.

Example:

- A raid befriends a dragon instead of killing it.
- The dragon corpse table is unavailable.
- A loot die cannot force corpse-only dragon boots to appear.
- A high roll with loot dice may instead unlock Dragonhide Boots Pattern, dragon-blessed leather rights, a dragon oath material, or future dungeon economy access.

Loot dice item types:

| Loot Die | Access | Effect | Risk |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Lucky Die | High-end crafted or rare merchant item | Small bonus to one personal reward roll. | Low risk, expensive. |
| Guild-Certified Raid Die | Guild reward, raid token vendor, official auction | Legal bonus roll on an approved raid table. | Tracked by guild records. |
| Shaohr's Wager Die | Merchant elite, finance shrine, black market | Higher reward chance, but may demand gold, debt, tax, or future trade obligation. | Greed curse, debt, or contract consequence. |
| Matrena Miscast Die | Rare artificer/rune craft, dangerous ruins | Rerolls a result or shifts to an adjacent reward table. | Unstable result, strange side effect, broken probability. |
| Dragonbone Die | Dragon raid material craft | Improves dragon raid reward rolls. | Dragon anger, rarity, hard crafting requirements. |
| Loaded Black Die | Thieves Guild or black market | Stronger hidden modifier if undetected. | Illegal, fake chance, curse, loot dispute, guild punishment. |
| Counterfeit Loot Die | Fraud market | May do nothing or worsen a roll. | Scam, curse, legal trouble. |
| Fate Cluster | Ultra-rare crafted bundle | Counts as multiple dice with better stability. | Extremely expensive, high skill requirement. |

Crafting loot dice should require high-end skills.

Possible requirements:

- Master Rank Jewelcrafting.
- Master Rank Runecrafting.
- Master Rank Enchanting.
- Advanced or Master Alchemy.
- High Luck.
- High Intelligence for probability framework.
- High Wisdom for safe handling.
- Access to rare monster cores, boss materials, gemstones, relic dust, dragon bone, or dungeon fate fragments.
- A proper rune table, enchantment station, or Mages Guild-level workshop.
- Legal guild certification, unless made illegally.

Example crafting paths:

| Recipe Concept | Likely Inputs | Requirements |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Lucky Die | Gemstone, slime core, silver dust, luck herb, rune ink | Jewelcrafting Master, Runecrafting Expert. |
| Guild-Certified Raid Die | Raid token, gemstone die blank, guild seal, binding rune | Guild rank, Runecrafting Expert, official certification. |
| Shaohr's Wager Die | Gold, contract parchment, finance shrine mark, double-headed coin shaving | Merchant rank, Shaohr pact, high risk tolerance. |
| Matrena Miscast Die | Failed gadget fragment, time-rune dust, polished crystal, unstable mana thread | Artificer or Runecrafting Master, high Intelligence. |
| Dragonbone Die | Dragon bone chip, dragon scale dust, dungeon core splinter, heat-treated gem | Dragon raid access, Master craft skill, high danger. |
| Loaded Black Die | Black gem, stolen guild seal, shadow ink, counterfeit rune | Thieves Guild access, Forgery or Runecrafting skill. |

Using multiple loot dice should improve odds with diminishing returns.

Suggested stacking rules:

| Dice Used | Effect |
| --- | --- |
| 1 die | Full listed effect. |
| 2 dice | Second die gives reduced effect or a smaller bonus. |
| 3 dice | Third die gives a small bonus and may trigger instability checks. |
| 4 or more dice | Only allowed through special Fate Cluster, guild ritual, black market cheating, or extremely high-risk magic. |

Multiple dice can improve:

- Final d20-style reward result.
- Percentile reward table weight.
- Chance to access a bonus table.
- Chance to reroll a poor result.
- Chance to improve material quality.
- Chance to receive a pattern instead of raw materials.
- Chance to turn a good reward into a rare reward.
- Chance to turn a rare reward into an ultra rare chance roll.

Multiple dice cannot:

- Guarantee Ultra Rare Gear.
- Skip contribution requirements.
- Bypass Need Before Greed restrictions.
- Create corpse loot from a living dragon.
- Create a relic that the raid does not support.
- Duplicate unique items.
- Force a reward from a hidden table the player has not unlocked.
- Override leader distribution rules without causing conflict.

Ultra Rare gear should require several gates:

1. Raid outcome supports the gear, pattern, or material.
2. Player or party qualifies for reward rolls.
3. Contribution threshold is met.
4. Loot dice or Luck modifiers improve the chance.
5. Roll succeeds.
6. If the reward is a pattern, a skilled crafter still has to make the item.
7. If the item is unique, ownership or contested loot rules resolve who receives it.

Black market loot dice should be tempting and dangerous.

Black market risks:

- Counterfeit dice.
- Cursed dice.
- Loaded dice detected by guilds.
- Debt to thieves or Shaohr-linked lenders.
- Loot dispute if cheating is revealed.
- Wanted status in legal raids.
- Guild ban from official raid rewards.
- Relationship damage with party members.
- Hidden obligation to a criminal seller.

Publicly legal loot dice should usually be weaker, safer, and tracked. Illegal dice should be stronger, riskier, and capable of causing social, legal, or magical consequences.

Party rules:

- Personal loot dice affect personal reward rolls.
- Group loot dice affect shared raid table rolls only if the raid leader or party contract allows it.
- A party can pool dice for a shared roll.
- Dice ownership should be recorded before the raid reward ceremony.
- If one player supplies the dice and the party benefits, reward split rules should account for that contribution.
- A leader who hides group dice, steals dice, or lies about dice use can suffer reputation consequences.

Loot dice should create stories:

- A poor player saves for weeks to buy one Guild-Certified Raid Die.
- A rich noble burns five fortunes on black dice and still fails.
- A Master Runecrafter becomes famous for crafting stable Fate Clusters.
- A Thieves Guild die wins an Ultra Rare roll, then starts a loot scandal.
- A dragon notices Dragonbone Dice and demands to know where the bone came from.
- Laucian sees someone use a Matrena Miscast Die and says, "Oh, absolutely not. That thing has patch notes written all over it."

Anti-exploit rules:

- Damage to immune, friendly, irrelevant, or intentionally farmed targets should not count.
- Overhealing should not count as full healing contribution.
- Fake tanking, intentional damage farming, grief pulls, and staged deaths should not count.
- Players who join at the last second need minimum contribution to qualify.
- Players who disconnect may receive partial credit only if they contributed meaningfully before leaving.
- Sabotage can remove rewards and create criminal, guild, or reputation consequences.
- Raid bosses should not be farmed infinitely unless designed as repeatable server content.

Raid completion should update the world.

Raid world updates can include:

- Boss defeated, escaped, befriended, tamed, sealed, or enraged.
- Dungeon state changed.
- Local monster population changed.
- Market flooded with boss materials.
- Guild boards updated.
- Survivors remember who helped.
- Cities celebrate, mourn, rebuild, or panic.
- Rival factions react.
- World Popularity changes for visible heroes.
- World Favor changes if the raid protects Huwan's balance.

Dragon-born dungeon raids should use special outcome rules. Killing the dragon may collapse the dungeon, befriending the dragon may make it protected and infinitely farmable, and taming the dragon may grant dungeon control and economy rights. Raid rewards should respect which ending occurs.

##### Group Urgency Display

Party and group UI should make urgent shared quests hard to miss without flooding the HUD.

Group quest display should show:

- Shared quest list
- Most urgent group quest
- Timer
- Distance or direction
- Who owns the quest
- Who is currently closest
- Needed items already held by party members
- Suggested role actions
- Reward split rule

Example:

- Mara needs 20 meal portions before evening.
- Player A has 6 fish.
- Player B has 5 vegetables.
- Player C is closest to Mara.
- The party quest UI can show that the group already has 11/20 meal portions and suggest delivery or gathering.

This allows parties to coordinate naturally without needing every player to manually compare inventories.

##### Other Players and NPC Resolution

Quests should not wait forever for one player.

A quest can be resolved by:

- The player who accepted it.
- A party member.
- Another player.
- A hired NPC.
- A guild worker.
- A merchant purchase.
- A noble order.
- A criminal group.
- A faction intervention.
- The NPC solving it personally.
- The problem becoming impossible to solve.

If another player or NPC resolves the issue first, the original quest can:

- Complete with reduced credit.
- Transform into a follow-up.
- Expire.
- Become a rivalry or missed opportunity.
- Become a thank-you if the player had promised help but did not need to act.
- Become resentment if the player promised help and failed to arrive.

The world should not punish every delay equally. The consequence depends on promise, urgency, relationship, and harm.

##### Urgency Example: Mara's Medicine Crisis

Mara Kettleblack needs herbs for sick children.

Possible outcomes:

- Fast help: Mara gains trust and gratitude, children recover, medicine prices remain stable.
- Delayed help: Sister Ameline steps in, reward lowers, Mara is still grateful but less impressed.
- No help: sickness spreads, medicine demand rises, Dawnmere offers "protective relocation," Demon and Beastfolk families become afraid.
- Exploitation: player overcharges Mara, sells spoiled herbs, or steals medicine; poor district distrust rises and future kindness quests become harder.
- Party completion: one party member gathers herbs, another escorts them, another delivers them; reward split follows party leader rules, but Mara remembers each visible helper.

##### Schedule Memory and Missed Opportunities

NPCs should remember time-sensitive behavior.

Examples:

- A player who arrives after Mara's meal service may have to help prepare tomorrow's food instead.
- A player who follows Sable Quill at Deep Night may discover a hidden clue but gain suspicion.
- A player who helps Bessa at Dawn may get better bread prices later.
- A player who bothers Halka Stonejaw during a dangerous patrol may be treated coldly.
- A player who repeatedly misses a promised delivery may lose trust.

Schedules should make Eldoria feel lived in. The city is not waiting motionless for the player; it is moving, eating, working, sleeping, lying, trading, praying, stealing, and remembering.

### Combat, Death, Revival, And Consequence System Completion V1.0

Combat in Huwan should feel dangerous, readable, and tied to the living world. A fight is not only a health bar reaching zero. It can create wounds, blood, reputation, property damage, legal evidence, monster materials, market shortages, grief, fame, faction pressure, and story consequences.

Core combat rule:

- **Every serious fight should leave a record somewhere.**

That record may be a corpse, scar, witness, blood trail, broken wall, guild report, monster population change, loot table, crime case, bard song, faction grudge, dragon memory, or NPC trauma.

#### Combat Identity

Huwan supports many combat styles.

| Combat Style | Typical Users | Strength |
|---|---|---|
| Weapon Combat | Knights, rogues, adventurers, barbarians, archers. | Reliable physical damage and skill expression. |
| Unarmed Combat | Brawlers, monks, gauntlet users, beastfolk, barbarians. | Grapples, strikes, mobility, close pressure. |
| Magic Combat | Mages, Wizards, Sorcerers, Warlocks, Priests, Druids. | Range, utility, control, burst, support, special effects. |
| Holy Combat | Priests and Paladins. | Undead, demons, curses, smites, exorcism, faith pressure. |
| Rune Combat | Mages, enchanters, trapmakers, artificers. | Prepared traps, wards, locks, anti-magic zones. |
| Tamer Combat | Tamers, Druids, beastfolk, slime trainers. | Companions, positioning, commands, bond growth. |
| Siege Combat | Knights, factions, cities, contractors, artificers. | Walls, gates, engines, raids, settlement warfare. |
| Dungeon Combat | Adventurers, dungeon explorers, parties, raids. | Boss mechanics, traps, floor rules, resource pressure. |
| Social Combat | Nobles, merchants, bards, priests, criminals. | Reputation, law, contracts, intimidation, bribery. |

Social combat is not a replacement for fighting, but it should matter. A player may avoid bloodshed through threats, diplomacy, contracts, bribery, reputation, divine authority, guild rank, or relationship trust.

#### Rank And Threat Scale

Combat difficulty should use the same rank language as monsters, players, spells, dungeons, and legendary encounters.

| Rank | Meaning |
|---|---|
| F | Beginner-safe threats, basic slimes, weak animals, training fights. |
| E | New adventurer threats, small monsters, low bandits, rough wildlife. |
| D | Trained-party threats, organized bandits, dangerous beasts, minor undead. |
| C | Serious local threats, elite guards, strong monsters, dungeon bosses. |
| B | Veteran threats, regional beasts, strong undead, faction champions. |
| A | Elite threats, dangerous dungeon floors, major monsters, high-rank adventurers. |
| S | Legendary threats, city-scale danger, ancient monsters, top-tier NPCs. |
| SS | Raid-only or near-impossible threats, demi-god monsters, world bosses, catastrophic entities. |
| SSS | Protected mythic authority. Laucian is the only known SSS-ranked individual. |

SS rank should usually be impossible to defeat solo. It belongs to raids, server events, divine-scale threats, demi-gods, world bosses, and entities that change the shape of the world by existing.

#### Damage And Injury States

The game can use hit points, stamina, status effects, and visible injuries without needing every wound to become complicated.

Suggested injury ladder:

| State | Meaning | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| Healthy | No meaningful harm. | Normal action. |
| Scratched | Minor cuts, bruises, small burns. | Light blood, minor pain, quick healing. |
| Wounded | Clear damage, bleeding, limping, reduced performance. | Healing, rest, bandage, potion, or spell recommended. |
| Injured | Serious harm to limb, organ, senses, or mobility. | Treatment required; combat penalties. |
| Critical | Near death, severe bleeding, shock, magical instability. | Immediate help needed. |
| Downed | Cannot fight, may crawl, speak weakly, or wait for rescue. | Stabilize or revive. |
| Dying | Death timer, heavy risk. | Life magic, medical aid, or miracle needed. |
| Dead | Body has died. | Revival rules apply. |
| Obliterated | Body/soul/mana well destroyed beyond normal recovery. | No revival; reincarnation may be offered. |

Most common fights should end before permanent damage unless the enemy, weapon, spell, raid, dungeon, or story event is severe enough to justify it.

#### Blood And Gore Direction

Huwan is Mature, so combat may include blood and gore, but the strongest gore should connect to survival, monster harvesting, war aftermath, or truly devastating attacks.

Blood can appear through:

- Weapon hits
- Monster attacks
- Critical wounds
- Battlefields
- Dungeon boss fights
- Hunting and skinning
- Execution aftermath, where legally and tonally appropriate

Gore can appear through:

- Monster processing
- Animal processing
- Large creature harvesting
- Severe boss attacks
- Undead and horror scenes
- Alchemy preparation
- Dragon, chimera, or giant monster kills

The purpose is danger and survival texture, not shock for its own sake.

#### Combat Outcome Types

Not every fight needs to end in death.

| Outcome | Meaning | Use |
|---|---|---|
| Defeat | Enemy loses but survives. | Duels, dragon mercy, criminal capture, monster taming. |
| Kill | Enemy dies. | Hunting, lethal combat, war, monsters, murder, execution. |
| Capture | Target is restrained. | Bounties, law, taming, interrogation, rescue. |
| Route | Enemy flees. | Bandits, beasts, weak monsters, losing faction squads. |
| Negotiate | Fight stops through speech, deal, bribe, or threat. | Intelligent monsters, criminals, nobles, dragons. |
| Tame | Creature accepts command or care. | Slimes, animals, monsters, dragons at extreme level. |
| Befriend | Enemy becomes ally or neutral. | Dragons, intelligent monsters, faction rivals, NPC enemies. |
| Seal | Target is bound or contained. | Undead, demons, forbidden entities, dangerous relics. |
| Obliterate | Target is destroyed beyond normal revival. | Mana well overload, divine erasure, forbidden failure. |

Dragon-born dungeons rely heavily on this structure. Defeating, killing, befriending, taming, sealing, or enraging a dragon must all create different futures.

#### Revival And Resurrection Rules

Revival should be powerful, expensive, and limited enough that death still matters.

Revival sources:

- Church services
- Priests
- Paladins in rare battlefield cases
- Life Magic
- Green Magic support
- Druid weakened revival
- Relics
- Divine intervention
- Dragon pacts
- Laucian or Dawnspire intervention in protected cases

Revival checks should consider:

- Body condition
- Time since death
- Soul state
- Cause of death
- Mana well condition
- Curse or seal effects
- Divine approval
- Payment or contract
- Relationship importance
- Crime and legal records
- Dungeon state
- Reincarnation eligibility

Deaths that may block normal revival:

- Mana well obliteration
- Soul destruction
- Certain forbidden spells
- Divine judgment
- Dragon judgment
- Relic rejection
- Body erased beyond anchor
- Reincarnation already chosen
- Legal or religious refusal

Church resurrection can be expensive, political, and sometimes shady. The Church may charge heavily, demand favors, require confession, deny criminals, hide evidence, or use resurrection records as leverage. Good priests may see resurrection as mercy. Sinister priests may see it as control.

#### Reincarnated Path Integration

The Reincarnated path triggers only when a player dies and cannot be revived.

This means the system must distinguish between:

- Downed
- Dying
- Dead but revivable
- Dead with difficult revival
- Dead with blocked revival
- Obliterated with no revival

When revival is impossible and the player qualifies, the game can offer reincarnation as a secret path. The new character gains the deeper character creation options already defined: up to 15 skills, 15 abilities, unique or master options, and possible soul echoes.

Reincarnation should feel like Udum opening a new story, not like a normal respawn button.

#### PvE, PvP, Crime, And Consent

Huwan should allow danger without letting chaos erase fairness.

Combat legality depends on context:

| Context | Rule |
|---|---|
| PvE combat | Monsters, dungeons, hunting, raids, and defense follow local rules. |
| Duels | Legal if both sides consent and location allows it. |
| Arena fights | Governed by arena contracts, healing rules, and wagers. |
| Bounties | Legal if properly issued and jurisdiction recognizes the bounty. |
| Assassinations | Can be socially useful, criminal, protected, or ethically justified depending on contract, target, evidence, and law. |
| Banditry | Criminal unless recognized by corrupt authority or war context. |
| War | Governed by faction, kingdom, guild, or treaty rules. |
| Murder | Illegal killing without valid lawful, wartime, bounty, duel, defense, or contract justification. |
| Self-defense | Legal when evidence supports immediate threat. |

The system should record who attacked first, who witnessed it, what contracts existed, what jurisdiction applied, and whether the act was harm, defense, execution, duel, assassination, accident, or war.

#### Party Combat And Shared Consequences

When players fight as a party, the party should share information and consequences.

Party combat should track:

- Damage
- Healing
- Protection
- Control
- Interrupts
- Rescues
- Revives
- Objectives
- Loot contribution
- Friendly fire
- Criminal actions
- Witnessed behavior
- Quest progress
- Reputation change

If one party member commits a crime during a shared mission, the rest of the party may be treated differently depending on proof, distance, role, and response.

Examples:

- A party member steals from an NPC while others are fighting monsters.
- A healer refuses to help a dying ally and gains distrust.
- A tank protects poor civilians during a raid and gains local fame.
- A rogue performs an ethical assassination that prevents a massacre, but the law still investigates.

Parties create stories because responsibility becomes shared but not identical.

#### Raid Combat Finalized

Raid battles should be major coordinated events, not larger normal fights.

Raid encounters can include:

- World bosses
- SS-rank monsters
- Dragon-born dungeon bosses
- Ancient Dragons
- Settlement sieges
- Faction wars
- Clockwork outbreaks
- Demon incursions
- Undead infestations
- Relic disasters
- Divine or forbidden anomalies

Raid contribution should track the full battlefield:

- Last hit
- Most damage
- Most healing
- Most protection
- Most threat held
- Crowd control
- Objective completion
- Rescue actions
- Logistics
- Scouting
- Leadership
- Resource supply
- Death prevention
- Mercy or capture outcomes

Rewards should use loot dice, contribution, role awards, outcome tables, and story eligibility. A high roll can create an unforgettable reward, but it should not ignore the actual raid outcome.

#### Combat Data Handoff

Recommended combat record fields:

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| CombatId | Unique fight record. |
| Location | Where the fight happened. |
| Participants | Players, NPCs, monsters, summons, companions, factions. |
| Context | PvE, duel, raid, bounty, crime, war, dungeon, defense, accident. |
| Initiator | Who started hostilities. |
| Witnesses | NPCs, players, magic records, map records, divine or dragon witnesses. |
| DamageLog | Damage dealt and received. |
| SupportLog | Healing, protection, buffs, revives, rescues, logistics. |
| ObjectiveLog | Mechanics, captures, seals, negotiations, puzzle steps, escapes. |
| Outcome | Defeat, kill, capture, route, tame, befriend, seal, obliterate. |
| DeathRecords | Who died, how, revival state, body condition, soul state. |
| LootRolls | Dice, contribution, reward table, contested results. |
| CrimeLinks | Assault, murder, self-defense, bounty, property damage, witnesses. |
| ReputationImpact | Local, guild, faction, kingdom, monster, divine, world popularity. |
| WorldEffects | Monster population, market, dungeon, settlement, faction, story clock. |

#### Combat Implementation Checklist

Combat is implementation-ready when the game supports:

- Rank-based threat scale from F to SSS.
- Injury, downed, dying, dead, and obliterated states.
- Revival eligibility and blocked-revival logic.
- Reincarnated trigger after no-revival death.
- PvE, PvP, duel, bounty, crime, and war context records.
- Party combat contribution and shared quest visibility.
- Raid contribution scoring.
- Loot dice and outcome-based reward tables.
- Dragon kill, befriend, tame, seal, enrage, and escape outcomes.
- Crime and witness integration.
- Reputation and relationship consequences.
- Monster harvesting after eligible kills.
- Gore and blood presentation boundaries.
- AI moderation flags for exploits, griefing, staged contribution, and illegal behavior.

#### Final Combat Rules

- Combat should be exciting because it matters.
- Death should be scary because it changes the story.
- Revival should be possible, costly, political, and limited.
- Reincarnation should feel like a secret second story, not a reset.
- SS rank is raid-only or nearly impossible.
- Last hit matters, but never more than the whole fight.
- Killing is only one outcome; capture, mercy, friendship, taming, and sealing can be stronger stories.
- A battle ends when the world remembers what happened.

### Mature Character Creation Customization

Huwan may include optional nudity in character creation as part of its Mature rating.

Players can customize adult body proportions such as:

- Breast size
- Butt size
- Penis size

These options are cosmetic only and have no practical gameplay usage, stat advantage, combat effect, romance modifier, class impact, or job impact. They exist for player expression, body variety, and light fan-service without turning the system into explicit sexual gameplay.

Character creation should remain non-explicit in interaction. The customization screen may show nudity if allowed by settings, but it should not depict sexual acts or sexual mechanics.

### Secret Class: Reincarnated

**Reincarnated** is a secret class or secret race-state that becomes available only after true death.

Trigger:

- A player dies in-game.
- The player cannot be revived.
- The player is offered the choice to reincarnate.

If the player chooses reincarnation, their next playthrough becomes easier and more detailed. Reincarnation turns permanent failure into a rare progression path.

Reincarnated character creation benefits:

- More detailed character creation.
- Can choose up to 15 skills.
- Can choose up to 15 abilities.
- Gains access to unique skills.
- Gains access to master-level skills and abilities.
- May gain special memory, destiny, soul, or world-history options.

The Reincarnated path should feel powerful but rare. It can connect to world lore, divine balance, soul cycles, sealed gods, Dawnspire, relics, or the hidden truth of the 10th Era.

Possible restrictions or costs:

- The previous character cannot return normally.
- Some memories may be fragmented.
- Certain factions may sense the reincarnated soul.
- World Popularity may partially echo into the new life.
- Divine, demonic, or magical forces may become interested in the player.

### Alignment and Reputation

Players can choose good or evil as their starting alignment direction, but reputation should reflect actual behavior over time.

Alignment should influence:

- NPC trust
- Faction access
- Divine attention
- Quest options
- Crime response
- Monster relationships
- Artifact corruption or blessing
- Class and job unlocks

Reputation should be local and global. A player might be loved in one town, feared by another kingdom, hunted by a guild, and worshiped by a cult.

## Starter Town

Every player begins in a pregenerated starter town named **Eldoria**, located on the continent of **Aurelith**.

The starter town is a mid-sized settlement with roughly **1,500 NPCs**. These NPCs should have lives, jobs, needs, schedules, ambitions, relationships, secrets, alignments, and social roles.

The town should act as the player's first living simulation:

- A place to learn basic survival.
- A place to meet factions.
- A place to find early jobs.
- A place to build reputation.
- A place to see class progression begin.
- A place where good or evil choices have visible consequences.

### Starter Town Design Needs

- Town name: Eldoria
- Continent: Aurelith
- Founding history
- Region within Aurelith
- Dominant races
- Government type
- Major guilds
- Religious sites
- Markets and crafting stations
- Nearby wilderness threats
- Starter quests
- Hidden evil or corruption
- Peaceful coexistence examples
- At least 20 important named NPCs

### Eldoria City Generation Engine

Eldoria should eventually be generated and maintained by a dedicated city generation engine.

This engine should use a hybrid approach:

- Hand-authored rules for lore, districts, landmarks, leaders, guilds, and important NPCs.
- Procedural generation for buildings, interiors, containers, streets, traffic, decorations, minor NPC homes, resource placement, and repeatable city details.
- Seeded generation so the city can be reproduced, saved, inspected, and updated consistently.

The goal is not to create a random city. The goal is to generate a living Eldoria that obeys Huwan's logic.

#### Generation Inputs

The engine should read:

- City name
- Continent and region
- District list
- Population count
- Race distribution
- Wealth distribution
- Guild presence
- Noble house influence
- Crime level
- Guard level
- Resource needs
- Market supply
- Current world events
- Faction influence
- Nearby threats
- Player actions
- Server history

#### District Generation

The engine should generate or maintain:

- Noble district
- Market district
- Poor district
- Guild row
- Church quarter
- Residential blocks
- Crafting streets
- Tavern lanes
- Brothel/adult social district
- Guard barracks
- Armory zone
- Warehouses
- Farm edges
- City gates
- Underground routes
- Sewer access
- Hidden Thieves Guild routes
- F-rank outskirts
- Road toward The Verdant Maw

Each district should have its own wealth level, crime risk, building quality, traffic pattern, guard presence, lighting style, resource needs, and NPC routine profile.

#### Building Structure Generation

Buildings should be generated from templates and local rules.

Building types:

- Homes
- Shops
- Taverns
- Inns

- Underground hideouts

Building generation should define:

- Exterior style
- Interior rooms
- Doors
- Windows
- Stairs
- Cellars
- Attics
- Furniture
- Lighting
- Storage
- Ownership

#### Interior Item Generation

Buildings should contain logical items based on owner, wealth, job, district, and profession.

Generated items can include:

- Tools
- Art
- Gold sacks
- Coin purses
- Inventories
- Chests
- Storage crates
- Wardrobes
- Cooking utensils
- Weapons
- Armor

- Contracts
- Food
- Drinks
- Pouches
- Materials
- Farm goods
- Religious objects
- Merchant ledgers
- Rune-marked objects
- Hidden contraband

Items should not be placed randomly without context. A blacksmith has tools, ore, bars, tongs, anvils, and repair orders. A noble has art, wardrobes, records, jewelry, guarded chests, and servant supplies. A poor home may have few valuables but strong personal story items.

#### Container and Inventory Rules

Containers should have ownership and risk.

Container types:

- Chests
- Wardrobes
- Shelves
- Lockboxes
- Barrels
- Crates
- Sacks
- Gold sacks
- Hidden compartments
- Magical storage boxes
- Guild vaults
- Bank vaults

Each container should define:

- Owner
- Lock level
- Trap/rune status
- Theft value
- Legal access
- Witness chance
- Guard response
- Contents
- Restock rules
- Whether items are personal, commercial, stolen, sacred, or quest-critical

#### Streets, Traffic, and Movement

The engine should generate city movement systems.

Street data:

- Main roads
- Alleys
- Noble lanes
- Market streets
- Service roads
- Sewer entrances
- Patrol paths
- Caravan routes
- Festival routes
- Escape routes
- Thieves Guild shortcuts

Traffic should include:

- Guards
- Caravans
- Horses

Traffic density changes by time of day, weather, district, danger, market events, festivals, crimes, and quests.

#### Lighting, Shading, and Atmosphere

Lighting should help each district feel distinct.

Generated lighting can include:

- Torches
- Lanterns
- Rune lights
- Candles
- Hearthlight
- Streetlamps
- Moonlight
- Noble magical lighting
- Poor district oil lamps
- Church holy light
- Thieves Guild low-light routes

Shading and atmosphere should support stealth, safety, class tension, and mood.

Examples:

- Noble district has clean bright lighting.
- Market has warm busy lighting.
- Poor district has patchy oil light and deep shadows.
- Thieves routes avoid strong light.
- Church quarter uses holy glow and candlelight.
- Guard stations use visible light to deter crime.

#### Plants, Animals, and Farmables

The engine should place natural life inside and around the city.

Plants:

- Street trees
- Gardens
- Noble hedges

- Honey

- Livestock products
- Managed trees

Farmables should tie into NPC needs, market supply, taverns, bakers, poor district meal halls, and quest generation.

#### Theft and Crime Generation

The city engine should understand theft opportunities and consequences.

Theft generation should consider:

- Item value
- Owner
- District
- Witnesses
- Lighting
- Container locks
- Guard patrols
- Rune traps
- Social reputation
- Thieves Guild influence
- Poverty level
- Noble corruption

Theft does not only affect inventory. It affects trust, suspicion, crime reports, market shortages, guard behavior, noble retaliation, and underworld opportunities.

#### Underground Generation

Eldoria should have an underground layer.

Underground spaces:

- Sewers
- Old ruins
- Smuggling tunnels
- Hidden Thieves Guild halls

- Black market rooms

- Rune-sealed vaults

Underground routes should support stealth, crime, hidden quests, escape, contraband movement, monster infestations, and secret faction activity.

#### Walls, Gates, Guard Stations, and Armories

#### Battalions and Defense Forces

The city engine should track organized defense groups.

Defense groups:

- Knights Guild detachments

#### Dragon Defense

Because dragon-born dungeons and Ancient Dragons exist, major cities should plan for dragon-scale threats.

Possible dragon defense systems:

- Ballista towers
- Rune-shield walls
- Firebreak streets
- Underground shelters
- Evacuation routes
- Dragon alarm bells
- Anti-flight nets
- Water reserves
- Mages Guild emergency seals
- Knights Guild dragon response teams
- Adventurers Guild raid musters
- Church protection rites
- Druid weather calls

Eldoria may not begin fully prepared for a dragon attack, but wealthy districts, guild leaders, and paranoid nobles may push for dragon defense funding after The Verdant Maw becomes known as a true dragon-born dungeon.

This output should eventually feed Unreal Engine maps, NPC simulation, inventory systems, theft systems, quest generation, lighting, AI navigation, and server persistence.

### Eldoria Civic Identity

Eldoria was founded as an open-gate city where every race could be welcomed without needing to hide their bloodline, homeland, faith, or ancestry. Its public ideal is coexistence. Its daily reality is more complicated.

The city is ruled by a **High Noble** who acts as the highest local authority. Several noble houses sit beneath the High Noble and compete for influence, trade rights, district control, marriage alliances, guard appointments, and guild favor.

Eldoria contains wealthy estates, merchant streets, mixed-race neighborhoods, working-class homes, temples, guild halls, markets, and a poor district where the city's promise of equality is most visibly tested.

### Eldoria District Map

Eldoria's district map should act as the first playable city skeleton. Each district has a social role, economy role, quest identity, guard profile, and relationship pressure.

| District | Role | Wealth | Crime | Guard Level | Main Influence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Serville Rise | Noble/government district | Very high | Low visible crime, high political crime | Very high | House Serville, noble houses, Knights Guild |
| Guild Row | Guild halls and quest traffic | Mixed | Medium | High | Adventurers Guild, Merchants Guild, Archers Guild, Knights Guild |
| Goldscale Market | Trade, banks, auctions, stalls | High | Medium | High | Merchants Guild, Shaohr worshippers, caravan houses |
| Low Lantern Ward | Poor district and survival pressure | Very low | High | Low to medium | Poor residents, Dawnmere charity, criminals, reformers |
| Dawnmere Commons | Charity, orphanages, meal halls, public virtue | Medium | Low visible crime, high hidden manipulation | Medium | House Dawnmere |
| Candlecourt | Church quarter, healing, exorcism, funerary rites | Medium to high | Low visible crime | Medium to high | The Church, Arch Diocese Ranquith |
| Ashdoor Lanes | Taverns, inns, adult social venues, rumor economy | Mixed | Medium to high | Medium | Tavern owners, brothels, performers, informants |
| The Underveins | Sewers, tunnels, black market, hidden routes | Hidden | Very high | Very low official presence | Thieves Guild, smugglers, hidden factions |
| Mossgate Outskirts | F-rank fields, streams, small forests, Verdant Maw route | Low to medium | Low to medium | Low patrols | Archers Guild, gatherers, Adventurers Guild |
| Northgate Barracks | Walls, guards, armory, knight staging, defense | Medium | Low | Very high | Knights Guild, town guard, Marquis Serville |

Serville Rise is the cleanest and most controlled district in Eldoria. It contains noble estates, administrative halls, legal chambers, guarded gardens, diplomatic rooms, and high-status residences.

Above-ground ore veins are useful for F-rank and early crafting because they let new players learn mining without entering deep caves or dangerous dungeons. These veins should be low yield, slow to respawn, and watched by farmers, contractors, guards, or merchants when near owned land.
