| DebugNotes | Should feel rare, strange, political, and emotional. |

#### Complete Implementation Checklist

Legendary encounters are considered implementation-ready when the game supports:

- Eligible moment detection.
- Context tag generation.
- Weighted encounter pools.
- Probability roll and caps.
- Cooldown tracking.
- Player, party, region, faction, legend, and server memory.
- Rumor, lead, proof, contact, trust, crisis, and fate journal states.
- Map fog interaction for vague and confirmed markers.
- Guild board integration.
- NPC dialogue rumor integration.
- Reward table integration.
- Failure surge integration.
- Critical success deck integration.
- Loot dice integration.
- Market and economy effects.
- Party and raid contribution tracking.
- Anti-farm detection.
- Server rumor and announcement scopes.
- Data-driven tuning tables.
- Debug tools for designers.

#### Final Legendary Encounter Rules

- Legendary encounters are rare, but the possibility should always breathe in the background.
- Players should not be able to farm destiny.
- Luck helps, but choices matter more.
- Reputation opens doors, but proof keeps them open.
- Critical success creates opportunity.
- Failure creates story.
- Betrayal creates memory.
- Power must be earned through path, trust, skill, and consequence.
- The best legendary encounter is one the player tells another player about.

## Culture and Conflict

Cultures should draw from recognizable fantasy patterns and open inspirations, then become original to Huwan through local history, divine influence, geography, and gameplay consequences.

### Common Conflicts

- Wealthy versus poor
- Humans versus demons
- Elves versus dwarves
- Extremists versus coexistence movements
- Divine loyalists versus anti-god movements
- Artifact hunters versus artifact sealers
- Kingdom law versus guild power
- Racial purity movements versus mixed-race communities

## NPC Simulation

NPCs in Huwan should feel like living people inside a simulated world, not static quest boards. Every meaningful NPC can have daily routines, needs, wants, ambitions, inventory, wealth, social status, stats, skills, memories, and relationships.

NPCs are simulated by AI controllers that manage everyday life. These controllers decide what an NPC tries to do based on schedule, personality, needs, available resources, job duties, social ties, danger, wealth, reputation, and world events.

### NPC Core Profile

Each NPC should eventually support:

- Name
- Race and sub-race
- Age and life stage
- Family and household
- Class and jobs
- Daily routine
- Wants
- Needs
- Ambitions
- Inventory
- Wealth
- Social status
- Reputation
- Alignment
- Stats
- Skills
- Memories
- Relationships
- Common knowledge
- World common sense
- Current problems
- Quest requests

### Daily Life Simulation

NPCs live through daily schedules. A normal day can include:

- Waking
- Eating
- Traveling
- Working
- Gathering materials
- Buying supplies
- Producing goods
- Training skills
- Socializing
- Worship
- Resting
- Shopping
- Visiting guilds
- Responding to emergencies
- Sleeping

Schedules should change based on job, wealth, race, district, season, danger level, health, family needs, and local events.

### Wants, Needs, and Ambitions

Needs are immediate requirements. Wants are preferences. Ambitions are long-term drives.

Examples:

- A blacksmith needs ore, coal, tools, food, and customers.
- A merchant wants profit, safe trade routes, and rare goods.
- A poor parent needs medicine, rent money, food, and work.
- A young adventurer wants fame and guild rank.
- A noble heir wants influence, marriage alliances, and status.
- A secret cultist wants privacy, forbidden texts, and recruits.

Ambitions can shape life paths. NPCs can seek jobs, lose jobs, gain wealth, lose wealth, gain status, lose status, train skills, develop stats, create rivalries, form friendships, and generate quests.

### Aging and Life Progression

NPCs can age, grow, change roles, and eventually die. Their lives should not remain frozen.

Possible life progression:

- Birth
- Childhood
- Apprenticeship or early training
- First job
- Career growth
- Marriage or partnership
- Family building
- Wealth gain or loss
- Skill growth
- Reputation change
- Injury or illness
- Retirement
- Death
- Legacy through family, memory, inheritance, or undeath

NPCs should be able to change over time. A poor child might become a knight. A merchant might go bankrupt. A guard might retire into farming. A grieving priest might become a necromancer. A fired worker might join the Thieves Guild.

### Jobs, Firing, and Status

NPC employment should be dynamic.

NPCs can:

- Get jobs
- Lose jobs
- Be promoted
- Be demoted
- Be fired
- Quit
- Change professions
- Join guilds
- Become apprentices
- Become criminals
- Gain noble patronage
- Lose public trust

Job outcomes affect wealth, inventory, routine, skill growth, social status, and quest generation.

### Inventory and Wealth

NPCs have inventories and personal wealth. Their inventory should matter to their job and daily needs.

An NPC can gain or lose items through:

- Work
- Trade
- Crafting
- Gathering
- Theft
- Gifts
- Taxes
- Quests
- Market purchases
- Dungeon rewards
- Inheritance
- Spoilage
- Disaster

Wealth can rise or fall based on work success, market prices, family expenses, guild fees, taxes, illness, crime, debt, trade, and player action.

### Dynamic Quest Generation

NPCs generate quests when they lack the means to complete a task or solve a need.

If an NPC does not have the resources, materials, skills, protection, information, money, or time needed for a daily task, the NPC can request help. This request can become a quest offered directly to the player, posted through a guild, or passed through rumor networks.

Examples:

- A baker needs flour because a caravan is late.
- A blacksmith needs iron ore to fulfill Adventurers Guild weapon demand.
- A healer needs moon herbs for a plague patient.
- A merchant needs escort protection for a dangerous trade route.
- A poor family needs help paying debt before eviction.
- A farmer needs wolves cleared from grazing land.
- A noble needs a stolen heirloom recovered without public scandal.
- A Church official needs undead cleansed before panic spreads.
- A Thieves Guild contact needs a map recovered from a rival.

Completing the quest updates the NPC's inventory, wealth, relationships, schedule, and ability to continue producing goods or services until they run low again.

### Economy Loop

NPC inventory and production feed Eldoria's economy.

The basic loop:

1. NPC needs task completion.
2. NPC checks inventory, wealth, skills, tools, safety, and access to materials.
3. If the NPC can complete the task, they consume resources and produce goods, services, or social outcomes.
4. If the NPC cannot complete the task, they generate a request.
5. Requests become direct favors, guild quests, market orders, or dark contracts.
6. Player or other NPCs fulfill the request.
7. NPC inventory updates.
8. Market supply changes.
9. Prices fluctuate.
10. The cycle continues when inventory runs low again.

This creates a steady flow of quests while keeping the economy connected to actual needs rather than random task generation.

### Market and Auction Fluctuation

World inventory should operate on a basic sliding scale. Supply and demand affect market prices, merchant behavior, auctions, and quest rewards.

Examples:

- Low iron supply raises weapon and armor prices.
- High monster population raises demand for healing items, weapons, escorts, and Adventurers Guild contracts.
- Food shortages raise bread, grain, livestock, and meal hall demand.
- Too many gathered herbs lower common potion prices but may increase demand for storage, export, or specialist crafting.
- Bandit activity raises escort prices and lowers import reliability.

Gold is the exception. Gold is always in high demand, no matter how much world inventory exists. Its exchange value remains fixed at a high rate to stabilize the economy and make wealth meaningful.

### NPC Memory

NPCs can remember events from birth to death. They also possess common knowledge and world common sense appropriate to their race, class, job, culture, education, age, and region.

Memory categories:

- Personal memories
- Family memories
- Trauma
- Favors owed
- Wrongs suffered
- Player interactions
- Witnessed crimes
- Guild events
- Market events
- Religious experiences
- Monster attacks
- Noble scandals
- Rumors
- Common local knowledge
- World common sense

NPC memory should influence trust, fear, prices, quest access, dialogue, alliances, grudges, romance, betrayal, and reputation.

### Simulation Design Needs

- Define memory limits for performance, such as major lifetime memories versus short-term routine memories.
- Define how AI controllers prioritize urgent needs over routine schedules.
- Define how NPC-generated quests expire, escalate, or resolve without player involvement.
- Define how market prices update without overwhelming the player.
- Define how NPCs pass knowledge through rumors, guilds, family, and public records.

### Simulation Depth

Not every NPC needs the same level of active simulation at all times. Eldoria uses two main simulation tiers.

#### Fully Simulated NPCs

Fully simulated NPCs receive deeper AI controller attention, stronger memory tracking, more detailed inventory and wealth changes, richer schedules, and more frequent quest generation.

Eldoria's fully simulated groups:

- Merchants
- High nobles
- The poorest residents

These groups are prioritized because they create the strongest pressure on the world:

- Merchants move goods, prices, imports, exports, loans, banks, auctions, and market shortages.
- High nobles move law, politics, land, military pressure, scandals, faction influence, and district control.
- The poorest residents reveal whether the city is actually functioning, and they generate urgent human-scale quests around food, shelter, medicine, debt, crime, exploitation, and survival.

#### Background Simulated NPCs

Background simulated NPCs still exist, age, work, eat, sleep, form relationships, and participate in the world, but they use lighter rules unless they become important to a quest, relationship, crime, inheritance, guild matter, or player interaction.

Background simulated groups include:

- Spouses
- Children
- Low-impact household members
- NPCs with few skills
- NPCs with few abilities
- Routine laborers who are not currently tied to a major economic, social, or quest event

Background NPCs can be promoted into full simulation when their life becomes important. For example, a merchant's child might become fully simulated after inheriting a business, witnessing a crime, joining a guild, gaining unusual magic, or becoming central to a quest.

### NPC Memory, Personality, And AI Simulation Engine V1.0

NPC simulation should operate as a suite of AI engines rather than one overloaded system. Multiple AI engines can run simultaneously across regions, settlements, factions, economies, crime, quests, relationships, and moderation support. This reduces the amount of non-AI moderation required while keeping the world active, reactive, and manageable.

Core rule:

- **NPCs live through AI engines, but the world records consequences through shared state.**
- Each engine can specialize in one kind of decision.
- Engines should share memory, reputation, law, contract, economy, and world-event records so they do not contradict each other.
- AI engines can handle routine simulation and low-risk moderation, while major sanctions, protected lore changes, real-money disputes, and severe player appeals should be escalated to owner/moderator review.

#### AI Engine Suite

| Engine | Purpose | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| NPC Life Engine | Runs schedules, needs, routines, aging, work, eating, sleep, family, and daily choices. | A baker wakes before sunrise, bakes bread, notices flour is low, posts a request. |
| Personality Engine | Maintains traits, habits, fears, values, moods, ambitions, and behavioral style. | A proud guard refuses a bribe; a timid clerk avoids confrontation. |
| Memory Engine | Stores, summarizes, compresses, recalls, and shares memories. | An NPC remembers a rescued child, a witnessed theft, or a broken oath. |
| Relationship Engine | Tracks trust, affection, love, fear, resentment, debt, family, romance, and rivalries. | A spouse grows resentful; a friend becomes loyal; a rival respects the player. |
| Economy Engine | Runs inventory, wages, prices, stockpiles, shortages, auctions, banks, and production. | Low iron raises sword prices and creates smithing quests. |
| Quest Generation Engine | Converts NPC needs, faction pressure, market shortages, and threats into quests. | A merchant lacks guards and posts an escort contract. |
| Rumor Engine | Spreads news, lies, gossip, bard songs, scandals, and reputation changes. | A failed bribe becomes tavern gossip. |
| Law And Crime Engine | Tracks crimes, evidence, witnesses, warrants, trials, punishments, and corruption. | A stolen map creates a local investigation and underworld interest. |
| Contract Engine | Handles contracts, oaths, bribes, dark contracts, warlock pacts, and dragon treaties. | A breached contract marks the player as unreliable. |
| Faction Engine | Runs faction goals, diplomacy, wars, recruitment, treasury, contracts, and territory. | A player faction posts construction contracts and negotiates with a guild. |
| Settlement Engine | Runs housing, services, taxes, laws, migration, crime, construction, and public order. | Poor sanitation raises disease risk and slime labor demand. |
| Dungeon Engine | Runs dungeon floors, resource states, monster populations, maps, dragon mood, and raids. | Over-farming strengthens floor guardians. |
| Combat And Raid Engine | Tracks battles, contribution, boss states, loot dice, rescue, and danger escalation. | A healer earns raid reward credit without landing the final hit. |
| Legendary Encounter Engine | Runs legendary lottery odds, clues, failure surges, and critical success cards. | A player finds a black-route token after a perfect stealth delivery. |
| AI Moderation Engine | Watches for rule issues, exploits, abuse, harassment, griefing, economy manipulation, and protected-lore conflicts. | Flags suspicious faction bank withdrawals or repeated harassment reports. |
| World Director Engine | Coordinates major events and prevents systems from contradicting each other. | A city riot, dungeon leak, and faction war are merged into one coherent crisis. |

#### Engine Coordination

All engines should write to shared world records rather than making isolated decisions.

Shared records:

- NPC memory
- Player reputation
- Faction reputation
- Crime records
- Contract records
- Settlement state
- Economy state
- Quest state
- Relationship state
- Dungeon state
- Map and battle fog state
- World event clocks
- Server rumor and announcement history
- Moderator review queue

Coordination rules:

- The World Director Engine resolves conflicts between engines.
- Law and Crime Engine can pause or alter NPC routines when an investigation begins.
- Economy Engine can create Quest Generation Engine requests when inventory is low.
- Relationship Engine can override routine behavior during family emergencies, romance conflicts, betrayal, or grief.
- Settlement Engine can raise faction, crime, disease, migration, and construction events.
- AI Moderation Engine can flag suspicious behavior but should not rewrite lore or permanently punish high-impact cases without review.

#### Simulation Depth Tiers

NPCs should move between simulation tiers as their importance changes.

| Tier | Used For | Simulation Detail |
|---|---|---|
| Dormant Record | Distant NPCs, old records, inactive households. | Name, role, location, key relationships, summary state. |
| Statistical Background | Crowd NPCs and low-impact routine citizens. | Population math, job category, mood, wealth band, family count. |
| Background Simulated | Spouses, children, routine workers, low-skill citizens. | Light schedule, needs, family, job, basic inventory, simple memory. |
| Active Simulated | Nearby NPCs, quest givers, witnesses, merchants, guards. | Detailed routine, inventory, needs, memory, relationship and quest generation. |
| Fully Simulated | Merchants, high nobles, poorest residents, important NPCs. | Deep memory, goals, wealth, politics, social web, frequent decision ticks. |
| Critical Simulated | Legendary NPCs, rulers, faction heads, dragon-linked actors, major witnesses. | Highest detail, long-term planning, event hooks, protected continuity. |

Promotion triggers:

- Player interaction
- Witnessing a crime
- Becoming a quest target
- Marriage, adoption, inheritance, or family importance
- Joining a faction
- Gaining wealth or losing everything
- Discovering magic
- Becoming tied to a contract
- Being harmed, rescued, betrayed, or publicly praised
- Owning important property
- Holding a political or criminal role
- Touching a legendary or dungeon event

Demotion triggers:

- No recent relevance
- No active quest or contract
- No nearby player
- No major relationship ties
- Memory compressed into summary
- Household or job state can be simulated statistically

#### Personality Model

NPC personality should drive decisions, not just dialogue.

Personality fields:

| Field | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Core Values | What the NPC believes matters most. |
| Temperament | Calm, anxious, proud, impulsive, cruel, kind, curious, cautious. |
| Ambition | What the NPC wants long-term. |
| Fear | What the NPC avoids or overreacts to. |
| Vice | Greed, pride, cowardice, envy, wrath, lust for power, addiction, cruelty. |
| Virtue | Mercy, honesty, loyalty, courage, patience, generosity, discipline. |
| Social Style | Friendly, formal, sarcastic, shy, manipulative, blunt, flirtatious. |
| Risk Tolerance | How much danger the NPC accepts. |
| Law Attitude | Lawful, pragmatic, corrupt, rebellious, criminal, idealist. |
| Race/Culture Pressure | How culture, race, family, and homeland shape behavior. |
| Faith Pressure | How deity, church, doubt, or superstition affects decisions. |
| Trauma | Major painful memory that shapes choices. |
| Soft Spot | Who or what can make the NPC bend. |

Personality should influence:

- Prices
- Dialogue
- Quest generation
- Crime choices
- Bribery risk
- Romance compatibility
- Work ethic
- Faction loyalty
- Voting behavior
- Law proposals
- Combat bravery
- Tamed creature care
- Child/family decisions
- Whether they forgive, retaliate, flee, lie, or ask for help

#### Memory Architecture

NPCs can remember from birth to death, but memory should be layered for performance.

Memory layers:

| Layer | Purpose | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Working Memory | Immediate context. | "The player is speaking to me right now." |
| Daily Memory | Recent events. | "My flour delivery failed today." |
| Relationship Memory | Important social history. | "This player saved my sister." |
| Trauma Memory | High-impact fear, grief, betrayal, or harm. | "Bandits killed my caravan." |
| Contract Memory | Deals, debts, oaths, bribes, and betrayals. | "The player broke a Shaohr-blessed contract." |
| Crime Witness Memory | Evidence and testimony. | "I saw the rogue leave with the stolen map." |
| Family Memory | Births, deaths, marriages, adoptions, inheritance. | "My son joined the Knights Guild." |
| Career Memory | Job, training, promotion, firing, craft history. | "I forged my first masterwork blade." |
| Reputation Summary | Compressed public opinion. | "People say she is a fair ruler." |
| Life Chapter | Large compressed era of personal life. | "The famine years." |
| Legacy Memory | Memories passed into family, songs, legal records, reincarnation echoes. | "Our house owes that player everything." |

Memory compression rules:

- Small routine memories fade into summaries.
- Repeated similar events become patterns.
- Major events become permanent or semi-permanent.
- Emotional memories decay slower.
- Public records preserve legal, contract, property, birth, death, crime, and title events.
- Family stories can preserve memories beyond one NPC's life.
- Reincarnation can echo selected soul-impression memories.

#### AI Decision Loop

NPC decisions should follow a consistent loop.

Decision loop:

1. Read immediate needs: hunger, thirst, health, safety, family, work, social state.
2. Read schedule and job duties.
3. Read personality, memories, relationships, and current mood.
4. Read inventory, wealth, tools, housing, and available resources.
5. Read law, contracts, faction rules, and current danger.
6. Read market prices, shortages, and local events.
7. Score possible actions.
8. Choose action.
9. Execute action or generate a request if blocked.
10. Update memory, inventory, wealth, relationships, reputation, and world state.

Action scoring factors:

| Factor | Example |
|---|---|
| Urgency | Starving beats shopping for luxury goods. |
| Duty | A guard patrols unless family crisis or danger interrupts. |
| Personality | A greedy merchant may exploit scarcity; a kind one may ration fairly. |
| Relationship | NPC helps a friend faster than a stranger. |
| Fear | A demon child may avoid Dawnmere-controlled streets. |
| Profit | Merchants choose profitable routes if risk is acceptable. |
| Law | Lawful NPC avoids crimes; corrupt NPC looks for cover. |
| Risk | Cowardly NPC flees; brave NPC helps. |
| Reputation | NPC may seek famous player help or avoid infamous ones. |
| Opportunity | A rare buyer, quest, romance, or contract can alter routine. |

#### Dynamic Quest Generation Rules

NPC-generated quests should come from real blockers.

Quest generation blockers:

- Missing materials
- Missing tools
- Missing money
- Missing workers
- Missing proof
- Lack of safety
- Lack of skill
- Lack of time
- Family emergency
- Legal issue
- Contract obligation
- Crime threat
- Market shortage
- Dungeon resource need
- Faction pressure
- Relationship crisis

Quest route options:

| Route | Use Case |
|---|---|
| Direct Request | NPC knows and trusts the player. |
| Guild Board | Formal work, public need, official request. |
| Contract Board | Deals, trade, dark work, land, faction, or oath tasks. |
| Rumor | NPC lacks status or wants secrecy. |
| Dark Contract | Illegal, hidden, assassination, smuggling, blackmail. |
| Faction Request | Internal faction need or political work. |
| Settlement Request | Public works, food, housing, guards, repairs. |
| Emergency Alert | Fire, plague, attack, riot, dungeon leak, missing child. |

Quest expiration:

- Another NPC may solve it.
- The problem may worsen.
- The NPC may suffer loss.
- The market may change.
- The request may escalate to guild, faction, crime, or settlement event.
- The NPC may remember that the player ignored them.

#### AI Moderation Engine

The AI Moderation Engine exists to reduce the need for constant non-AI moderators by handling routine observation, triage, evidence gathering, low-risk interventions, and escalation queues.

AI moderation can monitor:

- Harassment reports
- Exploit patterns
- Economy manipulation
- Faction bank theft
- Contract scams
- Repeated griefing
- Protected lore violations
- Child-safety rule violations
- Non-explicit adult-content boundaries
- Illegal building abuse
- Land-buy scams
- Chat or trade spam
- Suspicious raid reward manipulation
- Multi-account farming patterns
- Repeated false reports
- Moderator impersonation

AI moderation response tiers:

| Tier | Response |
|---|---|
| Observe | Log behavior and wait for pattern. |
| Warn | Send private warning or rule reminder. |
| Soft Limit | Temporarily slow spam, block repeated exploit action, or require confirmation. |
| Evidence Pack | Collect logs, contract records, chat, trade, location, witness, and system state. |
| Temporary Containment | Freeze suspicious transaction, pause disputed contract, lock faction withdrawal, or hold land sale. |
| Escalate | Send case to Laucian/moderator dashboard or human review. |
| Emergency Protect | Stop severe ongoing harm, especially protected child-safety, real-money, protected lore, or major exploit cases. |

AI moderation limits:

- It should not permanently ban, delete major property, rewrite protected lore, seize kingdoms, alter paid purchases, or resolve severe harassment appeals without review.
- It can handle low-risk routine cases automatically.
- It should always leave an audit trail.
- Players should have appeal paths.
- False reports should affect reporter credibility.
- AI moderation should be visible enough to feel fair, but not so detailed that exploiters can game it.

#### Parallel AI Engine Deployment

Several AI engines can run simultaneously by region, system, or priority.

Deployment model:

| Engine Group | Scope |
|---|---|
| Regional Sim Engines | Run daily life in cities, towns, wilderness zones, and continents. |
| Settlement Engines | Manage services, housing, crime, taxes, construction, and migration. |
| Economy Engines | Manage markets, auctions, supply, demand, contracts, and gold flow. |
| Social Engines | Manage relationships, romance, family, rumors, reputation, and memory. |
| Law Engines | Manage crime, evidence, courts, warrants, punishments, and bribery. |
| Faction Engines | Manage faction diplomacy, war, ranks, treasury, and territory. |
| Dungeon Engines | Manage floors, monsters, resources, boss states, and dragon relationships. |
| Moderator Engines | Monitor player behavior, exploits, disputes, and escalation queues. |
| World Director | Coordinates cross-engine events and resolves contradictions. |

Priority rules:

- Nearby player activity gets higher simulation priority.
- Fully simulated NPCs get deeper ticks.
- Background NPCs use lighter ticks.
- Emergencies override routine schedules.
- Server-wide events get World Director coordination.
- AI moderation cases are prioritized by harm, exploit risk, and number of affected players.

#### AI Engine Data Handoff

Recommended NPC AI data fields:

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| NpcId | Unique NPC identifier. |
| SimulationTier | Dormant, statistical, background, active, full, critical. |
| CurrentEngineOwner | Which engine currently has decision priority. |
| Profile | Name, race, age, household, job, class, stats, skills. |
| Personality | Values, temperament, ambition, fear, virtue, vice, risk tolerance. |
| Needs | Food, water, sleep, safety, health, money, social, family, work. |
| Schedule | Current routine and planned tasks. |
| Inventory | Items, tools, goods, food, money, work materials. |
| WealthState | Income, debt, savings, wages, taxes. |
| RelationshipState | Known relationships and track values. |
| MemoryState | Working, daily, relationship, trauma, contract, crime, family, legacy. |
| ReputationView | What the NPC believes about players, factions, guilds, races, and settlements. |
| CurrentProblems | Blockers that may generate quests. |
| QuestHooks | Potential requests, contracts, rumors, or emergencies. |
| LegalState | Wanted, witness, victim, suspect, criminal, official, protected. |
| FactionState | Membership, loyalty, duties, rank, ideology. |
| LocationState | Home, work, travel, hidden, captured, dead, missing. |
| LastTick | Server time of last simulation update. |
| AuditNotes | Important system decisions for debugging and moderation. |

Recommended AI engine data fields:

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| EngineId | Unique engine identifier. |
| EngineType | Life, economy, law, faction, settlement, dungeon, moderation, etc. |
| Scope | Region, settlement, faction, dungeon, server, or system. |
| Priority | Current urgency level. |
| Inputs | Records and events consumed. |
| Outputs | Decisions, quests, events, updates, alerts. |
| ConflictRules | What happens if another engine disagrees. |
| AuditLog | Why important decisions happened. |
| EscalationRules | When to notify moderator, Laucian, or owner review. |

#### AI Simulation Implementation Checklist

NPC AI simulation is implementation-ready when the game supports:

- Multiple AI engines running in parallel.
- World Director coordination.
- Simulation depth tiers.
- NPC personality fields.
- Layered memory and memory compression.
- Daily needs and schedules.
- Inventory and wealth decisions.
- Dynamic quest generation from blockers.
- Economy loop integration.
- Reputation and relationship integration.
- Crime, law, contract, and faction integration.
- Settlement and dungeon integration.
- AI moderation engine with audit logs.
- Escalation queue for severe cases.
- NPC promotion and demotion between simulation tiers.
- Living server time ticks.
- Performance-safe background simulation.
- Debug tools to inspect why an NPC or engine made a decision.

#### Final AI Simulation Rules

- NPCs should feel like people, not quest machines.
- AI engines should reduce manual moderation by handling routine world movement, routine disputes, and routine evidence gathering.
- Human or owner-level review should remain available for severe, protected, paid, appealed, or world-changing cases.
- Fully simulated NPCs carry the emotional weight of the world.
- Background NPCs keep the world populated without overwhelming performance.
- Memory should be deep where it matters and compressed where it does not.
- Quests should come from needs, not random emptiness.
- The best AI simulation is one where the player feels the world was living before they arrived and will keep living after they leave.

## Admin, AI Moderation, And Server Operations

Server operations are the hidden structure that keeps Huwan alive, fair, stable, and playable. The world should feel self-running through AI-controlled engines, but final authority belongs to the creator/Admin/Moderator when the AI cannot determine a safe outcome.

### Admin, AI Moderation, And Server Operations System Completion V1.0

Core rule:

- **The Admin is final authority. AI engines run the world until a decision becomes unsafe, unclear, protected, or too important to automate.**
- The creator plays as **Laucian Xilocent Goldpetal**, the in-world Admin/Moderator identity and SSS-ranked Mages Guild leader.
- Server operations run primarily through AI-controlled engines.
- The server may use as many AI moderators as needed.
- AI moderators can resolve routine cases, gather evidence, stabilize disputes, and keep the world moving.
- Admin approval is required when AI cannot determine a safe outcome.

#### Authority Structure

| Authority | Role | Scope |
|---|---|---|
| Admin/Creator | Final server authority. | Protected lore, severe disputes, unsafe AI outcomes, major sanctions, world-changing approvals. |
| Laucian Xilocent Goldpetal | In-world Admin/Moderator identity. | Magic authority, Dawnspire, special skills, live events, moderation appearances, fourth-wall comments. |
| AI Moderator Engines | Routine moderation and operations. | Reports, disputes, evidence gathering, anti-exploit checks, minor enforcement. |
| World Director Engine | Coordinates AI systems. | Cross-engine events, conflicting outcomes, crisis merging, safe escalation. |
| System Engines | Specialized world operations. | Economy, law, contracts, factions, settlements, dungeons, NPC life, reputation, relationships. |
| Trusted Human Moderators | Optional future helpers. | Appeals, events, safety review, player support, if appointed by Admin. |

#### AI-Controlled Server Operations

Server operations should be divided across specialized engines.

| Operation Engine | Runs |
|---|---|
| Economy Operations | Markets, auctions, banks, gold flow, resource shortages, price changes. |
| NPC Operations | NPC routines, needs, jobs, memory, inventory, quests. |
| Settlement Operations | Services, housing, taxes, construction, crime, migration. |
| Faction Operations | Ranks, diplomacy, wars, treasury, contracts, territory. |
| Law Operations | Laws, courts, warrants, trials, punishments, pardons. |
| Crime Operations | Evidence, witnesses, wanted levels, bounties, underworld actions. |
| Contract Operations | HUD Contract Board, bribes, oaths, dark contracts, pacts, treaties. |
| Dungeon Operations | Floor states, bosses, maps, resources, dragon relationships, raids. |
| Reputation Operations | Fame, infamy, rumors, titles, World Popularity. |
| Relationship Operations | Romance, family, heirs, tamed companions, legacy, consent states. |
| Legendary Operations | Legendary encounter lottery, rewards, failures, critical success cards. |
| Moderation Operations | Reports, exploits, griefing, harassment, abuse, logs, containment, escalation. |
| Live Event Operations | Server events, announcements, crisis events, festivals, raids, emergency response. |

#### AI Moderator Responsibilities

AI moderators can handle:

- Routine player reports.
- Evidence collection.
- Chat, trade, contract, faction, land, and crime log review.
- Low-risk warnings.
- Spam throttling.
- Exploit pattern detection.
- Duplicate report filtering.
- Suspicious economy movement.
- Faction bank withdrawal flags.
- Land sale dispute holds.
- Contract scam triage.
- Harassment report packaging.
- Protected child-safety rule enforcement.
- Non-explicit adult-content boundary enforcement.
- Moderator impersonation detection.
- Temporary containment for unstable situations.
- Appeal packet creation.

AI moderators should always leave audit logs.

#### Admin Approval Required

AI must request Admin approval when:

- The AI cannot determine a safe outcome.
- The case involves protected lore.
- The case involves Laucian, Dawnspire, Myth Table characters, Legendary True characters, or special guest characters.
- A permanent ban, major suspension, account-level sanction, or paid-content dispute is involved.
- A city, kingdom, dragon-born dungeon, major faction, player settlement, or major player property may be seized, deleted, rolled back, or transferred.
- A world event would permanently alter terrain, laws, faction balance, dragon state, deity influence, or economy.
- A player appeal challenges AI moderation.
- Evidence is contradictory or likely forged.
- A harassment, abuse, or safety case is severe.
- A real-money purchase, premium item, or monetized asset is involved.
- The AI suspects large-scale exploitation but cannot prove intent.
- Two AI engines disagree and the World Director cannot safely resolve the conflict.

#### Safe Outcome Standard

An AI outcome is considered safe when it is:

- Reversible or low impact.
- Supported by clear evidence.
- Consistent with world rules.
- Consistent with server rules.
- Not tied to protected lore.
- Not tied to paid ownership or major property loss.
- Not permanently punitive.
- Not likely to create cascading world damage.
- Not likely to unfairly favor one player faction.
- Logged with a clear explanation.

If any of these are uncertain, the AI escalates.

#### Moderation Response Ladder

| Tier | Response | Approval Needed |
|---|---|---|
| Observe | Log behavior and wait for pattern. | No. |
| Nudge | Give quiet UI reminder or rule hint. | No. |
| Warn | Send formal warning. | Usually no. |
| Soft Limit | Slow spam, block repeated exploit action, require confirmation. | No for low-risk. |
| Temporary Hold | Freeze disputed trade, contract, land sale, or faction withdrawal. | No for short holds; yes for extension. |
| Evidence Packet | Prepare case for review. | No. |
| Temporary Containment | Short-term containment during active harm or exploit. | Sometimes, depending on severity. |
| Escalation | Send to Admin dashboard. | Yes. |
| Major Sanction | Long suspension, asset seizure, protected lore action, permanent ban. | Yes. |
| Emergency World Action | Rollback, major event halt, city/kingdom/dungeon protection. | Yes unless immediate harm requires temporary freeze. |

#### Admin Dashboard

The Admin dashboard is the out-of-world control center, while Laucian is the in-world face of that authority.

Dashboard panels:

- AI moderation queue
- Player reports
- Appeal requests
- Evidence packets
- Chat and trade logs
- Contract disputes
- Crime records
- Land and city ownership disputes
- Faction bank logs
- Economy anomaly reports
- Exploit pattern alerts
- Protected lore alerts
- World event queue
- Live event tools
- Server announcement tools
- Emergency freeze tools
- Rollback review
- AI engine health
- NPC simulation load
- Dungeon/dragon state
- Settlement crisis state
- Faction war state
- Premium/paid item dispute queue

#### Laucian In-World Moderator Role

Laucian allows moderation to appear diegetic inside Huwan.

Laucian can:

- Appear in Dawnspire.
- Pull players into a controlled white lab space.
- Grant or revoke magic-related permissions when appropriate.
- Investigate forbidden magic.
- Pause dangerous magical events in-world.
- Explain world rules through fourth-wall humor.
- Deliver warnings as magical notices.
- Create test spaces.
- Run live magical events.
- Approve special magic creation.
- Witness high-tier pacts, contracts, or oaths.
- Intervene when AI engines escalate a case that needs Admin judgment.

Laucian should not be used casually to solve normal player problems. His appearances should feel rare, funny, unsettling, and important.

#### Evidence And Audit Logs

Every automated moderation decision should generate an audit trail.

Audit records can include:

- Player IDs
- NPC IDs
- Faction IDs
- Settlement IDs
- Contract IDs
- Crime IDs
- Trade logs
- Chat logs
- Location history
- Combat logs
- Inventory movement
- Bank movement
- Property deeds
- Build Mode changes
- Law and court records
- Witness memories
- AI reasoning summary
- Engine disagreement notes
- Recommended action
- Escalation reason
- Admin decision
- Appeal result

Audit logs should be readable enough for review but protected from players who might exploit the details.

#### AI Engine Health And Oversight

Server operations must monitor the AI engines themselves.

AI health checks:

- Is an engine making repetitive bad decisions?
- Are too many cases being escalated?
- Are too few cases being escalated?
- Is one faction benefiting too much from AI outcomes?
- Are NPC economies spiraling?
- Are quests flooding the board?
- Are crime records becoming inconsistent?
- Are rumors spreading too fast?
- Are settlements collapsing from simulation errors?
- Are dungeons over-producing rare resources?
- Are moderation warnings being applied unevenly?

If an AI engine appears unsafe, the World Director or Admin can:

- Pause the engine.
- Lower its authority.
- Route decisions to review.
- Reset a bad event clock.
- Roll back limited state.
- Apply a hotfix rule.
- Mark cases for audit.

#### Live Event Operations

Live events can be run by AI engines, Admin tools, or Laucian in-world.

Live event types:

- Festivals
- Dungeon raids
- Dragon attacks
- City emergencies
- Faction wars
- Market crashes
- Divine omens
- Dawnspire invitations
- Legendary NPC appearances
- Slime strikes
- Court trials
- Kingdom auctions
- World boss awakenings
- Server anniversary events

AI can manage routine live events. Admin approval is required for world-changing events, protected lore events, major reward events, and anything involving Laucian's unique authority.

#### Rollback And Emergency Protection

Rollback should be rare and targeted.

Rollback can be considered for:

- Major exploit damage.
- Severe economy break.
- Unauthorized protected-lore alteration.
- Accidental deletion or transfer of major property.
- Paid item corruption.
- AI engine runaway event.
- Severe griefing with clear evidence.
- City, kingdom, faction, or dungeon corruption caused by system error.

Preferred order:

1. Pause the affected system.
2. Preserve evidence.
3. Freeze affected assets.
4. Notify affected players where appropriate.
5. Review scope.
6. Apply targeted rollback if needed.
7. Restore or compensate.
8. Record audit.
9. Adjust AI rules to prevent repeat.

#### Server Operations Data Handoff

Recommended data fields:

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| CaseId | Unique moderation or operations case. |
| CaseType | Report, exploit, land dispute, contract dispute, harassment, economy, protected lore, appeal. |
| EngineSource | AI engine that created the case. |
| Severity | Low, moderate, high, severe, world-critical. |
| SafeOutcomeConfidence | AI confidence that it can resolve safely. |
| EscalationRequired | Whether Admin approval is required. |
| AffectedPlayers | Players involved. |
| AffectedNPCs | NPCs involved. |
| AffectedAssets | Items, land, cities, factions, dungeons, contracts, paid assets. |
| EvidenceLinks | Logs, records, memory, contracts, trades, chats, world state. |
| RecommendedAction | AI recommendation. |
| TemporaryAction | Any containment already applied. |
| AdminDecision | Final decision if escalated. |
| AppealState | None, pending, accepted, denied, resolved. |
| AuditSummary | Human-readable summary. |
| FinalOutcome | Warning, no action, fix, rollback, sanction, compensation, event update. |

#### Server Operations Implementation Checklist

Server operations are implementation-ready when the game supports:

- Admin account and role permissions.
- Laucian moderator login identity.
- Multiple AI moderator engines.
- World Director escalation logic.
- Admin approval queue.
- Evidence packets.
- Audit logs.
- Player reports.
- Appeals.
- Temporary containment tools.
- Trade, contract, land, faction, crime, and chat log review.
- Protected lore flags.
- Paid asset protection.
- Live event tools.
- Server announcements.
- AI engine health monitoring.
- Targeted rollback tools.
- Compensation tools.
- Rule update tools.
- Moderator impersonation protection.

#### Final Server Operations Rules

- The AI engines run the server, but the Admin owns the final call.
- AI moderators can be numerous, tireless, and specialized.
- AI should resolve routine problems and escalate unsafe ones.
- If the AI cannot determine a safe outcome, it asks for Admin approval.
- Laucian is the in-world face of Admin authority, not a normal NPC.
- Protected lore, paid assets, major sanctions, and world-changing outcomes require extra care.
- Every serious decision needs evidence.
- Every automated decision needs an audit trail.
- The goal is not to remove human judgment. The goal is to save human judgment for the moments that truly need it.

## Survival and Adventure Layer

Huwan's wilderness should feel alive, dangerous, useful, and economically connected to settlements. Biomes contain monster populations, animal populations, flora, fauna, resources, ruins, and farmable threats. These areas feed quests, crafting, guild work, market supply, faction movement, and player progression.

### Biome Types

| Biome | Adventure Role | Common Resources |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Deep Forests | Dangerous old-growth wilds, hidden fae paths, treants, dryads, hags, sprites, monster nests, and ancient ruins. | Rare herbs, magical wood, mushrooms, monster hides, fae materials, dryad bark, old relics. |
| Regular Forests | Early hunting grounds, gathering zones, bandit cover, animal habitats, and starter monster areas. | Wood, herbs, berries, game meat, hides, basic monster drops. |
| Mountains | Harsh travel, wyvern nests, griffin cliffs, dragons, mining routes, hidden monasteries, and old fortresses. | Ore, gems, stone, dragon scales, wyvern parts, griffin feathers. |
| Great Plains | Caravan routes, horse herds, goblin camps, oversized beast migrations, hunting grounds, and battlefield ruins. | Grain, wild grasses, meat, hides, bones, riding animals, battlefield scrap. |
| Wide Rivers | Trade movement, fishing, river monsters, mermaid routes, bridges, ferries, smuggling, and flood danger. | Fish, reeds, river pearls, clay, water herbs, aquatic monster parts. |
| Huge Lakes | Deep water mysteries, mermaids, sharks, megalodons, lake ruins, drowned towns, and hidden temples. | Fish, pearls, lake minerals, alchemy plants, scales, ancient salvage. |
| Deep Caverns | Underground exploration, undead pockets, slimes, bugs, goblins, imps, ore veins, and sealed era tunnels. | Ore, crystals, fungi, slime cores, bug shells, bones, ancient stonework. |
| Quarries | Stone production, labor quests, golem activity, accidents, resource shortages, and guild contracts. | Stone, marble, granite, construction materials, iron golem parts. |
| Heavy Ruins | Destroyed-era remnants, artifacts, undead, clockwork monsters, magical traps, cursed rooms, and lore discoveries. | Relics, enchanted scrap, old coins, cursed items, artifact fragments, lost records. |

Each biome should support its own ecosystem instead of existing only as a combat arena.

### Ecology Categories

Huwan creatures can be grouped by how they affect survival, economy, and quests.

| Category | Examples | World Function |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Basic animals | Horses, deer, wolves, bears, birds, fish, livestock | Food, transport, hunting, hides, farming, animal handling, local danger. |
| Small monsters | Slimes, bugs, goblins, imps | Starter combat, gathering drops, infestation quests, village threats. |
| Humanlike threats | Bandits, raiders, deserters, smugglers, cult scouts | Road danger, ambushes, criminal quests, escort contracts, stolen goods, faction pressure. |
| Mutated oversized animals | Giant wolves, giant boars, huge spiders, oversized bears | Escalated wilderness danger and monster-population quests. |
| Magical beasts | Wyverns, chimera, griffins, dragons | High-risk hunts, rare materials, territory threats, prestige quests. |
| Nature spirits and fae creatures | Treants, sprites, dryads | Forest diplomacy, fae pacts, resource protection, magical ecology. |
| Undead | Skeletons, ghosts, liches, zombies, wraiths | Church quests, cursed ruins, necromancy, death lore, graveyard danger. |
| Dark folklore creatures | Hags, bog monsters | Swamps, curses, bargains, kidnappings, hidden lairs, moral-choice quests. |
| Farmable monsters | Iron golems, fire wraiths, ice drakes, dryads | Area-specific resource loops and repeatable but risky gathering. |
| Aquatic creatures | Mermaids, sharks, megalodons | Water travel, lake danger, underwater diplomacy, fishing economy. |
| Artificial monsters | Clockwork monsters | Artificer accidents, ruin guardians, broken automation, rare mechanical parts. |

### Monster Hierarchy and Biome Level Logic

Monster difficulty should follow basic lore and ecological logic. Safer biomes near towns contain weaker creatures. Older, deeper, harsher, more magical, or more isolated areas contain stronger monsters.

Suggested threat ranks:

| Threat Rank | Typical Player Readiness | Monster Role |
| --- | --- | --- |
| F Rank | New players and Progression Zero characters | Starter gathering threats, weak pests, low-risk hunting. |
| E Rank | Early trained players | Slightly dangerous wildlife, small monster packs, weak bandits. |
| D Rank | Players with early gear upgrades or first class progress | Organized monster groups, dangerous animals, minor magical threats. |
| C Rank | Competent adventurers | Elite monsters, stronger undead, local bosses, serious wilderness threats. |
| B Rank | Veteran adventurers | Large magical beasts, ruin guardians, deadly monster packs. |
| A Rank | High-level parties | Dragonscale threats, ancient undead, powerful dungeon bosses. |
| S Rank | Legendary adventurers | Ancient dragons, demi-god monsters, world-level threats. |
| SS Rank | Raid groups, server events, or story-scale intervention | Nearly impossible solo threats, demi-gods, world bosses, catastrophic entities. |
| SSS Rank | Unique absolute individual classification | Reserved for Laucian Xilocent Goldpetal, the only known SSS-ranked individual ever. |

Hierarchy inside a monster family:

- Lesser or young variant
- Common adult variant
- Pack leader or mature variant
- Elite variant
- Named monster
- Boss variant
- Apex or mythic variant
- SS rank world boss variant, when the creature becomes a raid-only or world-scale threat

Example slime hierarchy:

- Basic Slime
- Elemental or material slime
- King Slime
- Gargantuan Slime
- Demi-God Slime

### Biome Monster Level Bands

| Biome | Common Threat Band | Logical Monsters | Higher-Tier Exceptions |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Open Fields | F-E | Slimes, small bugs, basic animals, weak goblin scouts, stray imps, bandits in tiny groups. | King Slime, mutated oversized animals, hidden faction ambush. |
| Small Streams | F-E | Water slimes, small fish predators, bugs, snakes, small bog creatures. | Mermaid encounter, river hag, polluted slime swarm. |
| Small Forests | F-D | Basic animals, wolves, slimes, bugs, goblins, bandits, sprites. | Treant guardian, hag hut, corrupted animal pack. |
| Regular Forests | E-C | Wolves, bears, goblins, bandits, sprites, dryads, treants, oversized animals. | Forest chimera, ancient treant, fae curse boss. |
| Deep Forests | D-A | Treants, dryads, hags, bog monsters, sprites, giant insects, corrupted animals. | Fae lord, elder treant, ancient forest dragon, demi-god slime. |
| Great Plains | F-C | Horses, wild game, goblins, bandits, oversized boars, wolves, raiders. | Griffin hunt, chimera migration, warband leader. |
| Mountains | D-A | Wyverns, griffins, mountain beasts, bandits, ice drakes, stone creatures. | Dragon lair, ancient giant, high mountain chimera. |
| Wide Rivers | E-C | Fish predators, river slimes, bog monsters, bandits near bridges, mermaids. | River hag queen, giant serpent, corrupted mermaid faction. |
| Huge Lakes | D-A | Sharks, mermaids, lake monsters, drowned undead, aquatic slimes. | Megalodon, ancient lake dragon, drowned dungeon entrance. |
| Deep Caverns | E-B | Bugs, slimes, goblins, imps, undead pockets, cave beasts. | Lich remnant, deep slime colony, demon breach, ruin guardian. |
| Quarries | F-C | Bugs, stone slimes, bandits, iron golems, clockwork accidents. | Rampaging iron golem, buried ruin guardian, artificer-made boss. |
| Heavy Ruins | C-S | Undead, clockwork monsters, traps, cursed creatures, artifact guardians. | Lich, ancient construct, sealed demon, relic-bound boss. |
| Dragon-Born Dungeons Floors 1-10 | F-D | Slimes, bugs, goblins, weak undead, dungeon animals, traps. | Floor 10 boss. |
| Dragon-Born Dungeons Floors 11-40 | D-B | Stronger monsters, elite packs, magical beasts, complex traps. | Boss every 10 floors. |
| Dragon-Born Dungeons Floors 41-70 | C-A | Mana-warped monsters, high-density ore guardians, stronger undead, dangerous labyrinths. | Mana crystal guardians after floor 50. |
| Dragon-Born Dungeons Floors 71-99 | B-S | Apex dungeon monsters, dragon-touched elites, high-risk bosses, severe traps. | Legendary floor bosses. |
| Dragon-Born Dungeon Floor 100 | S-SS | Ancient Dragon. | Killing it kills the dungeon. Some ancient dragons may be SS rank and require raid-level preparation. |

### Biome Logic Rules

- Monsters near towns should usually be weaker unless a crisis, dungeon leak, faction plot, or boss migration disrupts the region.
- Intelligent monsters may avoid strong patrol routes but gather near trade roads, ruins, poor borderlands, or weakly defended villages.
- Magical creatures grow stronger near mana crystals, artifacts, dragon-born dungeons, deep forests, Dawnspire influence, or destroyed-era ruins.
- Undead gather near battlefields, graveyards, cursed ruins, death magic, failed resurrection sites, or neglected burial grounds.
- Bandits follow money, hunger, desperation, politics, and weak enforcement rather than pure biome logic.
- Farmable monsters should have predictable habitats so players can learn where to gather specific materials.
- Bosses should have ecological or story reasons for existing, such as territory, mutation, leadership, artifact exposure, dungeon mana, or faction control.

### Initial Creature List

Confirmed creatures and monster types:

- Slimes
- Bugs
- Basic animals
- Mutated oversized monster animals
- Wyverns
- Chimera
- Griffins
- Treants
- Sprites
- Dragons
- Horses
- Goblins
- Imps
- Bandits
- Undead creatures
- Hags
- Bog monsters
- Iron golems
- Fire wraiths
- Ice drakes
- Dryads
- Mermaids
- Sharks
- Megalodons
- Clockwork monsters

### Farmable Monster Design

Some monsters are dangerous but economically important. These are area-specific farmable monsters, meaning guilds, merchants, crafters, and players may repeatedly seek them for materials.

Examples:

- Iron golems provide iron cores, enchanted metal, stone plates, and construction-grade parts.
- Fire wraiths provide ember essence, heat crystals, fire salts, and flame-resistant materials.
- Ice drakes provide frost scales, chilled blood, ice glands, and cold-infused bones.
- Dryads provide living bark, healing sap, nature-thread, and rare seeds, but farming them may create moral or faction consequences.

Farmable does not mean harmless. Overhunting can anger factions, damage biomes, change local prices, or create stronger monster variants.

### Clockwork Monsters

Clockwork monsters are artificial creatures accidentally created by artificers. They may come from failed experiments, abandoned workshops, self-replicating machines, broken guardians, or ruin technology that modern crafters do not fully understand.

They can provide:

- Gears
- Springs
- Clockwork hearts
- Artificer cores
- Precision metal
- Strange batteries
- Automaton limbs

They can also create unique problems:

- Attacking workers in quarries
- Repeating old commands forever
- Guarding ruins after their creators are dead
- Mistaking civilians for intruders
- Replicating without permission
- Rebuilding themselves from market scrap

### Adventure Economy

Biomes and monster populations feed the same living economy as NPC simulation.

- More monsters create more Adventurers Guild quests.
- Resource shortages create merchant and crafting requests.
- Dangerous travel raises escort prices.
- Overhunting can lower monster drops but damage ecology.
- Rare creature parts feed auctions and high-end crafting.
- Church demand rises when undead activity rises.
- Knights Guild patrols increase when roads become unsafe.
- The Archers Guild gains importance when gathering, hunting, fishing, and raw materials are in high demand.

### Weapons, Armor, Gear, and Clothing

Weapons, armor, gear, and clothing in Huwan can scale from basic starter equipment to legendary relics, living slime equipment, dragon-forged gear, divine artifacts, cursed tools, guild uniforms, noble fashion, and player-created masterpieces.

Because item possibilities are nearly endless, equipment design should begin with systems before giant item lists.

#### Gear Design Order

1. Item category
2. User role
3. Required materials
4. Required tools or crafting station
5. Required job, class, skill, or ability
6. Base stats
7. Durability
8. Weight and handling
9. Rarity
10. Special traits
11. Upgrade path
12. Repair rules
13. Market value
14. Lore or origin

#### Core Weapon Families

- Swords
- Daggers
- Axes
- Maces
- Hammers
- Spears
- Polearms
- Staffs
- Wands
- Bows
- Crossbows
- Throwing weapons
- Shields used offensively
- Unarmed weapons
- Whips
- Firearms or artificer weapons, if allowed later
- Living weapons, such as weapon slimes

Each weapon family can contain many variants. For example, bows alone can include shortbows, longbows, recurve bows, composite bows, war bows, hunting bows, spirit bows, bone bows, dragonhorn bows, ironwood bows, mana bows, slime bows, and guild-crafted bows.

#### Armor Families

- Cloth armor
- Light armor
- Medium armor
- Heavy armor
- Robes
- Shields
- Helmets
- Gloves
- Boots
- Cloaks
- Rings
- Amulets
- Belts
- Living armor, such as armor slimes
- Mount armor

Armor should protect against more than physical damage. Some armor can resist fire, cold, poison, curses, holy energy, infernal energy, death magic, drowning, disease, fear, or mana instability.

#### Gear and Utility Items

- Quivers
- Arrows
- Bolts
- Tools
- Pickaxes
- Hatchets
- Fishing rods
- Skinning knives
- Alchemy kits
- Cooking kits
- Lockpicks
- Climbing gear
- Lanterns
- Backpacks
- Tents
- Bedrolls
- Maps
- Compasses
- Spellbooks
- Holy symbols
- Mana focuses
- Mount tack
- Monster harvesting kits
- Repair kits

#### Clothing

Clothing is separate from armor but still matters for roleplay, social status, warmth, disguise, guild identity, noble fashion, culture, and class requirements.

Clothing types:

- Common clothes
- Work clothes
- Noble clothes
- Guild uniforms
- Religious garments
- Mage robes
- Traveler outfits
- Cold-weather clothing
- Formalwear
- Disguises
- Race-specific clothing
- Enchanted clothing

#### Player Crafting

Players can create weapons, armor, gear, and clothes if they have the correct combination of:

- Skills
- Abilities
- Jobs
- Classes
- Resources
- Tools
- Crafting stations
- Recipes
- Time
- Knowledge
- Assistants, if required

Crafting should connect directly to the resource loop. A player who wants to create a bow may need wood, string, horn, glue, tools, a workbench, bowyer skill, and possibly feathers or special arrowheads for matching ammunition.

#### Crafting Professions and Job Hooks

- Blacksmith: metal weapons, armor, tools, repairs.
- Carpenter: wood gear, bows, handles, shields, furniture.
- Bowyer: bows, crossbows, bowstrings, arrow shafts.
- Fletcher: arrows, bolts, feathers, specialty ammunition.
- Tailor: clothes, robes, light armor, padding.
- Leatherworker: leather armor, boots, gloves, belts, packs.
- Armorer: specialized armor creation and fitting.
- Jeweler: rings, amulets, socketed gems, noble accessories.
- Alchemist: coatings, oils, bombs, monster-part processing.
- Enchanter: magical traits, mana channels, element binding.
- Artificer: clockwork gear, mechanical weapons, special tools.
- Slime Breeder: weapon slimes, armor slimes, cleaner slimes, utility slimes.

#### Item Rarity

Suggested rarity ladder:

- Common
- Uncommon
- Rare
- Epic
- Legendary
- Mythic
- Divine
- Relic

Relic items do not respawn and may require compatibility by job, class, race, bloodline, alignment, mana wells, or divine connection.

#### Starter Gear Philosophy

Starter gear should be humble, useful, and replaceable. It should help the player begin a life rather than immediately feel heroic.

Examples:

- Worn knife
- Simple bow
- Crude arrows
- Cloth tunic
- Traveler boots
- Small pouch
- Basic gathering knife
- Wooden shield
- Training sword
- Beginner staff
- Work gloves
- Simple backpack

Starter gear can come from family, guild registration, charity, old military stock, thrift markets, poor district hand-me-downs, or the player's first job.

#### Confirmed Starter Weapons

Starter weapons are basic, low-rank weapons that let players begin experimenting with class direction before committing to a full class path.

| Weapon | Category | Early Role |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Basic Sword | Sword | Balanced starter melee weapon. |
| Basic Knife | Dagger/knife | Fast close-range weapon and utility tool. |
| Basic Bow | Bow | Starter ranged weapon for hunting, scouting, and basic combat. |
| Basic Staff | Staff | Starter weapon for travelers, monks, mages, and defensive fighters. |
| Basic Gloves | Unarmed weapon | Starter hand-to-hand weapon for brawlers, martial artists, and laborers. |
| Basic Longsword | Sword | Heavier sword for strength-focused melee players. |
| Basic Axe | Axe | Chopping tool and starter weapon with strong basic damage. |
| Basic Glaive | Polearm | Reach weapon for players who want spacing and sweeping attacks. |
| Basic Morning Star | Mace/flail family | Blunt and piercing weapon useful against armor or hard-bodied monsters. |
| Basic Shuriken | Throwing weapon | Starter throwing weapon for stealth, rogues, scouts, and ninja-style paths. |
| Basic Falchion | Curved sword | Slashing weapon with aggressive melee identity. |
| Basic Katana | Sword | Technique-focused blade for disciplined sword paths. |
| Basic Spirit Weapon | Spiritual/magical weapon | Starter mystical weapon tied to spirit, mana, class awakening, or special origin. |

Tools determine what players can gather, craft, repair, farm, cook, steal, travel with, or safely harvest from the world.

| Tool or Item | Category | Early Role |
