Because mana crystals are deep-dungeon resources, they should create major economic and political pressure. Guilds, nobles, mages, artificers, thieves, and warlords all want them.

#### Relics

Relics are found in ruins and destroyed-era sites. They are not respawnable.

Relics are finite pieces of history, magic, faith, lost technology, or divine influence. Finding one should matter.

Relic rules:

- Relics do not respawn.
- Relics are usually found in ruins, heavy ruins, sealed sites, ancient temples, destroyed-era battlefields, and hidden vaults.
- Only certain relics can be used by certain players.
- Compatibility can depend on job, class, race, sub-race, alignment, bloodline, mana wells, divine blessing, curse resistance, or artifact history.
- Some relics may reject, curse, injure, possess, or ignore incompatible users.

Relics can support:

- Class unlocks
- Job advancement
- Legendary quests
- Divine lore
- Era history
- Faction conflict
- Auctions
- Noble scandals
- Dungeon keys
- Hidden race unlocks

### Bandits

Bandits are a major non-monster survival threat. They appear along trade routes, forest roads, mountain passes, ruin outskirts, and poor borderlands where law enforcement is weak.

Bandit activity can affect:

- Merchant prices
- Caravan safety
- Escort quests
- Knights Guild patrols
- Thieves Guild contracts
- Poor district recruitment
- Stolen goods markets
- Noble house scandals
- Adventurers Guild bounty boards

Bandits should not all feel identical. Some are desperate poor citizens, some are ex-soldiers, some are professional criminals, some are faction agents, and some are monsters or undead pretending to be ordinary raiders.

### Dragon-Born Dungeons

Dungeons in Huwan are created when an **Ancient Dragon** settles somewhere and sleeps for **1,000 years**.

While the dragon sleeps, its mana continues pouring into the surrounding area. Over centuries, this constant mana flow creates a dungeon. The dungeon is not only a cave or ruin. It is a magical structure shaped by the dragon's power, dreams, instincts, hoard, memories, element, fears, and ancient history.

Each true dragon-born dungeon contains **100 floors**.

Dungeon contents can include:

- High-density ores
- Treasure chests
- Monsters
- Traps
- Labyrinths
- Magical environments
- Rare resources
- Boss rooms
- Ancient records
- Dragon-influenced mutations

Each floor is magically created. One floor may look like a cave, while another may look like an open sky, a forest, a frozen lake, a ruined city, a desert, a battlefield, a temple, or a place that should not physically fit underground.

#### Dungeon Structure

| Dungeon Section | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| Floors 1-9 | Standard exploration, monsters, traps, chests, ores, and labyrinth paths. |
| Floor 10 | Rest point and boss room. |
| Floors 11-19 | Increased difficulty, stronger resources, deeper dungeon theme. |
| Floor 20 | Rest point and boss room. |
| Floors 21-99 | Continued escalation with rest points and boss rooms every 10 floors. |
| Floor 100 | Ancient Dragon chamber and final dungeon heart. |

Every 10 floors includes:

- A rest point
- A boss room

Rest points are temporary safe zones where players can recover, regroup, sort inventory, repair gear, manage wounds, and prepare for the next section. These zones may also include strange merchants, lost explorers, magical fountains, trapped spirits, dragon memories, or one-way exits.

#### Dragon Resolution Paths

Players do not have to solve every dragon-born dungeon by killing the dragon. A true Ancient Dragon can be defeated, befriended, or tamed, and each outcome changes the dungeon's future.

| Resolution | Dungeon Outcome | Player Outcome | World Impact |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Kill the Dragon | The dungeon dies and disappears forever after a short collapse period. | Players have limited time to gather loot and escape. | Region loses the dungeon economy permanently, but also loses the danger. |
| Befriend the Dragon | The dungeon remains alive, protected, and infinitely farmable. | Players gain dragon trust, access, protection, or negotiated benefits. | The dungeon becomes a stable long-term resource if the relationship is maintained. |
| Tame the Dragon | The dungeon remains alive and comes under player control. | Players can control the dragon-born dungeon, its contents, and its economy. | Massive political and economic consequences; guilds, nobles, mages, and factions will react. |

#### Killing the Dragon

Defeating and killing the Ancient Dragon on floor 100 kills the dungeon.

Once the dragon dies, the dungeon loses its source of mana and begins collapsing, unraveling, or ejecting itself from reality. Players have barely enough time to gather final loot and get out.

The final escape should feel urgent. Players may need to choose between:

- Gathering treasure
- Saving allies
- Recovering artifacts
- Escaping alive
- Stabilizing trapped NPCs
- Claiming dragon materials
- Preventing other adventurers from dying

After the dungeon dies, its long-term effects may remain. Nearby land may change, markets may flood with rare materials, factions may fight over dragon remains, and ancient secrets may escape into the world.

#### Befriending the Dragon

Befriending an Ancient Dragon preserves the dungeon.

The dungeon remains protected and infinitely farmable because the dragon continues supplying mana. The dragon may allow controlled access, safer rest points, negotiated resource harvests, protected trade, or special trials.

Befriending requires more than charisma. Players may need to understand the dragon's history, respect its domain, solve old wounds, return stolen hoard pieces, spare its children, defeat invaders, or prove they will not destroy the balance of the dungeon.

Benefits can include:

- Stable dungeon farming
- Dragon protection
- Special dragon quests
- Safer access to selected floors
- Dragon-touched crafting materials
- Reputation with dragon-aligned factions
- Rare knowledge about ancient eras

Risks:

- The dragon may demand tribute.
- Guilds may fight over access rights.
- Killing parties may try to break the alliance.
- The dragon may withdraw trust if exploited.

#### Taming the Dragon

Taming an Ancient Dragon is the most powerful and dangerous outcome.

If players tame the dragon, they gain control over the dragon-born dungeon and its contents. This includes influence over monster populations, resource flow, floor access, dungeon farming, loot availability, dungeon rules, and the dungeon economy.

Possible control options:

- Open or close access to floors.
- Adjust farming permissions.
- Protect certain resources.
- Allow guild contracts.
- Direct monster pressure.
- Assign dungeon guardians.
- Stabilize or alter rest points.
- Encourage specific resource growth.
- Control who profits from dungeon materials.

Taming a dragon should be extremely difficult and may require rare skills, abilities, class paths, social bonds, mana compatibility, dragon lore, relics, or story choices. It should be closer to forming a world-changing pact than simply capturing a mount.

Consequences:

- Merchants Guild may want contracts.
- Adventurers Guild may demand fair access.
- Mages Guild may monitor the player's control of dungeon mana.
- Noble houses may try to claim ownership.
- Thieves Guild may try to steal maps, keys, or access rights.
- Factions may view the player as a future ruler, threat, or disaster.

### First Dragon-Born Dungeon: The Verdant Maw

**Name:** The Verdant Maw  
**Location:** Beneath the old rootlands east of Eldoria, where regular forest thickens into deep forest.  
**Dungeon Type:** Dragon-born dungeon  
**Sleeping Dragon:** Ysgravelle the Root-Crowned, Ancient Forest Dragon  
**Dragon Rank:** S, potentially SS if awakened improperly  
**Current Public Knowledge:** Most citizens believe it is an unusually dangerous ruin-cave system. Only Orro Vane, the Adventurers Guild, the Thieves Guild, and parts of House Moonveil suspect it is a true dragon-born dungeon.

The Verdant Maw formed after Ysgravelle the Root-Crowned settled beneath a colossal root basin and slept for 1,000 years. Her mana seeped into roots, stone, old ruins, underground rivers, and buried bones, creating a 100-floor dungeon that feels alive.

The dungeon's identity is forest, hunger, memory, and growth. Its labyrinths shift like roots searching for water. Some floors resemble caves. Some resemble moonlit forests. Some open into impossible skies beneath the earth. Some contain ruins from earlier eras tangled in living wood.

#### Dungeon Entry Region

The entrance sits inside the **Mossgate Sink**, a collapsed woodland hollow surrounded by tall grass, root arches, old stones, and slime-filled pools.

Early threats around the entrance:

- Basic slimes
- Wood slimes
- Bugs
- Weak goblin scavengers
- Small corrupted animals
- Bandits pretending to be guides

Early resources around the entrance:

- Basic wood
- Herbs
- Moss
- Slime gel
- Slime cores
- Small animal hides
- Old stone
- Clay
- Low-grade iron traces

#### Labyrinth Logic

The Verdant Maw uses living labyrinth logic.

Rules:

- Paths shift slowly like growing roots, usually after parties leave a floor.
- Main routes remain stable enough for maps to work, but shortcuts and side chambers can change.
- Labyrinth walls may be root, stone, bark, bone, moss, or ruin masonry.
- The dungeon produces false clearings that look safe but contain ambush roots, spores, or slime pools.
- Some doors grow open only when fed water, blood, mana, sunlight magic, or specific monster drops.
- Chests may grow from root knots, stone hollows, ancient coffers, or dragon-memory blooms.
- Floors remember repeated player behavior and may adapt minor traps against common tactics.

The Thieves Guild has the most accurate maps, but their maps are expensive and sometimes intentionally incomplete. Orro Vane has old maps that are safer for early floors but unreliable after floor 10.

#### Floor Themes

| Floors | Theme | Threat Band | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1-9 | Root Caves and Slime Grass | F-D | Starter dungeon region with slimes, bugs, goblins, roots, pits, and low-grade ores. |
| 10 | Bramble Rest and Thornjaw Boss Room | D | First rest point and boss. Tests whether players understand traps, slimes, and positioning. |
| 11-20 | Moonlit Underforest | D-C | Artificial night forest with sprites, dryads, wolves, poison plants, and shifting paths. |
| 21-30 | Buried Orchard Ruins | C | Old ruins wrapped in fruiting trees, undead gardeners, corrupted animals, and puzzle doors. |
| 31-40 | River Under Sky | C-B | Impossible river valley with open sky illusion, mermaid traces, water slimes, and drowning traps. |
| 41-50 | Ironroot Quarry | B | Dense ores, iron slimes, stone bugs, golem fragments, and mining hazards. |
| 51-60 | Crystal Canopy | B-A | Mana crystals begin appearing. Mana-warped beasts, spell traps, and crystal growths. |
| 61-70 | Gravewood Spiral | A | Death slimes, ghost slimes, undead roots, old adventurer echoes, and memory traps. |
| 71-80 | Dragon's Dream Hunt | A-S | Chimera, wyvern echoes, griffin shades, dragon-touched predators, and elite labyrinth hunters. |
| 81-90 | Crown of Devouring Green | S | Living forest palace, ancient treant wardens, dryad queens, and divine-scale nature magic. |
| 91-99 | Ysgravelle's Coiled Memory | S-SS | Floors shaped by the dragon's past, fear, hunger, and hoard. Reality becomes unstable. |
| 100 | Root-Crowned Dragon Heart | S-SS | Ysgravelle sleeps in the final chamber. Defeating her kills the dungeon. |

#### First 10 Floors

| Floor | Name | Layout Logic | Main Threats | Resources and Loot |
| ---: | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Mossgate Descent | Simple branching cave with root doors and slime pools. | Basic slimes, bugs. | Herbs, slime gel, low coins, basic wood. |
| 2 | Tallgrass Underpass | Grass-filled tunnels under fake sunlight. | Slimes, weak goblin scouts. | Slime cores, grass fiber, seeds. |
| 3 | Rootturn Maze | Circular root maze with two false exits. | Wood slimes, small bugs. | Basic wood, moss, small chests. |
| 4 | Hollow Burrows | Tight animal tunnels and nest rooms. | Oversized rats, bugs, corrupted rabbits. | Small hides, small bones, wild vegetables. |
| 5 | Stone Teeth Hall | Rocky corridor with spike-like stones and falling pebble traps. | Stone bugs, goblins. | Stone, clay, low-grade iron traces. |
| 6 | Slimewater Run | Stream floor with slick paths and hidden pools. | Water slimes, basic slimes. | Small fish, water herbs, slime gel. |
| 7 | Bandit Camp Remnant | Abandoned camp swallowed by roots. | Bandits, goblin scavengers. | Basic gear, coins, arrows, map scraps. |
| 8 | Bramble Switchbacks | Thorny paths, root bridges, and snare traps. | Poison slimes, thorn bugs. | Poison sacs, herbs, flax bundles. |
| 9 | The Gnawed Gate | Large locked root-gate requiring gathered drops or a hidden route. | Mixed slime pack, goblin brute. | Better chest chance, first boss key, iron traces. |
| 10 | Thornjaw Rest | Rest point followed by boss room. | Thornjaw, Root-Fed King Slime. | Boss chest, oaken upgrade materials, rare slime core. |

#### Floor 10 Boss: Thornjaw, Root-Fed King Slime

Thornjaw is a King Slime that has absorbed root magic, wood fragments, thorns, and old adventurer bones.

Threat rank: D

Boss mechanics:

- Splits into smaller slimes when heavily damaged.
- Creates thorn patches.
- Absorbs wood debris to regain armor.
- Slams the ground to shake loose ceiling roots.
- Becomes weaker when players cut root channels feeding it.

Rewards:

- Rare slime core
- Thorn gel
- Oaken bow upgrade material
- Slime gear catalyst
- Low chance at living slime equipment seed
- Adventurers Guild proof of Floor 10 clear

#### Dungeon Factions and Interest

- Adventurers Guild wants safe maps, monster population data, and proof of floor clears.
- Merchants Guild wants ores, herbs, slime materials, and future trade rights.
- Thieves Guild wants the best maps, hidden chests, and relic access.
- Mages Guild quietly monitors mana growth after floor 50.
- House Moonveil worries the dungeon may corrupt nearby forest routes.
- House Dawnmere may use dungeon danger as an excuse to relocate poor demon and beastfolk residents.
- Archers Guild can gain legitimacy by providing gatherers, scouts, trail markers, and resource reports around the entrance.

#### Verdant Maw Dragon Resolution Options

Players can resolve The Verdant Maw in three major ways:

- Kill Ysgravelle, causing The Verdant Maw to collapse and disappear forever.
- Befriend Ysgravelle, keeping The Verdant Maw protected and infinitely farmable under negotiated trust.
- Tame Ysgravelle, allowing the player or player group to control The Verdant Maw, its contents, and its economy.

Befriending Ysgravelle may require healing the forest around Mossgate Sink, returning stolen hoard roots, sparing dryad guardians, and proving the player will not strip the dungeon bare.

Taming Ysgravelle should require exceptional progression, dragon lore, mana compatibility, and possibly help from Dawnspire or Laucian Xilocent Goldpetal.

#### First Dungeon Design Purpose

The Verdant Maw should be introduced early but not conquered early.

Early players can:

- Gather near the entrance.
- Clear F-D threats on floors 1-3.
- Join groups for floor 10 attempts.
- Recover map fragments.
- Help NPCs who need dungeon resources.
- Learn how labyrinths work.

Later players return for:

- Mana crystals after floor 50.
- Relics in deeper floors.
- Dragon lore.
- Advanced slime evolution.
- Rare ores.
- Major guild quests.
- The final dragon raid.

### Dragon-Born Dungeon System Completion V1.0

This section completes the core design for dragon-born dungeons. The Verdant Maw is the first known example, but these rules apply to all true dragon-born dungeons in Huwan.

Design status:

- **World Bible Status:** Complete enough for systems design.
- **Content Status:** Ready for additional dungeon themes, dragon profiles, loot tables, floor templates, boss packs, and quest chains.
- **Implementation Status:** Needs dungeon director logic, floor-generation data, map/fog systems, loot tables, raid rules, dragon relationship states, economy hooks, and long-term server-state tracking.

Core promise:

- A dragon-born dungeon is not just a place to grind. It is a living magical ecosystem formed from an Ancient Dragon's mana, dreams, hoard, memories, instincts, fears, and domain.
- Every dragon-born dungeon can become a resource engine, political crisis, raid target, economic prize, settlement threat, legendary encounter source, or player-controlled world system.
- Killing, befriending, taming, sealing, sparing, or enraging the dragon must all create different futures.

#### Dungeon Lifecycle

| State | Meaning | Player Knowledge | World Impact |
|---|---|---|---|
| Dormant Seed | An Ancient Dragon has settled, but the dungeon is not yet obvious. | None or ancient rumor. | Local mana slowly warps land, monsters, dreams, and resources. |
| Hidden Growth | The dragon has slept long enough for early dungeon structures to form. | Strange caves, ruins, monster changes, missing travelers. | Local monsters mutate, ores enrich, plants change, maps become unreliable. |
| Rumored Dungeon | Locals know something dangerous exists. | Rumors, guild notes, old maps, missing NPCs. | Guilds send scouts; merchants speculate; thieves seek maps. |
| Confirmed Dungeon | The entrance and early floors are verified. | Map markers and guild board quests appear. | Resource economy begins; adventurers arrive; factions maneuver. |
| Active Delve | Players and NPCs regularly enter. | Floor data, boss proofs, loot tables, route maps. | Monster populations, resource flow, markets, and guild ranks react. |
| Deep Pressure | Floors 50+ are reached and mana crystals become relevant. | Mages, nobles, thieves, and artificers pay attention. | Mana crystal politics, black markets, and magical accidents increase. |
| Dragon Stirring | The dragon reacts to delvers, theft, damage, rituals, or respect. | Dreams, tremors, dragon memories, altered floors. | Floors become smarter; dragon-aligned events begin. |
| Final Confrontation | Floor 100 becomes reachable. | Raid preparation, faction claims, dragon diplomacy. | Server-notable danger, market pressure, military interest. |
| Resolved | Dragon is killed, befriended, tamed, sealed, spared, escaped, or enraged. | Public outcome depends on witnesses and guild reporting. | Dungeon future changes permanently or semi-permanently. |
| Legacy State | Consequences continue after resolution. | New routes, ruins, trade rights, scars, successors, or dead zones. | The world remembers what happened. |

#### Dungeon Identity Seeds

Every dragon-born dungeon should be built from identity seeds. These seeds decide floor themes, monsters, resources, traps, bosses, puzzles, music mood, map behavior, and dragon personality.

| Seed | Questions It Answers | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| Dragon Element | What mana flavor shapes the dungeon? | Forest, fire, ice, storm, shadow, metal, dream, death, ocean, crystal. |
| Dragon Domain | What does the dragon claim as "mine"? | Root basin, volcano, glacier, ruined city, buried sea, mountain crown, old battlefield. |
| Dragon Memory | What past does the dungeon keep repeating? | Betrayal, lost children, ancient war, famine, worship, hoard theft, divine wound. |
| Dragon Fear | What does the dungeon defend against? | Fire, silence, hunger, abandonment, gods, mortals, thieves, other dragons. |
| Dragon Desire | What does the dungeon produce or lure in? | Treasure, prey, songs, knowledge, worship, challengers, heirs, apology, silence. |
| Local Biome | What natural world existed before the dungeon? | Forest, cavern, river, lake, desert, quarry, ruin, city, mountain, swamp. |
| Hoard Theme | What treasure logic shapes rewards? | Gold, books, bones, seeds, weapons, memories, contracts, gems, machines, relics. |
| Era Scar | What destroyed-era remnant is buried inside? | 3rd Era temple, 6th Era city, divine battlefield, failed pocket world, old guild vault. |

#### Dungeon Director Floor Generation

The dungeon director should generate each floor using layered logic rather than pure randomness.

Generation order:

1. Read dungeon identity seeds.
2. Read current floor band.
3. Select floor theme, layout type, threat tier, resource tier, and puzzle density.
4. Add dragon memory influence.
5. Add local biome influence.
6. Add current world state, such as over-farming, faction invasion, dragon mood, or raid preparation.
7. Place main route, side routes, secrets, traps, resources, monsters, chests, shortcuts, and exits.
8. Place optional NPCs, lost parties, faction scouts, merchants, spirits, or dragon-memory scenes.
9. Apply map reliability and battle fog rules.
10. Save persistent discoveries, cleared bosses, depleted resources, altered rooms, and player-made changes.

Floor layout types:

| Layout Type | Gameplay Feel |
|---|---|
| Branching Cave | Simple exploration, early combat, basic gathering. |
| Looping Maze | Route memory, map value, ambush control. |
| Vertical Descent | Climbing, falling hazards, ropes, stamina, rescue danger. |
| Open Impossible Space | A sky, forest, lake, city, or battlefield that should not fit underground. |
| Puzzle Gate Floor | Progress requires keys, drops, rune logic, levers, offerings, or knowledge. |
| Resource Floor | High gathering value, stronger guardians, merchant interest. |
| Hunt Floor | A dangerous monster stalks the party across the floor. |
| Faction Floor | Bandits, guilds, cults, noble forces, or invaders establish a foothold. |
| Memory Floor | The dragon's past reshapes the environment and creates story clues. |
| Collapse Floor | Time pressure, unstable terrain, escape choices, rescue moments. |

#### Ten-Floor Arc Structure

Each ten-floor arc should feel like a chapter.

| Arc Part | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Floor 1 of Arc | Introduces the new theme, hazards, and monster behavior. |
| Floors 2-4 of Arc | Expands routes, resources, enemy variations, and side objectives. |
| Floor 5 of Arc | Adds a mid-arc complication, elite monster, puzzle, faction event, or resource temptation. |
| Floors 6-8 of Arc | Tests mastery of the arc's theme under higher pressure. |
| Floor 9 of Arc | Gate floor, key floor, maze, mini-boss, or final preparation challenge. |
| Floor 10 of Arc | Rest point plus boss room. Boss proves whether players understand the arc. |

Bosses every 10 floors should teach, punish, or transform the mechanics players saw during that arc.

#### Dungeon Threat And Reward Bands

| Floors | Threat Band | Reward Band | System Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1-10 | F-D | Basic materials, early gear, low coins, starter boss proof. | Teaches dungeon basics. |
| 11-20 | D-C | Better herbs, hides, ores, maps, uncommon drops. | Introduces stronger floor identity. |
| 21-30 | C | Puzzle rewards, ruins, class/job clues, faction interest. | First serious party expectation. |
| 31-40 | C-B | Rare resources, stronger chests, specialized materials. | Economic value becomes visible. |
| 41-50 | B | High-density ores, elite monster parts, deep maps. | Pre-mana crystal pressure. |
| 51-60 | B-A | Mana crystals, spell materials, magical boss drops. | Mages Guild and black market attention increases. |
| 61-70 | A | Relic fragments, advanced crafting materials, dangerous knowledge. | Death, memory, and curse risks rise. |
| 71-80 | A-S | Legendary material chances, elite boss proofs, dragon-touched gear paths. | Raid scouting begins. |
| 81-90 | S | Relic paths, rare titles, dragon memory, major faction events. | World-level parties and guild politics. |
| 91-99 | S-SS | Final keys, dragon history, hoard rights, raid unlocks. | Reality instability and dragon judgment. |
| 100 | S-SS | Dragon resolution rewards. | Kill, befriend, tame, seal, spare, escape, or enrage outcome. |

#### Resource Rules

Dungeons can produce resources, but the source and depth matter.

- Shallow floors provide starter materials, common monster parts, basic ores, herbs, slime resources, small chests, and early crafting drops.
- Mid floors provide better monster parts, rare plants, stronger ores, advanced hides, puzzle chests, map fragments, and class/job hints.
- Floor 50 and deeper can produce mana crystals.
- Deep floors can produce relic fragments, dragon-touched materials, high-density ore, rare alchemy parts, and legendary crafting paths.
- Relics are not normal respawning resources.
- Dragon hoard items should not respawn unless the dragon intentionally creates, trades, or gifts them.
- Over-farming can trigger stronger guardians, lower yields, dragon irritation, guild restrictions, or faction conflict.

Respawn logic:

| Resource Type | Normal Dungeon Respawn |
|---|---|
| Common herbs and fungi | Several in-game days, modified by dungeon health and floor theme. |
| Common ores | About 3 in-game days in active mining zones, slower when over-mined. |
| Plants and organic resources | About 5 in-game days unless the dragon's nature supports faster growth. |
| Trees, terrain, and damaged structures | About 1 in-game week unless major story damage blocks restoration. |
| Monster populations | Replenish through dungeon mana, ecology, breeding, portals, or floor memory. |
| Boss rooms | Reset on controlled timers, unless story-cleared, befriended, tamed, killed, or raid-locked. |
| Chests | Refill through dungeon logic, hoard seepage, faction drops, or magical renewal. |
| Mana crystals | Slow, deep-floor, high-pressure respawn only; never shallow or common. |
| Relics | Non-respawnable unless explicitly created by story, deity, dragon memory, or era event. |

#### Dungeon Economy

A dragon-born dungeon can become the economic heart of a region.

Economic outputs:

- Monster materials
- Ore
- Gems
- Mana crystals
- Herbs
- Dungeon wood, bone, slime, fungi, scales, hides, cores, and strange parts
- Chests and coins
- Relic fragments
- Maps
- Floor clear proofs
- Boss materials
- Legendary patterns
- Dragon-blessed substitutes when the dragon is befriended
- Dragon corpse materials when the dragon is killed

Economic pressure:

- Merchants want legal trade rights.
- Thieves want hidden routes and black-market materials.
- Adventurers want fair floor access.
- Knights want safety, law, and public order.
- Mages want mana crystals and magical monitoring.
- Nobles want taxes, ownership claims, and prestige.
- Poor districts want work and affordable supplies.
- Factions want control, sabotage, or ideological proof.

#### Access Rights And Dungeon Law

Dungeon access should be political.

Possible access models:

| Access Model | Description | Conflict |
|---|---|---|
| Open Delve | Anyone can enter at their own risk. | Deaths, over-farming, theft, and chaos. |
| Guild Licensed | Adventurers Guild or local authority issues access. | Rank disputes, corruption, permit costs. |
| Noble Claim | A noble house claims the dungeon entrance or nearby land. | Taxes, class tension, exploitation. |
| Merchant Contract | Merchants control logistics, supply, and trade stalls. | Monopoly risk and price manipulation. |
| Thieves Route | Hidden routes bypass official access. | Smuggling, assassination, stolen relics. |
| Mages Watch | Mages monitor mana, spells, and dangerous floors. | Secrecy, magical restrictions, hidden agendas. |
| Dragon Treaty | A befriended dragon grants negotiated entry. | Tribute, behavior rules, dragon anger. |
| Dungeon Lord Control | A tamer or controller governs access and economy. | Political crisis, server rivalry, raids, rebellion. |

#### Mapping And Battle Fog

Dragon-born dungeons should have their own map rules.

- Undiscovered floors remain under battle fog.
- Purchased maps can reveal rough routes, rest points, known boss rooms, resource zones, or danger warnings.
- Thieves Guild maps are often the most detailed, but may hide profitable secrets.
- Adventurers Guild maps are safer and more honest, but often less complete.
- Old maps can become unreliable when floors shift.
- Player-made maps can be sold, stolen, copied, forged, or improved.
- If a party shares map data, members present can gain the discovered floor data.
- Map sellers can clear fog, but they cannot reveal dragon judgment, hidden relic states, or living floor changes with perfect accuracy.

#### Rest Points

Rest points appear every 10 floors and may also appear rarely through special events.

Rest point services can include:

- Safe recovery without speeding server time
- Limited repairs
- Basic merchants
- Wound treatment
- Inventory sorting
- One-way exits
- Party regrouping
- Lost NPC rescue points
- Dragon memory scenes
- Strange shrine effects
- Temporary buff fountains

Rest point limits:

- Rest points do not skip time.
- Rest points do not remove all consequences.
- Rest points can become unsafe during crisis, raid, dragon anger, faction invasion, or dungeon collapse.
- Rest points can remember player behavior.

#### Dungeon NPCs And Living Events

Dungeons should not only contain monsters.

Possible NPCs inside dungeons:

- Lost adventurers
- Wounded gatherers
- Thieves Guild scouts
- Merchant surveyors
- Mages Guild observers
- Bandits
- Cultists
- Faction agents
- Strange merchants
- Trapped spirits
- Dragon memories
- Dungeon-born intelligent monsters
- Evolved slimes
- Cartographers
- Rescue targets
- Rival player parties

Living events:

- Floor invasion
- Monster migration
- Resource rush
- Guild rescue call
- Map auction
- Boss mutation
- Dragon dream event
- Relic awakening
- Faction sabotage
- Dungeon collapse pocket
- Rare legendary encounter
- Hidden merchant visit

#### Dragon Relationship System

Ancient Dragons should track more than hostility.

Dragon relationship states:

| State | Meaning | Typical Result |
|---|---|---|
| Unknown | The dragon has not noticed the player. | Normal dungeon behavior. |
| Noticed | The dragon senses repeated activity. | Floors begin adapting slightly. |
| Irritated | The player over-harvests, kills guardians, steals hoard, or disrespects the dungeon. | Stronger traps, worse reactions, hostile dreams. |
| Curious | The player shows unusual restraint, skill, mercy, or intelligence. | Dreams, clues, safer tests. |
| Respected | The player proves strength or wisdom. | Dragon memories, negotiated trials, rare access. |
| Trusted | The dragon believes the player can be bargained with. | Befriend path opens. |
| Bonded | The dragon forms a strong relationship with the player or party. | Deep alliance, special quests, protected farming. |
| Tamed | The dragon accepts command, pact, or dungeon-lord control. | Dungeon control and economy rights unlock. |
| Enraged | The player causes deep harm, betrayal, or violation. | Raid danger, revenge events, regional threat. |
| Sealed | The dragon is bound by magic, relic, divine law, or pact. | Dungeon remains unstable or restricted. |
| Dead | The dragon is killed. | Dungeon death begins. |

Dragon relationship modifiers:

- Sparing guardians
- Returning hoard items
- Healing damaged floors
- Avoiding over-harvest
- Respecting dragon memory scenes
- Completing dragon trials
- High world popularity
- Dragon lore knowledge
- Mana compatibility
- Tamer abilities
- Warlock or draconic contracts
- Relic use
- Betrayal, theft, greed, cruelty, or corpse harvesting

#### Dragon Resolution Outcomes

Dragon resolution is the most important choice in a dragon-born dungeon.

| Outcome | Dungeon Future | Reward Style | Risk |
|---|---|---|---|
| Kill | Dungeon collapses and dies. | Huge immediate corpse, hoard, collapse, and dragon material opportunities. | Permanent loss of dungeon economy; faction fight over remains. |
| Defeat And Spare | Dragon survives but is weakened or humbled. | Mercy rewards, scale/fang gifts, future diplomacy. | Dragon may recover angry, grateful, or ashamed. |
| Befriend | Dungeon remains protected and farmable. | Long-term access, dragon-blessed materials, safer farming, special quests. | Requires ongoing trust and tribute. |
| Tame | Dungeon remains under player or group control. | Dungeon Lord powers, economy rights, floor permissions, guardian control. | Massive political, ethical, and raid pressure. |
| Seal | Dragon and dungeon are contained. | Relic fragments, rune knowledge, divine or guild favor. | Future seal failure, limited harvest, moral questions. |
| Drive Away | Dragon escapes or relocates. | Partial loot, chase quests, abandoned dungeon scars. | A new dungeon seed may begin elsewhere. |
| Enrage | Dragon becomes an active threat. | Poor immediate rewards, high danger. | Regional attacks, revenge raids, settlement defense. |
| Heal | Dragon and dungeon are restored from corruption. | Blessings, alliance, rare knowledge, clean resource flow. | Corrupting faction becomes enemy. |

#### Dungeon Lord Rules

A player, party, guild, or faction that tames a dragon or otherwise gains dungeon authority can become a **Dungeon Lord** or dungeon-controlling power.

Dungeon Lord powers:

- Open or close floors.
- Set entry rules.
- Assign farming rights.
- Adjust monster pressure.
- Protect or restrict resources.
- Stabilize rest points.
- Authorize guild contracts.
- Sell map rights.
- Permit or forbid market stalls.
- Request dungeon defense.
- Shape some dungeon events.

Dungeon Lord limits:

- They cannot create infinite wealth without consequence.
- They cannot instantly spawn arbitrary relics.
- They cannot bypass server time.
- They cannot erase dragon will if the relationship is not true trust.
- They cannot avoid guild, noble, faction, and player reactions.
- They cannot control protected moderator or guest content.
- They cannot prevent all theft, rebellion, sabotage, or raid attempts.

Dungeon Lord risks:

- Tax disputes
- Guild rebellion
- Noble lawsuits
- Thieves Guild infiltration
- Mages Guild investigation
- Dragon resistance
- Monster ecology collapse
- Market manipulation
- Player raids
- Faction propaganda
- Server-wide reputation pressure

#### Dungeon Raids

Dragon-born dungeon raids can happen at boss floors, crisis floors, faction invasion floors, or floor 100.

Raid types:

| Raid Type | Trigger |
|---|---|
| Boss Floor Raid | A boss becomes too powerful for normal parties. |
| Resource Rush Raid | A deep resource pocket opens and factions race for it. |
| Faction Invasion Raid | A faction tries to seize floors, maps, relics, or the dragon heart. |
| Dragon Stirring Raid | The dragon's dreams become hostile and spill through multiple floors. |
| Collapse Raid | The dungeon is dying and players race to escape or save others. |
| Defense Raid | Players defend a befriended or tamed dungeon from attackers. |
| Final Dragon Raid | Floor 100 confrontation and resolution. |

Raid rewards should use contribution, loot dice, dragon outcome, party role, mercy, survival, and objective completion.

#### Dungeon Failure Table

Dungeon failure should create consequences without always ending the story.

| Failure Type | Consequence |
|---|---|
| Party Wipe | Rescue quest, lost supplies, durability damage, debt, or floor reset. |
| Failed Boss | Boss mutates, moves, gains guards, or locks a shortcut. |
| Failed Rescue | NPC dies, becomes undead, joins a faction, or leaves a memory clue. |
| Over-Harvest | Resource yield drops and guardians strengthen. |
| Bad Map | Party enters wrong room, ambush route, trap loop, or false rest point. |
| Faction Failure | Rival faction claims a floor, chest, route, or resource contract. |
| Dragon Anger | Floors adapt against repeated tactics. |
| Relic Mishandling | Curse, seal crack, spirit release, faction alarm, or legendary encounter. |
| Collapse Delay | Escape route worsens and loot choices become harder. |
| Betrayal | Dragon memory records the act and future floors react. |

#### Dungeon Critical Success Table

Great dungeon play should create memorable rewards.

| Success Type | Reward |
|---|---|
| Perfect Map | Clears extra battle fog and creates a sellable map. |
| Clean Boss Clear | Bonus chest, title progress, guild proof, or better loot dice. |
| Mercy Clear | Dragon respect, guardian pass, future diplomacy. |
| No-Death Clear | Party reputation, better raid contribution score, rare title progress. |
| Puzzle Mastery | Shortcut, secret room, relic clue, or old-era lore. |
| Resource Mastery | Higher yield without damaging dungeon health. |
| Rescue Success | NPC ally, witness, discount, or future quest chain. |
| Faction Outsmarted | Rival loses influence; guild trust rises. |
| Dragon Memory Honored | Befriend path improves. |
| Collapse Escape | Server rumor, rare survival title, or emergency reward table. |

#### Dungeon Integration With Legendary Encounters

Dragon-born dungeons are prime legendary encounter locations.

Legendary encounter hooks:

- A legendary blacksmith needs deep ore.
- A legendary rogue sells better floor routes.
- A legendary barbarian joins a defense raid.
- A legendary slime negotiates with evolved dungeon slimes.
- A legendary tamer studies dragon-touched beasts.
- A legendary archer maps impossible sightlines.
- A legendary bard finds a dragon memory song.
- A legendary mage studies floor frameworks.
- A legendary merchant controls dungeon supply contracts.
- A legendary artificer investigates dungeon-born clockwork hazards.

Soft clues can appear on floors 1-10. Direct contact should usually wait until deeper floors, rare events, or proper context. Fate contact should be tied to dragon memory, relic awakening, floor 100, or world-changing choices.

#### Dungeon Integration With Survival

Dungeons must respect survival systems.

- Hunger and thirst matter.
- Food can spoil if not properly stored.
- Rest points do not skip server time.
- Gear durability matters.
- Carry space and pouch choices matter.
- Injuries can force retreat.
- Social levels can be affected by party conflict, rescue, betrayal, and fear.
- Light, air, poison, spores, curses, and mana pressure can become survival problems.
- F-rank players should learn dungeon caution before deep delving becomes possible.

#### Dungeon Integration With Crafting And Jobs

Dungeons should feed job identity.

| Job Or Class Path | Dungeon Role |
|---|---|
| Adventurer | Clears monsters, proves floor progress, responds to guild quests. |
| Dungeon Explorer | Maps routes, finds traps, identifies hidden rooms, survives deep floors. |
| Blacksmith | Uses ores, repairs gear, crafts boss-material weapons. |
| Merchant | Runs supply chains, stalls, auctions, and dungeon contracts. |
| Archer | Scouts, marks routes, hunts from distance, protects gatherers. |
| Thief | Opens locks, finds hidden chests, buys or sells secret maps. |
| Mage/Wizard/Sorcerer | Handles magical hazards, runes, mana pressure, and spell puzzles. |
| Tamer | Captures or bonds with dungeon beasts. |
| Farmer/Gatherer | Harvests dungeon plants, fungi, fibers, and tameable growths. |
| Contractor | Builds camps, bridges, gates, repairs, and dungeon infrastructure. |
| Leader Jobs | Manage dungeon access, taxes, defense, guild policy, and party logistics. |

#### Dungeon Data Handoff

Recommended dungeon data fields:

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| DungeonId | Unique dungeon identifier. |
| Name | Player-facing dungeon name. |
| DragonId | Sleeping or active dragon tied to the dungeon. |
| DragonState | Sleeping, stirring, awake, befriended, tamed, dead, sealed, enraged. |
| Region | World location and political jurisdiction. |
| EntranceState | Hidden, rumored, open, guarded, sealed, collapsed, controlled. |
| FloorCount | Usually 100 for true dragon-born dungeons. |
| FloorBands | Threat, resources, themes, and boss logic per band. |
| IdentitySeeds | Element, memory, fear, desire, hoard, biome, era scar. |
| AccessModel | Open, guild licensed, noble claim, dragon treaty, Dungeon Lord, etc. |
| MapState | Fog, discovered floors, sold maps, false maps, player maps. |
| EconomyState | Resource flow, market pressure, contracts, black market, taxes. |
| FactionState | Guilds, nobles, thieves, mages, churches, cults, factions involved. |
| DragonRelationship | Per player, party, guild, or faction. |
| BossStates | Alive, cleared, mutated, replaced, raid-locked, story-cleared. |
| ResourceStates | Nodes, respawns, over-harvest, depletion, restoration. |
| EventClocks | Raid clock, collapse clock, invasion clock, dragon-stirring clock. |
| ResolutionState | Unresolved, killed, befriended, tamed, sealed, escaped, enraged, healed. |
| LegacyEffects | Long-term world changes after resolution. |

#### Dungeon Implementation Checklist

Dragon-born dungeons are implementation-ready when the game supports:

- Dungeon identity seeds.
- Floor band templates.
- Floor generation from weighted modules.
- Persistent floor discoveries.
- Battle fog and map sale integration.
- Boss states every 10 floors.
- Rest point services and restrictions.
- Resource respawn and over-harvest rules.
- Mana crystal restrictions after floor 50.
- Relic non-respawn rules.
- Guild board requests tied to floor state.
- NPC rescue and lost-party logic.
- Faction footholds and invasions.
- Dragon relationship tracking.
- Dragon resolution outcomes.
- Dungeon collapse and escape timers.
- Befriend and tame economies.
- Dungeon Lord permissions.
- Raid contribution and loot dice integration.
- Legendary encounter integration.
- Market and regional consequence hooks.
- Server rumor and announcement rules.

#### Final Dragon-Born Dungeon Rules

- A dragon-born dungeon is alive because a dragon is alive.
- The dungeon is the dragon's dream, wound, memory, hoard, and territory made explorable.
- Floor 100 is never just a boss room. It is a judgment.
- Killing a dragon should feel profitable and tragic.
- Befriending a dragon should feel difficult and deeply valuable.
- Taming a dragon should feel like gaining a kingdom with teeth.
- Over-farming should have consequences.
- Maps should help, but never fully conquer the unknown.
- Deep dungeon resources should reshape markets.
- Relics should change stories, not simply fill bags.
- Every major dungeon outcome should leave a mark on Huwan.

## Major Factions

### The Light Among Us

The Light Among Us believes everyone can inherit the light. Their doctrine claims evil can be transformed into good, but their methods are extreme.

They may use forced conversion, magical purification, imprisonment, ritual cleansing, mind alteration, public penance, or divine experimentation. They are dangerous because their goal sounds merciful while their methods can become cruel.

Core tension: Are they saviors, zealots, or both?

### The Doomsday Faction

The Doomsday Faction believes the races are an infestation upon Huwan and should perish. They may see destruction as purification, mercy, divine correction, or ecological necessity.

They are likely drawn to ancient artifacts, forbidden magic, sealed gods of ruin, apocalyptic prophecy, and monsters that can reset civilization.

Core tension: Are they trying to end evil, or are they the purest expression of it?

### The Peace Between Us

The Peace Between Us believes all races can coexist by finding common ground. They are the most docile of the major factions and often choose to live in semi-large mixed cities where all races are treated as equals.

They are not weak, but they prefer diplomacy, mutual defense, shared work, trade, education, healing, and cultural exchange.

Core tension: Can peace survive in a world built on balance, ancient hatred, and active extremism?

### Major Factions System Completion V1.0

Factions are the long-term social, political, economic, military, and ideological structures of Huwan. They are larger than parties and more flexible than formal guilds. A faction can be a religious movement, noble alliance, mercenary company, trade consortium, rebel cell, monster-rights movement, dungeon-control coalition, player kingdom, cult, charity network, criminal family, settlement defense league, or world-ending conspiracy.

Core rule:

- **Parties are for immediate play. Factions are for long-term power.**
- A normal party is usually limited to small tactical play.
- A faction allows many players to organize beyond the normal party limit without turning every activity into one massive party.
- Factions can create squads, warbands, raid groups, trade teams, scouting teams, crafting circles, settlement crews, and political delegations.

#### Group Structure Above Parties

| Group Type | Suggested Size | Purpose | Notes |
|---|---:|---|---|
| Solo | 1 | Personal play, stealth, crafting, exploration, story choices. | Can still represent a faction. |
| Party | 2-8 | Standard tactical adventuring group. | Best for quests, small dungeons, escorts, and normal combat. |
| Squad | 4-12 | Slightly larger task team. | Useful for faction patrols, gathering, construction, and scouting. |
| Warband | 9-24 | Large field group. | Used for territory defense, faction missions, dangerous roads, and boss preparation. |
| Raid | 25-80 | Major coordinated encounter. | Used for world bosses, dragon-born dungeon events, settlement sieges, and faction wars. |
| Campaign | 80+ | Multi-party faction operation. | Not one combat party; organized as squads, raids, logistics, scouts, crafters, and command. |
| Faction | No fixed combat size | Persistent organization. | Holds members, ranks, treasury, permissions, reputation, bases, contracts, and history. |

Large groups should not remove the value of smaller parties. Instead, large groups should split into roles:

- Combat parties
- Scout parties
- Healer teams
- Crafting teams
- Supply teams
- Map teams
- Negotiation teams
- Siege teams
- Reserve teams
- Rescue teams

#### Player-Created Factions

Players can create factions when they meet basic requirements. Requirements may vary by server rules, region, faction type, and political context.

Suggested creation requirements:

- Faction name
- Banner or symbol
- Founding purpose
- Founding alignment or doctrine
- Minimum founding members
- Charter fee or legal bribe
- Headquarters, camp, rented hall, or registered address
- Leader or council structure
- Initial rank structure
- Public rules or hidden rules
- Guild, noble, church, criminal, or settlement recognition path

Player factions can begin small and grow over time.

| Faction Stage | Description | Unlocks |
|---|---|---|
| Crew | Small group with a shared name. | Shared chat, basic roster, small storage, group requests. |
| Company | Recognized working group. | Contracts, ranks, treasury, squad tools, guild-board posting. |
| House | Social or political faction. | Public reputation, diplomacy, property rights, noble attention. |
| Order | Ideological, religious, military, magical, or knightly group. | Doctrine, oath rules, training identity, faction quests. |
| Guild-Scale Faction | Large player organization. | Bases, markets, raid organization, faction economy, NPC recruitment. |
| Regional Power | Controls meaningful territory, trade, dungeon access, or settlement policy. | Taxes, warband permissions, diplomacy, regional events. |
| World Power | Server-notable force. | Server rumors, major alliances, faction wars, legendary attention. |

#### Faction Types

| Faction Type | Focus | Common Gameplay |
|---|---|---|
| Adventuring Company | Quests, monster hunting, dungeon delving. | Parties, raids, bounty boards, floor clear proofs. |
| Trade Consortium | Commerce, banking, transport, auctions. | Markets, contracts, caravans, price control. |
| Crafting House | Gear, tools, buildings, materials. | Workshops, recipes, resource claims, crafting orders. |
| Noble House | Status, politics, land, marriage, inheritance. | Diplomacy, laws, taxes, social influence. |
| Mercenary Band | Combat contracts and defense. | Escorts, wars, raids, bounties, settlement defense. |
| Religious Order | Faith, doctrine, healing, exorcism, conversion. | Blessings, curses, rituals, moral conflict. |
| Criminal Syndicate | Theft, smuggling, assassination, black market. | Dark contracts, secret routes, stolen goods. |
| Monster-Rights Movement | Coexistence with slimes, undead, demons, beastfolk, or intelligent monsters. | Legal battles, rescue quests, diplomacy. |
| Dungeon Coalition | Dungeon mapping, farming, rights, defense. | Floor access, loot rules, dragon diplomacy. |
| Rebellion | Resistance against nobles, guilds, kingdoms, or factions. | Sabotage, propaganda, rescue, uprising. |
| Settlement League | Town defense, rebuilding, agriculture, public works. | Construction, law, patrols, taxes, food security. |
| Magic Circle | Spell research, Dawnspire seeking, mana regulation. | Runes, experiments, forbidden magic, mage politics. |
| Cult | Secret worship, artifacts, forbidden goals. | Rituals, infiltration, relic hunts, moral danger. |

#### Faction Governance

Faction governance determines who can make decisions.

| Governance Type | How It Works | Risk |
|---|---|---|
| Single Leader | One player or NPC controls major decisions. | Tyranny, betrayal, burnout, succession crisis. |
| Council | Several officers vote or share authority. | Slow decisions, politics, faction splits. |
| Rank Chain | Leaders command officers, officers command squads. | Abuse of rank, military rigidity. |
| Democratic Vote | Members vote on policies, wars, taxes, and leadership. | Popularity contests, slow crisis response. |
| Contract House | Members obey written contracts and profit shares. | Legal tricks, loopholes, Shaohr-style deal tension. |
| Faith Oath | Doctrine and vows guide decisions. | Heresy, schism, fanaticism. |
| Criminal Code | Trust, fear, reputation, and underworld rules hold it together. | Betrayal, infiltration, law pressure. |
| Dragon Pact | A dragon treaty or tamed dungeon shapes authority. | Dragon anger, Dungeon Lord politics. |

#### Faction Roles And Permissions

Faction roles should be customizable.

Suggested roles:

| Role | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Founder | Created the faction and may hold special legacy rights. |
| Leader | Handles strategy, diplomacy, war, and major permissions. |
| Councilor | Votes or advises on faction direction. |
| Officer | Manages squads, recruitment, contracts, and discipline. |
| Quartermaster | Controls storage, gear loans, material requests, and supplies. |
| Treasurer | Controls gold, taxes, wages, debts, and faction payments. |
| Diplomat | Manages alliances, truces, trade deals, and noble contact. |
| Marshal | Organizes warbands, raids, patrols, and defense. |
| Spymaster | Handles scouts, intelligence, infiltration, and counter-intelligence. |
| Builder | Manages faction bases, camps, repairs, and construction. |
| Crafter | Handles faction crafting orders, recipes, and workshops. |
| Recruiter | Invites players or NPCs and screens applicants. |
| Member | Standard faction member. |
| Initiate | Trial member with limited permissions. |
| Contractor | Temporary helper paid by the faction. |
| Ally | External player, party, NPC, or faction with limited shared access. |

Permission categories:

- Invite members
- Remove members
- Promote or demote members
- Access storage
- Withdraw gold
- Post contracts
- Accept contracts
- Declare rivalry
- Declare war
- Sign alliances
- Build structures
- Set taxes or dues
- Assign raid leaders
- Manage markets
- Manage maps
- Manage dungeon rights
- Edit faction doctrine
- Send public announcements

#### Faction Reputation

Factions have reputations separate from individual players.

Reputation layers:

| Layer | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Local Reputation | How towns, districts, and nearby NPCs view the faction. |
| Guild Reputation | How official guilds trust or distrust the faction. |
| Kingdom Reputation | How rulers, nobles, and law systems treat the faction. |
| Underworld Reputation | How thieves, smugglers, assassins, and black markets treat the faction. |
| Religious Reputation | How churches, priests, paladins, and faiths view the faction. |
| Monster Reputation | How intelligent monsters, slimes, dragons, undead, and beastfolk communities view the faction. |
| World Reputation | Server-level fame, infamy, fear, or respect. |

Faction reputation changes through:

- Quests
- Trade
- War
- Protection
- Betrayal
- Taxes
- Charity
- Slavery
- Monster treatment
- Dungeon control
- Dragon outcomes
- Legendary encounters
- Public contracts
- Assassinations
- Failed promises
- Server announcements

#### Faction Alignment And Doctrine

Faction alignment is not only good or evil. It should include behavior, doctrine, and public identity.

Doctrine examples:

- Coexistence
- Purification
- Profit
- Freedom
- Order
- Rebellion
- Conquest
- Faith
- Knowledge
- Nature
- Monster rights
- Racial separation
- Anti-god ideology
- Dragon worship
- Dungeon control
- Craft supremacy
- Merchant law
- Survival at any cost

Faction alignment can drift over time. A faction founded as peaceful can become militant. A criminal faction can become a shadow government. A monster-rights faction can become extremist. A noble house can become a charity or a tyranny.

#### Faction Economy

Factions can hold and manage resources.

Faction economy systems:

- Treasury
- Shared storage
- Material stockpiles
- Gear vaults
- Food stores
- Building supplies
- Contract payouts
- Member wages
- Taxes or dues
- Shop stalls
- Auction listings
- Caravan routes
- Dungeon farming rights
- Crafting orders
- War supplies
- Bribe funds
- Debt ledgers

Faction wealth should create power and risk. Rich factions attract merchants, thieves, nobles, taxation, spies, and desperate applicants.

#### Faction Bases And Territory

Factions can use physical locations.

Base types:

| Base Type | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Rented Room | Small meeting point and storage. |
| Guild Hall | Official base with contracts and public identity. |
| Safe House | Hidden base for criminals, rebels, spies, or refugees. |
| Workshop | Crafting-focused faction base. |
| Market Stall Row | Trade faction presence. |
| Farmstead | Food production and settlement support. |
| Fortified Camp | Wilderness base, dungeon camp, or warband staging point. |
| Noble Estate | Political power and social leverage. |
| Temple House | Religious or oath-based faction base. |
| Dungeon Outpost | Dungeon access, rescue, maps, and resource processing. |
| Castle Or Keep | Regional military and political control. |
| Player Settlement | Long-term player-run town or district. |

Territory rules:

- Territory can be bought, granted, conquered, rented, inherited, claimed, or illegally occupied.
- Territory creates taxes, defense needs, resource rights, NPC relationships, and political pressure.
- Territory can be attacked, infiltrated, sabotaged, taxed, blessed, cursed, repaired, upgraded, or abandoned.
- Territory claims should interact with nobles, guilds, kingdoms, monsters, and local citizens.

#### Faction Quests And Contracts

Factions can generate quests.

Faction quest sources:

- Member needs
- NPC requests
- Guild contracts
- Market shortages
- Resource stockpiles
- Dungeon rights
- Base construction
- Territory defense
- War goals
- Diplomacy
- Espionage
- Public reputation
- Legendary encounters
- Dragon-born dungeon events

Contract types:

| Contract | Example |
|---|---|
| Gathering | Collect herbs, ore, hides, slime gel, food, or building supplies. |
| Crafting | Produce weapons, armor, tools, pouches, maps, potions, or siege gear. |
| Escort | Protect caravans, diplomats, refugees, prisoners, merchants, or priests. |
| Defense | Guard towns, gates, farms, roads, dungeon camps, or faction bases. |
| Investigation | Find proof, expose corruption, verify rumors, identify spies. |
| Assassination | Remove a target under dark-contract rules. |
| Rescue | Save members, NPCs, captured monsters, slaves, or lost delvers. |
| Diplomacy | Negotiate peace, trade, marriage, alliances, truces, or reparations. |
| Raid | Attack boss, dungeon floor, faction base, world monster, or enemy supply. |
| Sabotage | Break enemy logistics, traps, supplies, propaganda, or fortifications. |
| Construction | Build roads, walls, farms, stalls, bridges, halls, and outposts. |
| Settlement Work | Housing, agriculture, sanitation, policing, healing, and public order. |

#### Faction Diplomacy

Faction relationships should create living politics.

Relationship states:

| State | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Unknown | No established relationship. |
| Neutral | No active cooperation or hostility. |
| Friendly | Small cooperation and positive reputation. |
| Allied | Shared goals, contracts, defense, or resources. |
| Trade Partner | Economic relationship without full alliance. |
| Protected | One faction protects another. |
| Vassal | One faction submits to another for security or law. |
| Rival | Competition without open war. |
| Hostile | Open distrust and possible attacks. |
| At War | Direct conflict, raids, sieges, and territory battles. |
| Truce | Conflict paused by agreement. |
| Secret Ally | Publicly separate, privately cooperating. |
| Infiltrated | One faction has spies, agents, or compromised officers inside another. |

Diplomacy can be formalized through Shaohr-style trade symbols, contracts, double-headed bribery coins, oaths, divine witnesses, guild charters, noble seals, or illegal underworld agreements.

#### Faction Conflict And War

Faction war should be dangerous but structured.

War goals:

- Territory control
- Dungeon access
- Trade route control
- Resource claims
- Revenge
- Ideology
- Religious conflict
- Monster rights
- Noble succession
- Settlement defense
- Dragon-born dungeon ownership
- Relic control

War stages:

| Stage | Description |
|---|---|
| Tension | Rumors, insults, market pressure, propaganda. |
| Rivalry | Contract competition, resource denial, spy activity. |
| Skirmish | Small fights, ambushes, sabotage, raids. |
| Open War | Warbands, sieges, declared enemies, public danger. |
| Settlement Threat | Towns, roads, farms, and NPCs become endangered. |
| Resolution | Treaty, surrender, destruction, merger, exile, vassalage, or uneasy peace. |

War should create quests for combatants and noncombatants. Builders repair damage, merchants supply food, priests heal, thieves gather intelligence, crafters build equipment, farmers feed armies, and diplomats try to stop everything from becoming a disaster.

#### Factions And Player Parties

Player factions implement a larger party ecosystem without breaking the 8-person tactical party structure.

Rules:

- A faction can contain many players.
- A faction can create multiple parties.
- Parties remain useful for moment-to-moment combat, dungeon floors, quests, and travel.
- Faction leaders can assemble squads, warbands, raids, or campaigns from multiple parties.
- Faction missions can show objectives to all assigned members.
- Rewards can be split by party, contribution, faction policy, or leader discretion.
- Faction storage and treasury can support members before large operations.
- A player can belong to one main faction and possibly hold limited ally, contractor, or guest status with others, depending on server rules.

Faction group tools:

- Roster
- Ranks
- Roles
- Chat channels
- Party finder
- Raid organizer
- Contract board
- Shared map pins
- Shared rumor board
- Supply requests
- Treasury
- Storage
- Diplomacy page
- War status
- Settlement projects
- Dungeon rights page
- Contribution ledger

#### Factions And NPCs

NPCs can join, leave, serve, betray, or create factions.

NPC faction behavior:

- NPCs can request recruitment.
- NPCs can apply based on alignment, job, wealth, safety, ideology, or relationships.
- NPCs can leave if mistreated, unpaid, endangered, enslaved, starved, lied to, or ideologically betrayed.
- NPCs can generate faction quests from their daily needs.
- NPCs can become officers if trusted.
- NPCs can leak information if unhappy.
- NPCs can remember faction history.
- NPCs can promote a small player faction by spreading local reputation.

Faction NPC types:

- Soldiers
- Merchants
- Crafters
- Farmers
- Priests
- Spies
- Guards
- Scouts
- Builders
- Cartographers
- Slime handlers
- Dungeon explorers
- Refugees
- Noble patrons
- Criminal contacts
- Monster allies

#### Factions And Legendary Systems

Factions can interact with Legendary NPCs and legendary encounters.

Examples:

- A faction that protects slimes may attract Mallow Crown-Gel rumors.
- A crafting faction may earn Hilda Hammerwake's attention.
- A trade faction may be audited by Yumi Longwhisker.
- A rogue faction may cross paths with Sable Vey Rook.
- A faction controlling dungeon routes may trigger dragon-born dungeon politics.
- A warband that shows restraint may earn Ormak Wolfbanner's respect.
- A faction spreading truth may be helped or mocked by Fenroth Moonharp.

Legendary encounters should not automatically favor large factions. Sometimes a lone player, small party, or ordinary NPC should have the strange luck that changes everything.

#### Faction Creation Abuse Limits

Player-created factions need limits to prevent chaos.

Limits:

- Names and banners must obey server rules.
- Duplicate, impersonation, and scam names can be rejected or flagged.
- Faction creation can require gold, reputation, members, or a charter.
- War declarations can require cooldowns, costs, proof, territory claims, or mutual systems.
- Faction banks need permission logs.
- Leadership transfers need safeguards.
- Inactive factions can decay, pause, or lose territory.
- Exploit factions can be dissolved by moderation or world law.
- Faction spam can be blocked by creation cooldowns.
- Player factions cannot override moderator roles, guest characters, protected lore, or world-bible rules.

#### Faction Data Handoff

Recommended faction data fields:

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| FactionId | Unique faction identifier. |
| Name | Player-facing faction name. |
| Banner | Symbol, colors, icon, or crest. |
| Type | Adventuring company, trade consortium, noble house, criminal syndicate, etc. |
| Founder | Player, NPC, guild, noble, deity, dragon, or hidden actor. |
| Governance | Leader, council, vote, contract, oath, criminal code, dragon pact. |
| Doctrine | Ideology, alignment, public rules, hidden rules. |
| Members | Player and NPC roster. |
| Roles | Rank and permission structure. |
| Treasury | Gold, debts, wages, taxes, and bribes. |
| Storage | Shared resources, gear, maps, relics, tools, supplies. |
| Bases | Halls, camps, safe houses, estates, outposts, settlements. |
| Territory | Land, roads, dungeon rights, markets, farms, buildings. |
| Reputation | Local, guild, kingdom, underworld, religious, monster, world. |
| Diplomacy | Allies, rivals, wars, truces, trade partners, infiltrations. |
| Contracts | Active quests, jobs, bounties, construction, raids, trade orders. |
| Economy | Trade routes, shops, auctions, resource claims, market effects. |
| Military | Parties, squads, warbands, raids, guards, defenses. |
| History | Major actions, betrayals, victories, scandals, server announcements. |
| Cooldowns | War, recruitment, claims, raids, diplomacy, legendary interactions. |
| ModerationFlags | Protected names, abuse checks, ownership disputes, rule issues. |

#### Faction Implementation Checklist

Factions are implementation-ready when the game supports:

- Player faction creation.
- Faction names, banners, types, and doctrine.
- Roster and ranks.
- Role permissions.
- Treasury and transaction logs.
- Shared storage and access logs.
- Faction chat and announcements.
- Party, squad, warband, raid, and campaign organization.
- Faction contracts and quest boards.
- NPC recruitment and morale.
- Faction reputation layers.
- Diplomacy states.
- War declarations and cooldowns.
- Territory and base ownership.
- Market and trade route effects.
- Dungeon access rights.
- Legendary encounter hooks.
- Server rumor and announcement hooks.
- Abuse protections and moderation tools.

#### Final Faction Rules

- Factions let players build something larger than an 8-person party.
- Parties win fights; factions shape history.
- Factions should create work for every playstyle, not only fighters.
- A faction can become a home, business, army, religion, crime family, settlement, rebellion, or future kingdom.
- NPCs should react to faction reputation as much as player reputation.
- Player-created factions can rise, split, merge, betray, collapse, or become legendary.
- The strongest faction stories should come from choices, not menus.
- A faction that controls a dragon-born dungeon is not just rich. It is politically dangerous.
- Huwan should eventually be populated, run, managed, and balanced by players through factions, guilds, markets, settlements, and living consequences.

## Settlements And Player-Built Cities

Settlements are the physical backbone of player power. Factions give players organization, but settlements give them land, homes, roads, farms, laws, markets, walls, taxes, defense, public services, NPC populations, and something worth protecting.

Settlements can be founded, purchased, inherited, conquered, rebuilt, rented, granted by rulers, claimed through faction law, or developed from camps into cities.

### Settlements And Player-Built Cities System Completion V1.0

Core rule:

- **Players can shape the world, but the world pushes back.**
- A player with enough money can buy land, property, districts, cities, or even kingdom authority under the right conditions.
- Buying something does not erase the people who live there, the law that governs it, the debts attached to it, the factions watching it, or the consequences of changing it.

Design status:

- **World Bible Status:** Complete enough for systems design.
- **Content Status:** Ready for settlement templates, building data, property deeds, NPC migration rules, taxes, construction jobs, and ownership quests.
- **Implementation Status:** Needs build mode, buy mode, deed systems, zoning, construction placement, NPC housing, services, taxation, city-state tracking, and political consequence logic.

#### Settlement Scale

| Scale | Typical Population | Description | Player Use |
|---|---:|---|---|
| Camp | 1-25 | Temporary tents, carts, fires, and storage. | Travel staging, expedition camp, faction meetup. |
| Outpost | 25-150 | Guarded work site, farm post, mine camp, dungeon camp, or road station. | Resource control, patrol point, early faction base. |
| Hamlet | 150-500 | Small rural settlement. | Farming, local quests, basic shops, housing. |
| Village | 500-1,500 | Stable settlement with services and identity. | Player housing, markets, small guild offices, defense. |
| Town | 1,500-8,000 | Larger civic center with districts and politics. | Trade, guilds, courts, walls, public projects. |
| City | 8,000-100,000+ | Major regional population center. | Taxes, noble politics, faction influence, large economy. |
| Capital | 100,000+ | Kingdom seat or major cultural power. | High law, diplomacy, kingdom events, royal politics. |
| Kingdom | Many settlements | A political domain over multiple regions. | Lawmaking, armies, taxes, diplomacy, war, world pressure. |

Eldoria begins as a mid-sized starter settlement with roughly 1,500 NPCs, but the same settlement systems should eventually support camps, villages, cities, capitals, and player-run kingdoms.

#### Land Buy Mode

**Land Buy Mode** is the player-facing system for purchasing legal control over property, land, buildings, districts, settlements, or larger political holdings.

Buy Mode should feel powerful, but not like a cheat menu. Wealthy players can acquire enormous influence, but ownership comes with upkeep, law, citizens, rivals, contracts, debt, public opinion, and possible rebellion.

Buy Mode can be accessed through:

- Merchants Guild real estate offices
- Noble courts
- Banks
- Auction houses
- Kingdom land registries
- Black market deed brokers
- Conquest treaties
- Inheritance events
- Faction diplomacy
- Dungeon Lord agreements
- Special ruler or mayor interfaces

Buy Mode ownership tiers:

| Tier | Purchase Target | What Player Can Change | Main Risk |
|---|---|---|---|
| Property | House, shop, stall, warehouse, farm plot, room, workshop. | Interior layout, storage, decor, workstations, rental use. | Theft, taxes, upkeep, zoning rules. |
| Land Parcel | Field, forest plot, quarry claim, riverside plot, road lot. | Farming, gathering rights, building placement, fences, guards. | Monster threats, neighbors, land law, resource depletion. |
| Estate | Noble manor, large farm, vineyard, private compound. | Staff, guards, gardens, housing, walls, production. | Noble envy, staff morale, scandal, inheritance claims. |
| District Lot | Cluster of buildings or a neighborhood block. | Shops, housing, services, roads, lighting, security. | Citizen unrest, crime, housing pressure, guild attention. |
| Village | Entire small settlement. | Laws, taxes, public services, building projects, guards. | NPC happiness, food supply, local rebellion. |
| Town | Medium settlement or trade hub. | District planning, guild contracts, markets, walls, policy. | Faction politics, debt, corruption, public order. |
| City | Large urban center. | District redesign, tax policy, defenses, public works, zoning. | Noble conflict, riots, migration, guild pressure. |
| Capital Rights | Partial or full control of a capital. | Royal projects, high law, major diplomacy, army funding. | Succession war, assassination, kingdom revolt. |
| Kingdom Authority | Kingdom purchase, royal grant, conquest settlement, debt takeover, or dynastic transfer. | Kingdom laws, taxation, diplomacy, military, settlement policy. | Massive unrest, rival claimants, pantheon attention, faction wars. |

#### Buy Mode Rules

- A player must be able to inspect a property before buying it.
- The inspection should show price, taxes, upkeep, debts, zoning, population, risks, services, legal owner, disputed claims, and hidden issues if discovered.
- Players can overpay to win auctions, bribe officials, clear debts, or speed approvals, but bribery can create blackmail.
- Buying land does not automatically remove residents.
- Buying a city does not mean the citizens like the buyer.
- Buying a kingdom does not mean every noble, guild, temple, army, and village obeys instantly.
- Protected story locations, moderator spaces, pocket worlds, deity domains, and special guest content cannot be bought through normal systems.
- Player purchases must respect server rules and world-bible limits.

Buy Mode price factors:

| Factor | Effect |
|---|---|
| Population | More citizens increase value, taxes, unrest risk, and service needs. |
| Location | Roads, rivers, gates, markets, dungeons, ports, and capitals raise price. |
| Resources | Farms, mines, forests, quarries, mana flows, and fishing rights raise price. |
| Safety | Safer areas cost more; dangerous areas may be cheaper but harder to hold. |
| Buildings | Existing structures add value and upkeep. |
| Debt | Debt can lower purchase price but creates immediate obligations. |
| Legal Dispute | Disputed land is cheaper but can trigger court, noble, or faction conflict. |
| Reputation | Loved buyers may get discounts; hated buyers may face inflated prices or refusal. |
| Faction Backing | Strong factions can negotiate better terms or intimidate sellers. |
| War State | War can crash prices or make land impossible to buy legally. |
| Dungeon Access | Nearby dungeon rights can multiply value. |
| Dragon Threat | Dragon danger can lower price while raising long-term risk and prestige. |

#### Property Ownership States

| State | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Owned | Player or faction legally owns the property. |
| Rented | Player pays recurring fee for use. |
| Leased | Player controls property for a long term but not forever. |
| Mortgaged | Player owns it under debt obligation. |
| Disputed | Ownership is legally or politically contested. |
| Occupied | Someone controls it without legal ownership. |
| Conquered | Taken by force and awaiting recognition or retaliation. |
| Gifted | Granted by noble, guild, dragon, deity, NPC, or faction. |
| Inherited | Received through family, marriage, adoption, or story chain. |
| Condemned | Unsafe, cursed, ruined, illegal, or seized by authority. |

#### Build Mode

**Build Mode** allows players, factions, contractors, builders, nobles, mayors, and rulers to place, upgrade, repair, redesign, or demolish structures they have rights to modify.

Build Mode should use:

- Land rights
- Zoning
- Materials
- Labor
- Gold
- Permits
- Skill requirements
- Job requirements
- Construction time
- Server time
- Public disruption
- NPC housing needs
- Safety checks
- Road and utility access
- Terrain and resource rules

Players can change:

- Building layout
- Walls
- Roads
- Gates
- Farms
- Fields
- Fences
- Workshops
- Shops
- Stalls
- Taverns
- Housing
- Warehouses
- Guard posts
- Armories
- Temples
- Guild halls
- Banks
- Schools
- Sewers
- Wells
- Lighting
- Bridges
- Watchtowers
- Dragon defense structures
- Decorative objects
- Public signs
- Market stalls

Players cannot freely change:

- Protected lore landmarks
- Active quest-critical structures without story handling
- Another owner's property
- Public roads without permit or authority
- Sacred sites without religious or legal consequence
- Dragon-born dungeon structures without dungeon authority
- Living NPC homes without relocation, compensation, eviction, or law response

#### Construction Requirements

| Project Type | Typical Requirements |
|---|---|
| Small House | Wood, stone, clay, labor, basic permit. |
| Workshop | Building materials, tools, workstation, safety approval. |
| Farm | Land, seeds, water, tools, workers, fencing. |
| Market Stall | Stall kit, merchant permit, tax registration. |
| Tavern | Building, kitchen, beds, alcohol license, staff. |
| Guild Hall | Land, charter, materials, faction or guild approval. |
| Wall | Stone, wood, labor crews, engineer or contractor. |
| Gate | Wall access, guards, hinges, metalwork, city approval. |
| Road | Stone, gravel, labor, route survey, maintenance funding. |
| Bank | Vault, guards, ledger system, Merchants Guild approval. |
| Temple | Land, priest approval, deity alignment, public rites. |
| Armory | Weapons, armor, security, officer approval. |
| Dragon Tower | High engineering, alarm systems, ballistae, magic warning, rare materials. |
| Castle | Massive stone, engineers, labor, taxes, legal authority, defense planning. |

Construction should create jobs for builders, contractors, crafters, merchants, farmers, guards, miners, woodcutters, cartographers, and transport workers.

#### City Services

Settlements need services or they begin to decline.

| Service | Why It Matters |
|---|---|
| Housing | Prevents homelessness, unrest, disease, crime, and migration loss. |
| Food | Prevents hunger, price spikes, riots, and disease. |
| Water | Required for citizens, farms, alchemy, animals, and fire control. |
| Sanitation | Prevents disease, smell, slime labor disputes, and reputation loss. |
| Roads | Improves travel, markets, patrols, emergency response. |
| Lighting | Reduces crime and night danger. |
| Guards | Reduces crime, bandits, monster incursions, riots. |
| Healers | Reduces death, plague, injury downtime, undead risk. |
| Temples | Provides faith, rites, resurrection, cleansing, social control. |
| Markets | Supports trade, jobs, supplies, auction access. |
| Schools | Improves skills, literacy, apprentices, long-term citizen quality. |
| Banks | Supports storage, loans, taxes, large purchases. |
| Fire Response | Protects dense housing and markets. |
| Animal Care | Supports mounts, livestock, transport, taming. |
| Public Order | Courts, laws, prisons, mediation, and reputation stability. |

Service quality should affect NPC happiness, migration, crime, disease, productivity, taxes, and faction reputation.

#### NPC Migration And Citizen Simulation

NPCs should move toward opportunity and away from danger.

NPCs may migrate because of:

- Jobs
- Housing
- Safety
- Food
- Wages
- Religion
- Family
- Race acceptance
- Faction ideology
- Dungeon work
- War
- Slavery
- Poverty
- Taxes
- Crime
- Monster pressure
- Player reputation
- Settlement quality

Citizen happiness factors:

| Factor | Positive | Negative |
|---|---|---|
| Housing | Affordable homes. | Homelessness, forced relocation, segregation. |
| Food | Stable prices and farms. | Hunger, spoilage, famine. |
| Safety | Guards, walls, patrols. | Crime, monsters, raids. |
| Work | Jobs, wages, apprenticeships. | Unemployment, exploitation, unpaid labor. |
| Law | Fair courts and clear rules. | Corruption, cruelty, arbitrary punishment. |
| Race Relations | Mixed-race safety and equal access. | Discrimination, purges, segregation. |
| Taxes | Fair public services. | Crushing taxes or noble greed. |
| Culture | Festivals, temples, schools, taverns. | Oppression, censorship, public despair. |

#### Laws, Taxes, And Policy

Settlement owners can set policies if they have authority.

Policy categories:

- Tax rate
- Rent rules
- Market fees
- Stall permits
- Guild rights
- Building permits
- Curfew
- Guard patrol style
- Crime punishment
- Slavery law
- Monster citizen law
- Undead rights
- Demon acceptance
- Beastfolk rights
- Brothel regulation
- Alcohol and drug policy
- Magic use policy
- Public worship policy
- Dungeon access policy
- Resource harvest policy
- Immigration policy

Policy consequences:

- Low taxes increase happiness but reduce services.
- High taxes increase treasury but raise unrest.
- Harsh law reduces visible crime but can create rebellion.
- Fair law improves stability but may anger extremists.
- Open race policies increase diversity but attract purist hostility.
- Anti-monster policy may reduce fear but anger monster-rights factions and peaceful monsters.
- Heavy dungeon taxes enrich rulers but anger adventurers, merchants, and delvers.

#### Buy City And Buy Kingdom Mode

Extremely wealthy players can attempt to buy entire cities or kingdoms through special high-level systems.

Valid paths:

- Debt takeover
- Royal sale
- Noble succession purchase
- Merchant consortium buyout
- Conquest settlement
- Treaty transfer
- Bankruptcy auction
- Marriage contract
- Adoption into ruling house
- Dragon dungeon economy leverage
- Shaohr-blessed finance deal
- Black market shadow purchase
- Pantheon or relic-backed claim

City purchase requirements may include:

- Massive gold
- Existing reputation
- Faction backing
- Legal title or charter
- Guild approval
- Noble negotiation
- Citizen support or military control
- Debt settlement
- Public order plan
- Food and service plan
- Anti-corruption audit

Kingdom purchase requirements may include:

- Absurd wealth
- World popularity
- Political title
- Noble or royal claim
- Army or faction support
- Guild recognition
- Pantheon attention or approval
- Marriage, adoption, conquest, or treaty legitimacy
- Ability to pay debts and armies
- Ability to prevent rebellion

Buying a city or kingdom can unlock:

- Lawmaking
- Tax policy
- Public works
- Army funding
- Royal guards
- Noble appointments
- Guild contracts
- City redesign
- Settlement expansion
- Trade treaties
- Dungeon access policy
- Kingdom-wide construction projects
- Diplomatic recognition
- Server-notable status

Buying a city or kingdom can trigger:

- Noble outrage
- Citizen protests
- Assassination attempts
- Guild negotiations
- Tax revolts
- Religious objections
- Neighboring kingdom pressure
- Faction war
- Corruption offers
- Shaohr bargain events
- Doomsday sabotage
- Light Among Us conversion pressure
- Peace Between Us diplomacy
- Legendary NPC interest

#### Settlement Modification Ethics
