Players can change cities, but the game should treat citizens as people, not furniture.

If a player demolishes housing:

- Residents need relocation.
- Homelessness rises.
- Crime, disease, and unrest can rise.
- Poor districts may radicalize.
- Charitable houses or factions may respond.

If a player evicts citizens:

- Reputation changes.
- Guards may be needed.
- Refugee quests may generate.
- Factions may recruit the displaced.
- The Church, nobles, or Peace Between Us may intervene.

If a player improves services:

- Migration can increase.
- Tax base can grow.
- Crime can fall.
- NPCs may become loyal.
- Markets can stabilize.
- Player/faction reputation rises.

#### Settlement Defense

Settlements need defense against more than armies.

Threats:

- Bandits
- Monsters
- Slimes
- Undead
- Wyverns
- Dragons
- Faction raids
- Riots
- Fires
- Plague
- Sabotage
- Assassins
- Cults
- Dungeon leaks
- Noble coups
- Market collapse

Defense structures:

- Walls
- Gates
- Guard posts
- Watchtowers
- Alarm bells
- Patrol routes
- Armories
- Barracks
- Archer towers
- Mage wards
- Anti-undead shrines
- Fire stations
- Dragon alarm towers
- Ballista platforms
- Evacuation shelters
- Emergency food stores
- Secure wells

Defense should create ongoing jobs for guards, officers, knights, archers, builders, blacksmiths, merchants, priests, mages, scouts, and contractors.

#### Agriculture And Resource Zones

Player settlements need food and materials.

Agriculture zones:

- Grain fields
- Vegetable fields

Resource zones:

Overuse can damage the land, reduce yields, anger druids, attract monsters, or create poor-district shortages.

#### Settlement Quality Rating

Settlements should have a quality rating that affects NPC life and player value.

| Rating | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Collapsing | Unsafe, hungry, lawless, abandoned, cursed, or ruined. |
| Struggling | Basic survival works, but crime, poverty, or shortages are common. |
| Stable | Services function and citizens mostly trust the settlement. |
| Prosperous | Jobs, markets, safety, and housing are strong. |
| Renowned | The settlement attracts travelers, guilds, crafters, and skilled NPCs. |
| Legendary | The settlement becomes a server-known destination or political power. |

Quality inputs:

- Housing
- Food
- Water
- Sanitation
- Safety
- Jobs
- Taxes
- Roads
- Healthcare
- Education
- Trade
- Race acceptance
- Crime control
- Defense
- Culture
- Faction stability
- Dungeon access
- Dragon threat
- Player leadership

#### Settlement Events

Settlements should generate events based on needs, risks, and player policy.

Event examples:

| Event | Trigger |
|---|---|
| Housing Crisis | Population grows faster than homes. |
| Food Shortage | Crops fail, trade route breaks, or taxes distort markets. |
| Tax Protest | Taxes rise without visible services. |
| Market Boom | Trade route, dungeon loot, or festival improves wealth. |
| Fire | Dense housing, poor safety, sabotage, or accident. |
| Plague | Poor sanitation, undead contamination, bad water, overcrowding. |
| Monster Incursion | Weak patrols, nearby dungeon, over-hunting, wild migration. |
| Refugee Wave | War, faction conflict, dragon attack, racial persecution. |
| Noble Scandal | Corruption, inheritance, marriage, bribery, secret faction ties. |
| Faction Rally | Player faction recruits or protests in public. |
| Dragon Alarm | Dragon sighting, dungeon disturbance, or aerial attack. |
| Public Festival | Prosperity, faith day, bardic event, faction celebration. |

#### Settlement Implementation Checklist

Settlements are implementation-ready when the game supports:

- Land parcels.
- Property deeds.
- Land Buy Mode.
- City and kingdom purchase paths.
- Build Mode placement and upgrades.
- Construction material requirements.
- Construction time using living server time.
- Builder and Contractor job integration.
- Housing and homelessness.
- NPC migration.
- Settlement services.
- Taxes and treasury.
- Policy setting.
- Zoning and permits.
- Public order and crime.
- Agriculture and resource zones.
- Repair and decay.
- Defense structures.
- Dragon defense systems.
- Faction ownership.
- Guild and noble reactions.
- Market impact.
- Quest generation from settlement needs.
- Map and minimap integration.
- Battle fog for city districts and owned land.
- Server rumor and announcement hooks.
- Anti-abuse protections for ownership, demolition, eviction, and protected lore.

#### Final Settlement Rules

- Land is power, but land has people on it.
- Buying a city buys responsibility, not obedience.
- Buying a kingdom buys a throne-shaped problem.
- Build Mode should let players express creativity while still respecting law, materials, labor, time, and consequences.
- Rich players can reshape Huwan, but reshaping Huwan creates witnesses, enemies, debts, and history.
- NPC citizens should react to how they are governed.
- Factions need settlements; settlements need services; services need workers; workers need safety and pay.
- Player cities should become engines for quests, economy, politics, war, romance, betrayal, and legacy.
- The long-term dream is that Huwan can eventually be populated, run, managed, bought, rebuilt, defended, and transformed by players.

## Player Governance And Law

Governance is how ownership becomes authority. A player can buy land, build a city, found a faction, inherit a noble title, conquer a region, or purchase kingdom authority, but ruling means handling laws, citizens, taxes, courts, crime, rivals, lobbyists, guilds, nobles, churches, merchants, criminal powers, and public unrest.

### Player Governance And Law System Completion V1.0

Core rule:

- **The higher the authority, the more power the player has, but the less free time they have.**
- Low-authority players have more freedom and fewer responsibilities.
- High-authority players can change laws, taxes, land use, guild rights, and public policy, but they inherit hearings, petitions, crises, scandals, defense, debt, diplomacy, and blame.
- Money can influence law, but it does not guarantee law.
- Criminal authority exists if the group has not been caught, exposed, abolished, overthrown, or destroyed.

#### Authority Ladder

| Authority Level | Role | Power | Responsibility Load |
|---|---|---|---|
| Citizen | Legal resident or recognized person. | Petition, vote where allowed, report crimes, join public hearings. | Low. Mostly personal life. |
| Landowner | Owns property or land. | Local complaints, tenant rules, small taxes or rent, building requests. | Low to moderate. Upkeep and disputes. |
| Merchant Holder | Holds stalls, shops, trade rights, or contracts. | Market lobbying, supply influence, contract pressure. | Moderate. Goods, prices, workers, taxes. |
| Guild Representative | Speaks for a guild or profession. | Lobby laws, request exemptions, post formal demands. | Moderate. Must answer guild members. |
| Faction Officer | Leads members or squads. | Internal rules, contracts, faction votes, resource management. | Moderate to high. Member needs and conflicts. |
| Councilor | Holds civic vote or advisory seat. | Votes on laws, budgets, policies, appointments. | High. Hearings, bribes, public pressure. |
| Mayor | Runs a town or city district. | Local laws, city services, taxes, guards, building permits. | High. Citizens blame the mayor first. |
| Noble | Holds title, estate, land, retainers, and political claim. | Land law, private guards, taxes, court influence, succession. | High. Rival houses, scandals, obligations. |
| Governor | Oversees a region for a ruler, kingdom, or occupying power. | Regional law, defense, tax collection, settlement policy. | Very high. Multiple settlements and factions. |
| Ruler | King, Queen, Emperor, Dynasty head, or equivalent. | Kingdom law, war, diplomacy, armies, high taxes, succession. | Extreme. The kingdom never stops needing things. |
| Shadow Authority | Criminal boss, hidden syndicate, secret cult, or uncaught dark power. | Illegal law, protection rackets, smuggling, assassinations, black-market influence. | High and dangerous. Exposure can destroy everything. |

#### Law Proposal Flow

Laws should move through a process rather than appearing instantly.

Standard flow:

1. **Issue Appears:** Citizens, NPCs, guilds, factions, nobles, criminals, monsters, churches, or players identify a problem.
2. **Proposal Drafted:** A law, policy, tax, ban, right, permit, reform, or emergency rule is written.
3. **Sponsor Found:** Someone with enough authority agrees to bring the proposal forward.
4. **Lobbying Begins:** Supporters and opponents use reputation, gold, favors, evidence, threats, speeches, contracts, bribes, and public pressure.
5. **Hearing Held:** The highest authority, council, court, guild board, noble assembly, criminal table, or ruler hears arguments.
6. **Decision Path Chosen:** The highest authority may pass it directly, reject it, delay it, rewrite it, demand proof, or put it to a vote.
7. **Vote Or Decree:** The law passes or fails through ruler approval, council vote, guild vote, citizen vote, faction vote, court order, or criminal command.
8. **Enforcement Starts:** Guards, courts, guilds, tax collectors, priests, officers, or criminal enforcers apply the law.
9. **Reaction Occurs:** Citizens, factions, markets, criminals, nobles, monsters, and guilds react.
10. **Review Or Repeal:** Bad laws can be challenged, revised, ignored, rebelled against, or abolished.

#### Decision Models

| Model | Who Decides | Use Case | Risk |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ruler Decree | Highest ruler passes or rejects directly. | War, emergency, royal policy, kingdom law. | Tyranny, rebellion, assassination. |
| Council Vote | Councilors vote after debate. | City policy, budgets, permits, reforms. | Bribery, deadlock, faction blocs. |
| Public Vote | Eligible citizens vote. | Local matters, elected offices, major reforms. | Popular panic, propaganda, slow results. |
| Guild Vote | Guild members or leaders decide. | Profession rules, trade terms, guild contracts. | Monopoly, exclusion, corruption. |
| Noble Assembly | Noble houses negotiate or vote. | Land, succession, taxes, marriage, war support. | House rivalry, sabotage, secret deals. |
| Court Ruling | Judge, magistrate, or tribunal rules. | Crime, disputes, contracts, rights. | Corrupt judges, legal loopholes. |
| Faction Charter | Faction rules decide. | Player faction law, internal policy. | Splits, coups, member revolt. |
| Criminal Table | Hidden crime authority decides. | Underworld law, protection, smuggling, dark contracts. | Exposure, betrayal, violent enforcement. |
| Divine Or Dragon Pact | Oath, deity, dragon, or relic constrains law. | Sacred land, dragon dungeon, holy city. | Curse, divine anger, dragon retaliation. |

#### Lobbying And Influence

Players with lower authority can still shape law by lobbying. They may not have the power to pass laws directly, but they can win favor, buy attention, gather evidence, rally citizens, sway councilors, or pressure rulers.

Lobbying tools:

- Gold donations
- Campaign funding
- Bribes
- Shaohr trade contracts
- Double-headed coin trickery
- Public speeches
- Bard songs
- Guild pressure
- Faction backing
- Religious endorsement
- Noble marriage ties
- Blackmail
- Threats
- Assassination pressure
- Evidence gathering
- Citizen petitions
- Market strikes
- Labor strikes
- Monster or slime rights testimony
- Dragon or legendary NPC support
- Proof from quests

Influence rule:

- Funds can improve access, attention, and odds.
- Funds can buy lobbyists, lawyers, witnesses, guards, propaganda, bribes, and public events.
- Funds cannot guarantee passage.
- A wealthy player can still lose if the public hates the law, the ruler rejects it, evidence is weak, guilds oppose it, divine law interferes, or a rival outmaneuvers them.
- Failed bribery can become blackmail, scandal, arrest, faction hostility, or criminal leverage.

#### Law Categories

| Law Type | Examples |
|---|---|
| Tax Law | Property taxes, market fees, dungeon taxes, guild dues, import/export fees. |
| Land Law | Zoning, ownership, eviction, farming rights, building permits. |
| Trade Law | Stall permits, auction rules, banking, price controls, trade routes. |
| Crime Law | Theft, murder, assault, fraud, smuggling, slavery, kidnapping. |
| Magic Law | Spell damage, rune permits, resurrection rules, forbidden magic response. |
| Race Law | Demon rights, beastfolk rights, undead personhood, monster citizenship. |
| Guild Law | Guild authority, rank access, contract rules, professional standards. |
| Dungeon Law | Floor access, loot rights, map sales, dragon treaty rules, farming permits. |
| Faction Law | Recruitment, war declarations, uniforms, public assembly, faction bases. |
| Religious Law | Temple rights, holy days, exorcism rules, burial rites, heresy trials. |
| Military Law | Guard authority, militias, conscription, war taxes, weapon restrictions. |
| Social Law | Brothel regulation, alcohol/drugs, public conduct, curfews, festivals. |
| Emergency Law | Plague lockdown, monster siege, dragon alarm, famine rationing, riot control. |

#### Responsibility And Free Time

Authority should cost time.

| Authority Role | Daily Burden | Free Time Impact |
|---|---|---|
| Citizen | Personal needs, jobs, quests. | High free time. |
| Landowner | Repairs, rent, tenant problems, taxes. | Minor loss of free time. |
| Merchant Holder | Stock, staff, prices, contracts. | Moderate management. |
| Councilor | Hearings, votes, petitions, faction pressure. | Regular civic interruptions. |
| Mayor | Services, guards, taxes, unrest, construction, crime. | Heavy management unless delegated. |
| Noble | Land, retainers, marriage, rivals, court appearances. | Heavy social and political obligations. |
| Governor | Multiple settlements, military, taxes, policy enforcement. | Very heavy administration. |
| Ruler | Kingdom law, diplomacy, army, succession, rebellions, crises. | Constant responsibility. |
| Shadow Authority | Secrecy, enforcers, bribes, rivals, laundering, law avoidance. | High risk and constant hidden pressure. |

Delegation can restore free time. A ruler can appoint ministers, mayors, officers, judges, treasurers, marshals, spies, tax collectors, and contractors. Delegates can be loyal, incompetent, corrupt, ambitious, or secretly hostile.

#### Courts And Enforcement

Law needs people to enforce it.

Enforcement bodies:

- City guards
- Knights Guild
- Private guards
- Noble retainers
- Guild inspectors
- Tax collectors
- Courts
- Judges
- Magistrates
- Priests
- Paladins
- Exorcists
- Bounty hunters
- Prison wardens
- Criminal enforcers
- Faction officers
- Dungeon wardens
- Dragon treaty guardians

Court outcomes:

- Fine
- Warning
- Restitution
- Community service
- Jail
- Prison labor
- Exile
- Asset seizure
- Public apology
- Duel judgment
- Execution
- Magical binding
- Exorcism
- Forced oath
- Contract enforcement
- Pardon
- Corrupt dismissal

#### Crime As Authority

Criminal groups can function as authority structures if they have power, fear, secrecy, money, or public usefulness.

Criminal authority types:

| Type | Power Source |
|---|---|
| Street Crew | Local fear, theft, protection money. |
| Smuggling Ring | Trade routes, hidden goods, bribed guards. |
| Assassins' Table | Dark contracts, fear, elite killers. |
| Black Market Court | Stolen goods, illegal auctions, forbidden relics. |
| Protection Syndicate | "Safety" payments and neighborhood control. |
| Shadow Noble | A noble secretly controls crime. |
| Cult Authority | Faith, fear, relics, hidden rites. |
| Dungeon Cartel | Controls maps, keys, floor routes, or loot laundering. |
| Criminal Kingdom | Open law is weak; underworld law runs the region. |

Criminal authority remains valid while:

- It is not exposed enough for abolition.
- Law enforcement cannot remove it.
- Citizens rely on it.
- Nobles protect it.
- Guilds secretly benefit.
- It controls violence, money, routes, or secrets.
- It has enough fear or loyalty to survive.

Criminal authority can be abolished by:

- Investigation
- Public proof
- Assassination
- Rival takeover
- Guild raid
- Noble betrayal
- Player faction war
- Internal rebellion
- Economic collapse
- Church purge
- Dragon or deity intervention
- Thieves Guild restructuring

#### Rebellion, Impeachment, And Removal

Authority can be challenged.

Removal paths:

| Path | How It Works |
|---|---|
| Election Loss | Voters choose a different leader. |
| Council Removal | Council votes to remove or limit authority. |
| Noble Challenge | Rival house disputes legitimacy. |
| Guild Sanction | Guilds refuse services, contracts, or recognition. |
| Public Revolt | Citizens riot, strike, or overthrow authority. |
| Criminal Coup | Underworld replaces legal authority or captures it. |
| Military Coup | Guards, knights, or army seize control. |
| Debt Takeover | Bankers or merchants seize power through unpaid debts. |
| Divine Condemnation | Church, deity, relic, or curse delegitimizes rule. |
| Dragon Rejection | A dragon treaty or Dungeon Lord claim collapses. |
| Assassination | Leader is killed, creating succession crisis. |
| Abdication | Leader willingly gives up power. |
| Purchase Transfer | Authority is bought, sold, inherited, or contractually transferred. |

Removal should create consequences, not just a menu change. Succession disputes, faction wars, citizen unrest, debt claims, and revenge quests can follow.

#### Law Data Handoff

Recommended law data fields:

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| LawId | Unique law identifier. |
| Name | Player-facing law name. |
| Jurisdiction | Property, district, city, region, kingdom, faction, guild, underworld. |
| Sponsor | Player, NPC, faction, guild, noble, criminal, ruler. |
| ProposalText | What the law does. |
| LawType | Tax, land, trade, crime, magic, race, guild, dungeon, etc. |
| DecisionModel | Decree, council vote, public vote, court ruling, criminal table, etc. |
| Supporters | Groups or people backing it. |
| Opponents | Groups or people resisting it. |
| LobbyFunds | Legal funding spent to influence support. |
| BribeRisk | Chance of scandal or blackmail from illegal influence. |
| VoteState | Draft, lobbying, hearing, voting, passed, failed, delayed, repealed. |
| EnforcementBody | Guards, guild, court, faction, criminals, temple, dragon pact. |
| Penalties | Fines, jail, exile, seizure, oaths, execution, etc. |
| PublicReaction | Happiness, unrest, protests, support, fear. |
| MarketImpact | Taxes, prices, shortages, trade routes, black market effects. |
| FactionImpact | Reputation changes, alliances, rivalries, war pressure. |
| ReviewTimer | When the law can be challenged, renewed, or repealed. |
| History | Who proposed, funded, passed, broke, enforced, or exploited it. |

#### Governance Implementation Checklist

Governance is implementation-ready when the game supports:

- Authority roles and jurisdiction.
- Law proposal drafting.
- Sponsors and hearings.
- Voting and decree systems.
- Lobbying funds and influence tracking.
- Bribery, blackmail, and scandal risk.
- Courts and enforcement bodies.
- Crime records.
- Warrants, fines, and penalties.
- Citizen petitions.
- Council, guild, noble, faction, and criminal decision models.
- Responsibility load and delegation.
- NPC and player officials.
- Rebellion, removal, and succession systems.
- Criminal authority structures.
- Law impact on markets, settlements, factions, and NPC happiness.
- Server rumor and announcement hooks for major laws.

#### Final Governance Rules

- Law should feel like power moving through people, money, fear, belief, and responsibility.
- The highest authority can pass laws directly, reject them, rewrite them, delay them, or put them to a vote.
- Lower-authority players can still change history by lobbying, funding, gathering proof, winning public favor, and making alliances.
- Gold can open doors, but it cannot guarantee obedience.
- Rulers gain power and lose free time.
- Citizens have less power and more freedom.
- Criminals can be authority figures as long as their structure survives.
- Every law should create winners, losers, loopholes, and future quests.

## Crime, Punishment, And Underworld

Crime is not only a punishment system. In Huwan, crime is a competing form of power. A thief can feed a poor district, an assassin can prevent a war, a smuggler can save persecuted refugees, and a corrupt noble can make law itself criminal. The system should track the act, the proof, the witnesses, the law, the motive, the victim, the jurisdiction, and who benefits from pretending it never happened.

### Crime, Punishment, And Underworld System Completion V1.0

Core rule:

- **The act matters, but proof decides what the world can do about it.**
- Harming someone unlawfully is a crime.
- Useful tools, dangerous magic, weapons, assassinations, thieves, and dark contracts are not automatically illegal everywhere.
- The law cares about jurisdiction, evidence, witnesses, status, politics, bribes, guild pressure, and public reputation.
- Criminals can build authority if they remain hidden, protected, feared, useful, or too expensive to remove.

#### Crime Categories

| Crime Type | Examples | Common Response |
|---|---|---|
| Theft | Pickpocketing, burglary, stolen goods, chest looting, map theft. | Fine, restitution, bounty, jail, underworld interest. |
| Assault | Unlawful attack, tavern brawl, guard attack, magical harm. | Fine, jail, warrant, reputation loss. |
| Murder | Unlawful killing of player, NPC, citizen, noble, guild member, or protected monster citizen. | Warrant, bounty, prison, execution, faction war. |
| Contract Killing | Assassination under dark contract, bounty execution, political removal. | Legal gray zone; depends on proof, target, issuer, and jurisdiction. |
| Fraud | Fake goods, forged maps, false contracts, fake relics, market scams. | Fines, merchant blacklisting, court case, guild sanction. |
| Bribery | Paying officials, judges, guards, guild clerks, voters, or witnesses illegally. | Scandal, blackmail, fine, imprisonment, hidden leverage. |
| Smuggling | Illegal goods, forbidden magic, stolen relics, refugee routes, untaxed imports. | Confiscation, fines, underworld rank, guard raids. |
| Slavery And Forced Labor | Owning, trafficking, binding, or forcing people under illegal authority. | Severe penalties, faction war, rescue quests, rebellion. |
| Illegal Magic Use | Harmful spells, forbidden rituals, reckless spellwork, mana well attacks. | Mage investigation, fines, prison, magical binding, execution for severe cases. |
| Necromantic Abuse | Unlawful undead creation, corpse theft, soul binding, grave violation. | Church response, exorcists, paladins, public panic. |
| Illegal Dungeon Access | Bypassing permits, stealing floor rights, smuggling relics, false maps. | Guild fines, dungeon ban, bounty, Thieves Guild attention. |
| Tax Evasion | Hidden income, false ledgers, unpaid tariffs, dungeon loot laundering. | Fines, asset seizure, merchant sanctions, Shaohr-related consequences. |
| Treason | Aiding enemies, coup plotting, betraying kingdom, selling secrets. | Imprisonment, exile, execution, noble crisis. |
| Public Corruption | Abuse of office, false law, stolen taxes, illegal arrests. | Impeachment, revolt, court case, assassination risk. |
| Monster Abuse | Illegal harvesting of intelligent monsters, slime exploitation, dragon treaty violation. | Monster hostility, legal challenge, faction response. |

#### Crime Legality States

Not every suspicious act has the same legal status.

| State | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Legal | Allowed by local law. | Carrying a sword in a frontier town. |
| Regulated | Allowed with permit, rank, guild status, or tax. | Selling potions, dungeon maps, spell services. |
| Tolerated | Technically illegal or gray, but ignored unless abused. | Street gambling, minor bribes, informal protection. |
| Hidden | Illegal but unknown to law. | Smuggling relics through a cellar. |
| Disputed | Law, guild, noble, or faction authorities disagree. | Assassinating a corrupt leader without court proof. |
| Criminal | Clearly illegal with sufficient proof. | Murdering a citizen in public. |
| Protected Crime | Crime shielded by power, bribery, noble privilege, or fear. | A noble's illegal labor ring. |
| Shadow Lawful | Illegal under official law but lawful under underworld authority. | A Thieves Guild-approved retrieval job. |

#### Evidence And Witness System

Crime should depend on proof.

Evidence types:

- Eyewitness testimony
- Guard report
- Victim memory
- NPC memory
- Blood trail
- Footprints
- Stolen item ownership record
- Item provenance
- Contract ledger
- Bank record
- Market receipt
- Rune residue
- Spell signature
- Scrying record
- Corpse condition
- Monster remains
- Map route record
- Guild board proof
- Confession
- Bribed witness
- Forged evidence
- Bardic truth song
- Divine, draconic, or relic witness

Evidence quality:

| Quality | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Weak | Suspicion, rumor, circumstantial clue. |
| Useful | Supports a case but needs more proof. |
| Strong | Enough for warrants, fines, or court action. |
| Overwhelming | Very hard to deny without corruption. |
| Corrupt | Planted, altered, bribed, or magically tampered. |
| Sacred | Backed by deity, dragon, relic, oath, or truth magic. |

Witness rules:

- Witnesses can lie, forget, hide, be bribed, be threatened, be mistaken, or be protected.
- NPCs remember crimes based on proximity, fear, relationship, intelligence, and social risk.
- Poor citizens may know more than nobles expect.
- Criminal witnesses may sell proof to either side.
- Bardic, divine, rune, and dragon-memory evidence can be powerful but politically dangerous.

#### Wanted Level And Jurisdiction

Wanted status is not universal by default. It depends on where the crime happened, who was harmed, and who has authority.

Wanted scopes:

| Scope | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Local | One district, village, town, or city. |
| Guild | A guild recognizes the player or faction as wanted. |
| Faction | A faction hunts, bans, or marks the player. |
| Kingdom | Entire kingdom recognizes the crime. |
| Underworld | Criminal powers want the player punished, recruited, or silenced. |
| Religious | Church, temple, paladins, exorcists, or faith order responds. |
| Monster | Slimes, dragons, beastfolk, undead, or monster citizens react. |
| Server-Notable | Major crimes become public story or world rumor. |

Wanted levels:

| Level | Name | Meaning |
|---:|---|---|
| 0 | Clean | No active legal concern. |
| 1 | Suspect | Guards or NPCs may question the player. |
| 2 | Wanted For Questioning | Player may be stopped, searched, or summoned. |
| 3 | Warranted | Guards, bounty hunters, or guild officers can arrest. |
| 4 | Dangerous Fugitive | Armed response, bounties, asset freezes, travel difficulty. |
| 5 | Public Enemy | Kingdom, guild, or major faction mobilizes. |
| 6 | World Threat | Server-level danger, legendary hunters, war-level consequences. |

Wanted status can decay, escalate, be cleared, be bribed away, be tried in court, be pardoned, be transferred between jurisdictions, or become permanent history.

#### Punishment Types

Punishment should match crime, proof, law, status, and politics.

| Punishment | Use Case |
|---|---|
| Warning | Minor first offense or weak proof. |
| Fine | Theft, disturbance, minor assault, permit violation. |
| Restitution | Repay victim or repair damage. |
| Confiscation | Stolen goods, illegal magic items, smuggled resources. |
| Community Service | Repairs, monster clearing, public labor, healing aid. |
| Jail | Short-term containment before trial or fine payment. |
| Prison | Serious crimes, repeated offenses, violent crimes. |
| Prison Labor | Dangerous mines, road work, dungeon cleanup, city repair. |
| Exile | Removed from settlement, guild, city, kingdom, or faction. |
| Asset Seizure | Property, gold, shops, faction storage, trade rights. |
| Public Shame | Reputation loss, notice boards, bard songs, public apology. |
| Magical Binding | Prevents casting, violence, fleeing, or contract breaking. |
| Forced Oath | Legal or divine promise with consequences. |
| Bounty | Hunters are paid to capture or kill under legal terms. |
| Execution | Extreme crimes, treason, mass murder, severe war crimes. |
| Pardon | Crime forgiven by authority, often with political cost. |
| Secret Deal | Crime covered in exchange for service, bribe, or blackmail. |

#### Arrest, Trial, And Escape

Crime encounters should create gameplay.

Possible legal flow:

1. Crime occurs.
2. Witness or evidence is generated.
3. Suspicion or wanted level begins.
4. Guards investigate.
5. Player is questioned, searched, chased, arrested, or summoned.
6. Trial, fine, bribe, escape, pardon, duel judgment, or underworld intervention occurs.
7. Punishment or acquittal is applied.
8. Reputation and memory update.

Player options:

- Pay fine
- Return stolen goods
- Hire lawyer
- Bribe official
- Threaten witness
- Gather counter-evidence
- Ask faction for help
- Appeal to noble or ruler
- Request trial by combat where legal
- Escape jail
- Serve sentence
- Become fugitive
- Turn informant
- Accept dark deal
- Seek pardon

#### Underworld Rank

Players and NPCs can rise through criminal society.

| Rank | Meaning | Unlocks |
|---|---|---|
| Street Rat | Petty criminal or errand runner. | Small theft jobs, rumors, cheap contraband. |
| Cutpurse | Recognized thief. | Pickpocket work, stolen goods buyers. |
| Burglar | Skilled property thief. | House jobs, lock contracts, better tools. |
| Smuggler | Moves illegal goods. | Hidden routes, fake permits, bribed guards. |
| Enforcer | Uses intimidation or violence. | Protection jobs, debt collection. |
| Fixer | Arranges jobs and covers tracks. | Contacts, bribery, laundering. |
| Assassin | Performs dark contracts. | Target dossiers, stealth routes, special poisons. |
| Shadow Broker | Trades secrets, maps, blackmail, relic routes. | High-value intelligence. |
| Crime Lord | Controls crews, territory, protection, markets. | Shadow authority, faction-level crime power. |
| Underworld Sovereign | Regional criminal ruler. | Criminal law, syndicate diplomacy, hidden army. |

Underworld rank can rise through jobs, secrecy, profit, fear, loyalty, successful escapes, bribery, assassination, smuggling, and control over routes or markets. It can fall through failure, betrayal, exposure, unpaid debts, informant behavior, or public humiliation.

#### Thieves Guild And Dark Contracts

The Thieves Guild should be practical, dangerous, and morally flexible.

Thieves Guild services:

- Stolen goods fencing
- Map sales
- Dungeon routes
- Lockpicks and thieves tools
- Assassination contracts
- Retrieval jobs
- Black-market auctions
- Forged permits
- Hidden housing
- Safe houses
- Smuggling routes
- Evidence tampering
- Witness relocation
- Spy work
- Escape routes

Dark contract rules:

- A dark contract can target corrupt leaders, monsters, dangerous criminals, faction enemies, abusive nobles, witnesses, rivals, or innocent people depending on the issuer.
- The contract's legality depends on jurisdiction, proof, issuer, target, and political consequence.
- A successful assassination can prevent a war or start one.
- Assassination should never be treated as clean just because it is useful.
- Contract records can become blackmail, court evidence, faction leverage, or legendary encounter hooks.

#### Corruption System

Corruption lets money and power bend law without making it certain.

Corrupt actors:

- Guards
- Judges
- Clerks
- Priests
- Guild officers
- Nobles
- Merchants
- Bounty hunters
- Prison wardens
- Witnesses
- Tax collectors
- Faction leaders
- Criminal fixers

Corruption actions:

- Bribe
- Blackmail
- Evidence planting
- Evidence removal
- Witness intimidation
- False warrant
- Case delay
- Asset shielding
- Prison release
- Illegal pardon
- Smuggling permit
- Contract laundering
- Tax record alteration

Corruption risks:

- Scandal
- Blackmail
- Public rage
- Guild sanction
- Noble feud
- Criminal debt
- Shaohr bargain consequence
- Divine curse
- Evidence leak
- Informant betrayal

#### Bounty Hunters And Criminal Pursuit

Bounties turn crime into active gameplay.

Bounty types:

| Bounty Type | Goal |
|---|---|
| Capture Alive | Bring target to law, guild, or faction. |
| Dead Or Alive | Target is considered dangerous enough to kill. |
| Proof Only | Recover item, contract, head, badge, seal, or confession. |
| Exile Enforcement | Drive target from region. |
| Debt Collection | Recover unpaid money or goods. |
| Dark Bounty | Illegal under official law but valid in underworld. |
| Monster Bounty | Capture or kill dangerous monster or intelligent monster criminal. |
| Political Bounty | Target is wanted for power reasons, not necessarily justice. |

Bounty hunters can be players, NPCs, guild officers, orcs, knights, rogues, paladins, mercenaries, monsters, or legendary figures.

#### Crime And Settlements

Settlement crime reacts to services, poverty, law, guards, and opportunity.

Crime rises when:

- Poverty increases
- Food prices rise
- Housing fails
- Guards are corrupt
- Taxes are crushing
- Poor districts are ignored
- Slavery or exploitation spreads
- Nobles abuse power
- Factions fight
- Dungeons create black markets
- Refugees are unsupported
- Drug or alcohol abuse becomes unmanaged
- Criminal authority becomes more reliable than legal authority

Crime falls when:

- Jobs increase
- Food and housing stabilize
- Guards are fair
- Courts are trusted
- Public services function
- Citizens feel heard
- Criminal bosses are exposed
- Underworld incentives are reduced
- Factions provide protection without extortion
- Player leaders invest in quality of life

#### Crime And Player Freedom

Players can become:

- Law-abiding citizens
- Petty thieves
- Professional criminals
- Bounty hunters
- Informants
- Corrupt officials
- Dark-contract assassins
- Smugglers
- Crime bosses
- Underworld rulers
- Reformers
- Prisoners
- Fugitives
- Pardoned criminals
- Double agents

The game should allow criminal play, but criminal play should create risk, reputation, enemies, evidence, and consequences.

#### Crime Data Handoff

Recommended crime data fields:

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| CrimeId | Unique crime record. |
| CrimeType | Theft, assault, murder, fraud, smuggling, etc. |
| Actor | Player, NPC, faction, guild, noble, monster, unknown. |
| Victim | Person, faction, settlement, guild, kingdom, property, monster group. |
| Location | Jurisdiction and map location. |
| Time | Server time when crime occurred. |
| Witnesses | NPCs, players, magic records, guild reports, divine or dragon witnesses. |
| Evidence | Items, spell signatures, ledgers, footprints, bodies, testimony. |
| EvidenceQuality | Weak, useful, strong, overwhelming, corrupt, sacred. |
| WantedScope | Local, guild, faction, kingdom, underworld, religious, monster, server. |
| WantedLevel | 0-6. |
| LawReference | Which law was broken or disputed. |
| EnforcementBody | Guards, guild, faction, criminals, church, nobles, bounty hunters. |
| PunishmentOptions | Fine, jail, exile, bounty, execution, etc. |
| BribeState | None, attempted, accepted, failed, exposed, blackmail. |
| TrialState | None, pending, active, convicted, acquitted, escaped, pardoned. |
| ReputationImpact | Local, guild, faction, kingdom, underworld, world. |
| MemoryImpact | Who remembers and how strongly. |
| Resolution | Paid, served, escaped, pardoned, overturned, unresolved. |

#### Crime Implementation Checklist

Crime is implementation-ready when the game supports:

- Crime detection.
- Evidence generation.
- Witness memory.
- Jurisdiction tracking.
- Wanted levels by scope.
- Guard investigation.
- Arrest and pursuit.
- Trials and fines.
- Prison and escape.
- Bribery and corruption.
- Bounty boards.
- Underworld rank.
- Stolen goods provenance.
- Smuggling routes.
- Assassination contracts.
- Criminal faction authority.
- Settlement crime rate.
- Reputation and memory consequences.
- Pardon, appeal, and restitution.
- Server rumor and announcement hooks for major crimes.

#### Final Crime Rules

- Crime should create gameplay, not only punishment.
- Proof matters as much as guilt.
- Law can be fair, corrupt, slow, or weaponized.
- Criminals can become powerful if they remain useful or hidden.
- A dark contract can solve a problem and still stain the world.
- The poor, the rich, the guilds, the nobles, and the underworld should all experience law differently.
- Players can live outside the law, but the world should remember the cost.

## Contracts, Oaths, And Deals

Contracts are one of the most powerful forces in Huwan. Gold can buy labor, oaths can bind kingdoms, dark contracts can remove tyrants, warlock pacts can rewrite a life, dragon treaties can preserve a dungeon, and Shaohr-blessed agreements can turn ordinary trade into something the world itself seems to respect.

Contracts should feel official, dangerous, tempting, and alive.

### Contracts, Oaths, And Deals System Completion V1.0

Core rule:

- **A contract is a promise with teeth.**
- A contract can be legal, illegal, sacred, criminal, magical, divine, draconic, personal, factional, or economic.
- Breaking a contract should create consequences based on who witnessed it, what symbol sealed it, what power backs it, and how public the breach becomes.
- Contracts should connect to the HUD through a living **Contract Board** so players always know what they have agreed to, who they owe, who owes them, and what happens if the deal breaks.

#### HUD Contract Board

The **HUD Contract Board** is the player's portable contract ledger. It is not just a quest list. It is a living board of obligations, offers, debts, oaths, treaties, bribes, pacts, assassination contracts, trade agreements, faction deals, and legal risks.

HUD feel:

- Contracts appear as pinned cards, stamped notices, sealed letters, dark vellum, gold-edged trade papers, divine writs, dragon-scale treaties, or shadow contracts depending on type.
- Urgent contracts should pulse subtly.
- Dangerous contracts should show warning marks.
- Secret contracts should be hidden behind privacy locks, false labels, or underworld tabs.
- Shaohr-blessed deals should show a scale-of-finance seal.
- Double-headed coin bribes should show a coin mark only visible to involved parties or skilled investigators.
- Broken contracts should crack, bleed ink, burn at the edge, tarnish, or turn gray depending on backing power.

Contract Board tabs:

| Tab | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Offers | Available contracts the player can accept. |
| Active | Contracts currently binding the player or party. |
| Debts | Money, favors, goods, protection, or service owed. |
| Oaths | Personal, divine, guild, faction, knightly, or marriage vows. |
| Trade | Merchant deals, buy orders, sell orders, caravans, imports, exports. |
| Dark | Thieves Guild work, assassination contracts, black-market deals, smuggling. |
| Faction | Faction treaties, war terms, member duties, settlement contracts. |
| Land | Property deeds, leases, city purchases, kingdom authority, build contracts. |
| Magic | Warlock pacts, rune agreements, spell rights, Mages Guild restrictions. |
| Dragon | Dragon treaties, dungeon rights, tribute agreements, Dungeon Lord terms. |
| Completed | Finished contracts, rewards, reputation effects, and proof. |
| Broken | Failed, betrayed, voided, disputed, cursed, or litigated contracts. |

#### Contract Types

| Contract Type | Description | Common Backing |
|---|---|---|
| Legal Contract | Standard agreement recognized by law. | Courts, guilds, nobles, guards. |
| Trade Contract | Goods, services, imports, exports, markets, auctions. | Merchants Guild, Shaohr, banks. |
| Work Contract | Labor, crafting, gathering, construction, repair, escort. | Guilds, factions, settlements. |
| Quest Contract | Formal task with reward and proof conditions. | Guild boards, NPCs, factions. |
| Dark Contract | Illegal or hidden work. | Thieves Guild, criminals, underworld authority. |
| Assassination Contract | Contract to remove a target. | Thieves Guild, rulers, factions, private issuers. |
| Bribe Contract | Hidden exchange for influence or illegal favor. | Double-headed coin, blackmail, corrupt officials. |
| Debt Contract | Loan, payment plan, debt transfer, mortgage, ransom. | Banks, merchants, nobles, Shaohr. |
| Land Contract | Property, deeds, leases, city purchase, kingdom transfer. | Nobles, rulers, land registries, banks. |
| Faction Treaty | Alliance, truce, war terms, vassalage, trade pact. | Faction leaders, witnesses, courts. |
| Marriage Contract | Marriage, inheritance, alliance, dowry, succession. | Noble houses, temples, courts. |
| Warlock Pact | Magic power in exchange for patron demands. | Deity, Arch-Fey, Arch-Demon, dragon, SS monster, Laucian. |
| Divine Oath | Promise witnessed by deity, relic, church, or sacred law. | Pantheon, priests, paladins, relics. |
| Dragon Treaty | Agreement with dragon or dragon-born dungeon. | Dragon memory, dungeon mana, hoard witness. |
| Rune Contract | Contract enforced by runes. | Rune geometry, mana triggers, dragon language. |
| Blood Contract | Agreement sealed by blood, life force, or lineage. | Magic, curses, family law, dark rituals. |
| Soul Contract | Extreme pact tied to soul, reincarnation, afterlife, or patronage. | Deities, demons, liches, forbidden magic. |

#### Contract Anatomy

Every contract should track:

| Field | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Parties | Who is bound by the deal. |
| Issuer | Who created or posted it. |
| Beneficiary | Who gains if it succeeds. |
| Target | Person, object, place, faction, resource, law, or outcome involved. |
| Terms | What each side must do. |
| Reward | Gold, item, favor, access, land, title, reputation, protection, magic, information. |
| Penalty | Fine, reputation loss, curse, bounty, debt, exile, loss of access, enforcement. |
| Proof | Item, witness, kill proof, delivery mark, signature, ledger, magic seal, map stamp. |
| Deadline | Server time limit, if any. |
| Jurisdiction | Where the contract is recognized. |
| Visibility | Public, private, party, faction, guild, underworld, secret. |
| Backing Power | Court, guild, Shaohr, deity, dragon, rune, patron, criminal authority. |
| Break Conditions | What counts as failure, betrayal, loophole, or voiding. |
| Dispute Path | Court, guild arbitration, duel, underworld judgment, divine trial, dragon judgment. |

#### Contract Board Card States

| State | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Offered | Available but not accepted. |
| Drafting | Terms are being edited, negotiated, or reviewed. |
| Pending Signatures | Waiting on one or more parties. |
| Active | Binding and in progress. |
| Paused | Temporarily suspended by agreement, law, crisis, or server event. |
| Completed | Terms fulfilled and reward settled. |
| Failed | Terms not met without deliberate betrayal. |
| Breached | One party broke terms. |
| Betrayed | One party intentionally violated trust. |
| Disputed | Parties disagree on whether terms were met. |
| Litigated | Courts, guilds, or legal authorities are involved. |
| Underworld Review | Criminal authority is judging the breach. |
| Cursed | Magical, divine, draconic, or rune penalty activated. |
| Voided | Contract is legally canceled. |
| Fulfilled In Blood | Contract completed through death, sacrifice, or extreme cost. |

#### Shaohr-Blessed Deals

If both parties invoke Shaohr's symbol during a trade deal, the agreement becomes finance-blessed.

Effects:

- Both sides receive extra perks if the deal is honored.
- Trade reputation improves.
- Contract proof becomes easier to verify.
- Fraud becomes more dangerous.
- Breaking the deal can damage wealth, credit, luck, family finances, or merchant access.
- Shaohr may reward clever profit, but he despises being cheated through incompetence, unpaid debt, or disrespect.

Shaohr deal examples:

- Trade route contract
- Land sale
- Merchant partnership
- City purchase
- Auction guarantee
- Debt settlement
- Guild supply agreement
- Dungeon resource rights
- Faction treaty with financial terms

#### Double-Headed Coin Bribes

The **double-headed coin** is used when a deal is not clean, but both sides want it to feel satisfying.

Bribe effects:

- Improves the chance a corrupt official, witness, guard, clerk, judge, guild officer, or noble will cooperate.
- Can reduce paperwork, delay a case, unlock a hearing, hide evidence, alter a route, or move a contract forward.
- Does not guarantee success.
- Creates a hidden debt, blackmail risk, or corruption trail.
- If exposed, both briber and receiver can suffer scandal.

Double-headed coin outcomes:

| Outcome | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Smooth Turn | Bribe works quietly. |
| Greedy Hand | Bribe works, but the receiver wants more later. |
| Witnessed Flip | Someone saw the bribe happen. |
| False Coin | The receiver reports the player or sets a trap. |
| Blackmail Seed | The bribe works but becomes future leverage. |
| Shaohr's Smirk | The bribe creates profit, but also attracts finance-god attention. |

#### Assassination Contracts

Assassination contracts should have enough structure to support ethical, corrupt, political, and criminal use.

Assassination contract fields:

| Field | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Target | Who is to be removed. |
| Issuer | Who requested the contract. |
| Cause | Why the target is being removed. |
| Evidence | Proof of corruption, danger, abuse, or threat. |
| Collateral Rules | Who must not be harmed. |
| Method Rules | Poison, duel, accident, public execution, silent kill, capture first. |
| Proof Required | Ring, seal, confession, body, memory crystal, witness, contract mark. |
| Legal Risk | How law may respond if exposed. |
| Moral Weight | How society may judge the act. |
| Underworld Backing | Whether the Thieves Guild or dark court recognizes it. |
| Exit Terms | Payment, silence, witness relocation, evidence disposal. |

Ethical assassination conditions:

- The target is causing severe harm.
- Normal courts cannot act or are corrupted.
- Evidence exists.
- Collateral harm is forbidden.
- The issuer accepts risk.
- The assassin can refuse unjust terms.
- The contract may still be judged by law, faith, public opinion, or history.

#### Warlock Pacts

Warlock pacts are contracts for power.

Patrons can include:

- Deities
- Arch-Fey
- Arch-Demons
- Dragons
- Intelligent SS monsters
- Laucian Xilocent Goldpetal
- Ancient spirits
- Forbidden entities

Warlock pact rules:

- The patron must be able to provide ongoing mana, power, knowledge, or magic access.
- The warlock gains power but accepts obligations.
- If the warlock refuses patron demands, power can be reduced, locked, twisted, reclaimed, or contested.
- Some patrons are fair. Some are cruel. Some are hilarious. Some are terrifyingly legalistic.
- Pact terms should be readable in the HUD, but some clauses may be hidden unless discovered.

#### Dragon Treaties

Dragon treaties bind players, factions, settlements, or Dungeon Lords to a dragon or dragon-born dungeon.

Dragon treaty terms can include:

- Tribute
- Floor access
- Resource limits
- Guardian protection
- Hoard return
- No-kill rules
- Dungeon farming rights
- Safe rest points
- Dragon defense obligations
- Settlement protection
- Taming boundaries
- Dungeon Lord permissions

Breaking a dragon treaty can cause floor hostility, guardian attacks, dragon anger, market collapse, dungeon access loss, or regional threat.

#### Contract Breaking And Loopholes

Contracts can be broken, exploited, misread, forged, or twisted.

Break types:

| Break Type | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Failure | Terms not completed. |
| Breach | One party violates terms. |
| Betrayal | Violation with intent to harm or deceive. |
| Loophole | Terms technically obeyed but spirit violated. |
| Fraud | Contract built on lies or forged identity. |
| Coercion | One party was forced unfairly. |
| Impossible Terms | Contract cannot be completed as written. |
| Divine Conflict | Contract violates higher sacred law. |
| Dragon Rejection | Dragon or dungeon refuses the interpretation. |
| Underworld Reversal | Criminal authority declares the contract dishonorable. |

Loopholes can be valid gameplay, especially for merchants, lawyers, rogues, nobles, and Shaohr followers. However, exploiting loopholes can damage reputation, trigger future contract distrust, or draw attention from contract specialists.

#### Contract Rewards And Penalties

Rewards can include:

- Gold
- Items
- Land
- Discounts
- Guild rank
- Faction reputation
- Debt forgiveness
- Titles
- Legal rights
- Dungeon access
- Dragon favor
- Spell access
- Warlock power
- Black-market access
- Marriage alliance
- Noble recognition
- Public reputation

Penalties can include:

- Fines
- Debt
- Curses
- Loss of access
- Reputation damage
- Wanted level
- Asset seizure
- Contract ban
- Guild sanction
- Underworld bounty
- Divine disfavor
- Dragon anger
- Patron punishment
- Legal trial
- Public scandal

#### Contract Board Integration

The Contract Board should connect with:

- Quest menu
- Market and auction menu
- Faction menu
- Land Buy Mode
- Build Mode
- Crime records
- Law proposals
- Guild boards
- Party and raid objectives
- NPC memory
- Settlement services
- Dungeon rights
- Legendary encounters
- Warlock class progression
- Dragon relationship system

Contract visibility examples:

| Visibility | Who Can See |
|---|---|
| Public | Anyone checking the relevant board. |
| Guild | Members or eligible ranks. |
| Party | Party members. |
| Faction | Faction members with permission. |
| Private | Direct parties only. |
| Hidden | Disguised or locked until discovered. |
| Underworld | Criminal contacts, Thieves Guild, black market. |
| Divine | Priest, paladin, deity-linked, or oath-bound parties. |
| Draconic | Dragon, Dungeon Lord, dragon treaty parties. |

#### Contract Data Handoff

Recommended contract data fields:

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| ContractId | Unique contract identifier. |
| Name | Player-facing name. |
| ContractType | Legal, trade, dark, assassination, bribe, pact, treaty, land, etc. |
| Parties | Players, NPCs, factions, guilds, dragons, patrons, settlements. |
| Issuer | Who created the contract. |
| Beneficiary | Who benefits from completion. |
| Target | Person, item, land, resource, law, route, monster, or outcome. |
| Terms | Required actions. |
| Rewards | Payment, access, items, reputation, rights, magic. |
| Penalties | Failure or breach consequences. |
| Deadline | Server time limit or none. |
| Visibility | Public, private, guild, faction, underworld, hidden, divine, draconic. |
| BackingPower | Court, guild, Shaohr, coin, rune, deity, dragon, patron, underworld. |
| ProofRequired | What confirms completion. |
| Status | Offered, active, completed, failed, breached, disputed, cursed, voided. |
| Jurisdiction | Where the contract is recognized. |
| BreakConditions | What counts as breach or betrayal. |
| DisputePath | Court, guild, underworld, deity, dragon, duel, arbitration. |
| MemoryImpact | Who remembers the deal and how strongly. |
| ReputationImpact | Local, guild, faction, kingdom, underworld, divine, dragon. |
| HUDStyle | Visual treatment on the Contract Board. |

#### Contract Implementation Checklist

Contracts are implementation-ready when the game supports:

- HUD Contract Board.
- Contract offers and acceptance.
- Contract drafting and negotiation.
- Contract visibility permissions.
- Active contract tracking.
- Party, faction, guild, legal, underworld, divine, and draconic contract scopes.
- Deadlines using living server time.
- Proof submission.
- Completion rewards.
- Breach and betrayal handling.
- Dispute resolution.
- Bribes and double-headed coin outcomes.
- Shaohr-blessed deal bonuses and penalties.
- Assassination contract structure.
- Warlock pact tracking.
- Dragon treaty tracking.
- Land and city purchase contract integration.
- NPC memory and reputation updates.
- Contract history and audit logs.

#### Final Contract Rules

- A contract is a promise the world can remember.
- Honest contracts build trust.
- Dark contracts build leverage.
- Divine oaths build consequence.
- Dragon treaties build living danger.
- Warlock pacts build power with a hook in it.
- Bribes can work, but they should leave fingerprints.
- Shaohr-blessed deals should reward profitable honor and punish foolish betrayal.
- The HUD Contract Board should make the player feel like every promise, debt, oath, and dirty little deal is sitting right there, waiting to be honored or broken.

## Reputation, Fame, Infamy, And World Popularity

Reputation is how Huwan remembers. Every law passed, contract honored, crime hidden, dungeon cleared, dragon spared, city purchased, faction founded, romance built, assassination justified, oath broken, market saved, or poor district abandoned can change how the world talks about a player.

### Reputation, Fame, Infamy, And World Popularity System Completion V1.0

Core rule:

- **Relationships determine the story, and reputation is how those relationships echo through the world.**
- Reputation is local, regional, factional, racial, guild-based, religious, underworld, monster-facing, and global.
- Fame and infamy are not opposites. A player can be famous for heroic deeds, infamous for cruelty, respected for skill, feared for power, loved by the poor, hated by nobles, trusted by dragons, and wanted by a kingdom all at the same time.
- World Popularity is the hidden long-term stat that measures how widely the player is known across Huwan and server history.

#### Reputation Layers

| Layer | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Personal Reputation | How one NPC remembers the player. | Mara trusts the player because they brought food without demanding payment. |
| Household Reputation | How a family or home reacts. | A rescued child causes their family to welcome the player. |
| District Reputation | How a city district talks about the player. | Low Lantern Ward calls the player a protector. |
| City Reputation | How an entire settlement views the player. | Eldoria sees the player as useful, dangerous, heroic, or suspicious. |
| Guild Reputation | How official guilds treat the player. | Adventurers Guild offers better quests; Merchants Guild offers discounts. |
| Faction Reputation | How ideological, criminal, political, or player factions react. | Peace Between Us sends invitations; Doomsday marks the player as enemy. |
| Race Reputation | How a race or sub-race culture tends to view the player. | Demons remember whether the player defended or persecuted them. |
| Kingdom Reputation | How a kingdom's law, nobles, citizens, and guards treat the player. | Nuwen honors the player or places them under warrant. |
| Religious Reputation | How churches, priests, paladins, and deity-aligned groups react. | Pharmos followers bless the player or watch them with concern. |
| Underworld Reputation | How thieves, assassins, smugglers, and criminal powers view the player. | The player becomes trusted, hunted, mocked, or feared in dark markets. |
| Monster Reputation | How intelligent monsters, slimes, undead, dragons, and beastfolk communities react. | Mallow Crown-Gel recognizes the player as slime-safe. |
| World Reputation | Server-level reputation beyond one region. | Bards, forums, guilds, and distant NPCs begin repeating the player's name. |
| World Popularity | Hidden stat measuring global recognition, story weight, and future demi-god relevance. | The player becomes important enough that the world starts making room for their choices. |

#### Fame, Infamy, Respect, And Fear

Reputation should use several emotional tracks instead of one simple good/bad meter.

| Track | Meaning | Example Behavior |
|---|---|---|
| Fame | The player is widely known. | NPCs recognize the name, bards mention them, guilds send offers. |
| Infamy | The player is known for dangerous or immoral acts. | Guards watch them, victims fear them, criminals may respect them. |
| Respect | The player is considered capable, honorable, skilled, or reliable. | Leaders ask for help even if they do not like the player. |
| Fear | The player is considered dangerous. | Enemies avoid them, citizens whisper, rulers prepare countermeasures. |
| Trust | The player is believed to keep promises. | Better contracts, more sensitive quests, lower suspicion. |
| Suspicion | The player is believed to hide something. | Harder negotiations, more guards, fewer private meetings. |
| Love | The player is emotionally cherished by people or groups. | Citizens defend them, NPCs risk themselves, romance paths deepen. |
| Hate | The player is personally or culturally hated. | Sabotage, higher prices, hostile rumors, assassination attempts. |
| Awe | The player is seen as beyond normal scale. | Legendary encounters, cult attention, demi-god rumors, ruler interest. |
| Shame | The player is publicly embarrassed or morally stained. | Mocking songs, poor introductions, social penalties. |

#### World Popularity

World Popularity is a secret stat. It measures how widely a player matters to Huwan.

World Popularity is not the same as being good. A hero, tyrant, saint, war criminal, merchant prince, dragon tamer, crime lord, legendary crafter, beloved ruler, or infamous assassin can all gain World Popularity.

World Popularity affects:

- Legendary encounter odds
- Noble attention
- Guild invitations
- Faction recruitment
- Player faction growth
- Political marriage offers
- Contract offers
- Assassination attempts
- Bard songs
- Server rumors
- Market confidence
- Rival creation
- Hidden class/job clues
- Reincarnated soul echoes
- Demi-god path requirements
- Pantheon attention

World Popularity sources:

| Action | World Popularity Effect |
|---|---|
| First major dungeon clear | Major increase if publicly verified. |
| Dragon killed, befriended, tamed, sealed, or healed | Very large increase depending on outcome and witnesses. |
| Founding a successful faction | Gradual increase as faction grows. |
| Buying or ruling a city | Large increase with ongoing gain or loss based on governance. |
| Buying or ruling a kingdom | Massive increase and massive responsibility. |
| Winning major raids | Major increase based on contribution and publicity. |
| Creating legendary gear | Large increase if the item becomes known. |
| Ethical assassination of a corrupt power | Variable; can create fame, infamy, or secret underworld respect. |
| Public scandal | Fame and infamy increase, trust may fall. |
| Saving a poor district | Local love and possible world fame if story spreads. |
| Betraying a dragon treaty | Infamy, fear, and monster reputation damage. |
| Becoming top server champion | Major recurring increase. |
| Ten-year champion streak | Required part of demi-god path. |

#### Reputation Sources

Reputation can change through:

- Quest outcomes
- NPC memories
- Contracts
- Oaths
- Crimes
- Trials
- Bounties
- Bribes
- Assassinations
- Guild work
- Faction wars
- Settlement governance
- Taxes and laws
- Public services
- Romance and relationships
- Dragon-born dungeon outcomes
- Monster treatment
- Slime rights
- Dungeon farming ethics
- Resource overuse
- Crafting quality
- Market manipulation
- Charity
- Cruelty
- Public speeches
- Bard songs
- Rumors
- False accusations
- Legendary encounters
- Server announcements

#### Rumor Spread

Rumors are how reputation moves before official records catch up.

Rumor channels:

| Channel | Spread Style |
|---|---|
| Tavern Talk | Fast, exaggerated, emotional. |
| Market Gossip | Trade-focused, practical, price-sensitive. |
| Guild Boards | Formal, task-based, credibility varies by guild. |
| Bard Songs | Memorable, sticky, artistic, sometimes unfair. |
| Noble Letters | Political, selective, reputation-heavy. |
| Church Sermons | Moral framing, faith-based praise or condemnation. |
| Underworld Whispers | Secretive, transactional, dangerous. |
| Guard Reports | Legal, dry, used for warrants and suspicion. |
| Merchant Ledgers | Money-focused, trusted by banks and traders. |
| Player Forums | Server-wide, fast, chaotic, meta-aware. |
| Monster Memory | Slimes, dragons, undead, spirits, and intelligent monsters remember differently. |
| Divine Echo | Rare; blessings, curses, relics, and deity attention. |

Rumor quality:

| Quality | Meaning |
|---|---|
| True | Mostly accurate. |
| Exaggerated | Based on truth but inflated. |
| Incomplete | Missing key context. |
| False | Wrong or fabricated. |
| Planted | Created by a faction, noble, criminal, guild, bard, or rival. |
| Corrupted | Altered through fear, politics, magic, or bribery. |
| Sacred | Backed by oath, deity, dragon memory, relic, or truth magic. |

False rumors should be playable. A player can ignore them, fight them, sue over them, duel over them, hire bards to counter them, expose the source, bribe witnesses, use magic proof, or turn the rumor into a better story.

#### Titles, Nicknames, And Public Identity

Players can earn official titles, informal nicknames, mocking names, criminal names, guild names, faction names, noble names, and legendary epithets.

Title types:

| Type | Example |
|---|---|
| Guild Title | F-Rank Adventurer, Certified Cartographer, Master Smith. |
| Civic Title | Citizen Defender, Mayor, Councilor, Lord Protector. |
| Noble Title | Baron, Count, Marquis, Duke, King, Queen, Emperor. |
| Underworld Title | Cutpurse, Fixer, Shadow Broker, Crime Lord. |
| Dungeon Title | Floorbreaker, Maw-Walker, Dragon Heart Witness. |
| Dragon Title | Treaty Bearer, Hoard-Friend, Dungeon Lord, Dragon-Betrayer. |
| Religious Title | Blessed, Heretic, Oathkeeper, Cursemarked. |
| Monster Title | Slime-Safe, Beast-Friend, Undead Advocate, Wyrm-Offender. |
| Reputation Title | Hero, Tyrant, Folk Saint, Butcher, Peacemaker, Silver-Tongue. |
| Mocking Title | Wrong Door Champion, Slime Court Defendant, Bard's Favorite Mistake. |

Title rules:

- Titles can affect dialogue, prices, intimidation, romance, contracts, guild offers, and law.
- Some titles are hidden until discovered.
- Some titles are local and vanish outside the region.
- Some titles become server-known.
- Mocking titles can fade, become beloved, or transform into real legend.

#### Reputation Memory And Decay

Not every memory should last forever at full strength.

Memory types:

| Memory Type | Decay |
|---|---|
| Minor kindness | Fades into general goodwill unless repeated. |
| Minor insult | Fades unless tied to pride, status, or trauma. |
| Paid debt | Becomes trust if paid on time; becomes suspicion if late. |
| Broken oath | Long-lasting, especially if witnessed. |
| Public heroism | Becomes local history if repeated or sung. |
| Murder or betrayal | Long-lasting and may become permanent history. |
| Dragon outcome | Permanent or era-level memory. |
| City or kingdom rule | Long-term public record. |
| Demi-god progress | World-history memory. |

Reputation decay should not erase major history. It should compress old events into summaries, titles, public records, bard songs, rumors, scars, monuments, or legal archives.

#### Reputation Consequences

| Reputation State | Possible Consequences |
|---|---|
| Loved Locally | Free meals, tips, shelter, witnesses, discounts, warnings. |
| Trusted By Guild | Better quests, higher pay, sensitive contracts. |
| Feared By Criminals | Less petty crime, more assassination attempts, underworld respect. |
| Hated By Nobles | Court obstruction, inflated taxes, social snubs, smear campaigns. |
| Respected By Monsters | Safer negotiation, companion paths, monster-rights quests. |
| Infamous Killer | Guards watch, bounties appear, dark contracts improve. |
| Saintly Reputation | Priests support, corrupt factions test or exploit the image. |
| Known Liar | Contract difficulty, higher deposits, fewer witnesses. |
| Known Oathkeeper | Better pacts, dragon trust, divine interest. |
| Tyrant Ruler | Fear compliance, rebellion growth, assassination risk. |
| Beloved Ruler | Citizen defense, migration growth, stronger taxes through trust. |
| World Famous | Legendary attention, rivals, server rumors, pantheon notice. |

#### Reputation And Systems Integration

Reputation should integrate with:

- NPC dialogue
- Quest offers
- Contract Board
- Crime records
- Law proposals
- Courts
- Bribery
- Assassination contracts
- Faction diplomacy
- Guild rank
- Settlement ownership
- Buy Mode prices
- Build Mode permits
- Merchant discounts
- Auction confidence
- Dating, romance, and marriage
- Brothel social rumors
- Dragon relationships
- Dungeon access
- Legendary encounters
- Warlock pacts
- Divine blessings and curses
- Reincarnation
- Demi-god path

#### Reputation UI And HUD

Reputation should be visible enough to understand but not so exposed that mystery dies.

UI places:

- Character sheet reputation page
- Faction menu
- Guild menu
- Contract Board
- Crime record
- Settlement management
- Map region tooltips
- NPC dialogue hints
- Title list
- Rumor journal

Suggested visibility:

| Reputation Type | Visibility |
|---|---|
| Local reputation | Visible once discovered through NPCs or menus. |
| Guild reputation | Visible through guild halls and character sheet. |
| Faction reputation | Visible when faction relationship is known. |
| Underworld reputation | Hidden unless connected to underworld. |
| Monster reputation | Mostly hidden, shown through behavior and special menus. |
| Religious reputation | Visible through temples, blessings, curses, or priests. |
| World Popularity | Secret or partially revealed through milestones, titles, rumors, and late-game systems. |

#### Reputation Data Handoff

Recommended reputation data fields:

| Field | Purpose |
|---|---|
| ReputationId | Unique reputation record. |
| OwnerId | Player, party, faction, NPC, settlement, or guild. |
| Scope | Personal, local, city, guild, faction, race, kingdom, underworld, monster, world. |
| TargetId | Who holds the opinion. |
| Fame | How widely known the owner is. |
| Infamy | How widely feared or morally condemned the owner is. |
| Respect | Skill, honor, or competence recognition. |
| Fear | Perceived danger. |
| Trust | Belief that promises will be kept. |
| Suspicion | Doubt, secrecy, or concern. |
| Love | Emotional loyalty or affection. |
| Hate | Hostility or resentment. |
| Awe | Legendary or mythic perception. |
| Shame | Public embarrassment or moral stain. |
| WorldPopularity | Hidden global recognition value. |
| SourceEvents | Events that created or changed the reputation. |
| RumorState | True, false, planted, exaggerated, sacred, corrupted. |
| Titles | Earned, active, hidden, mocked, or revoked titles. |
| DecayRules | How the reputation changes over time. |
| Visibility | Visible, hidden, partial, rumor-only, underworld-only. |
| LastUpdated | Server time of last change. |

#### Reputation Implementation Checklist

Reputation is implementation-ready when the game supports:

- Multiple reputation scopes.
- Fame, infamy, respect, fear, trust, suspicion, love, hate, awe, and shame tracks.
- Hidden World Popularity.
- NPC memory feeding reputation.
- Rumor generation and spread.
- False rumor handling.
- Bard songs and public narrative.
- Titles and nicknames.
- Reputation decay and compression.
- Reputation changes from crime, contracts, law, factions, settlements, dungeons, dragons, markets, and relationships.
- UI pages for known reputation.
- Hidden reputation for underworld, monsters, and secret factions.
- Server rumors and announcements.
- World Popularity milestones.
- Reincarnation memory echoes.
- Demi-god path requirements.

#### Final Reputation Rules

- Reputation is not morality. It is memory, interpretation, gossip, proof, fear, love, and story.
- A player can be hero and villain at the same time, depending on who is speaking.
- Fame opens doors and paints targets.
- Infamy scares enemies and poisons trust.
- Trust is harder to earn than fame.
- False rumors should create quests, not just frustration.
- World Popularity is the world beginning to recognize that the player matters.
- The highest form of reputation is not being liked by everyone. It is becoming impossible for Huwan to ignore.

## Relationships, Romance, Family, And Legacy

Relationships are the emotional engine of Huwan. Reputation is what the world says about a player, but relationships are what individual people, families, factions, companions, spouses, rivals, children, patrons, monsters, dragons, and communities actually feel and remember.

This is the system that makes the story personal.

### Relationships, Romance, Family, And Legacy System Completion V1.0

Core rule:

- **Relationships determine the story.**
- A relationship can open a quest, close a door, start a war, prevent a murder, create a family, save a city, corrupt a faction, tame a monster, expose a crime, or echo into reincarnation.
- Romance, family, childbirth, and intimacy should remain PG-13 in presentation. The game can support mature themes, attraction, marriage, heirs, pregnancy, adoption, family drama, and legacy without explicit sexual depiction or graphic childbirth.

#### Relationship Tracks

Relationships are not one number. A character can love the player but distrust their politics, fear their power but respect their honor, or hate their faction while owing them a life debt.

| Track | Meaning | Gameplay Use |
|---|---|---|
| Trust | Belief that the player keeps promises. | Contracts, secrets, romance, delegation, dragon pacts. |
| Affection | Personal warmth, fondness, friendship, or care. | Companionship, gifts, family, social support. |
| Love | Deep emotional attachment or romance. | Marriage, soul mate paths, sacrifice, jealousy, heartbreak. |
| Respect | Recognition of skill, honor, strength, wisdom, or leadership. | Mentorship, politics, guild access, rival admiration. |
| Fear | Belief that the player is dangerous. | Intimidation, obedience, avoidance, rebellion risk. |
| Desire | Romantic or physical attraction between eligible adults. | Courtship, mate discovery, jealousy, romantic tension. |
| Loyalty | Willingness to stand by the player or group. | Companions, faction members, guards, family, tamed creatures. |
| Debt | One side owes the other. | Favors, rescue, repayment, coercion risk. |
| Resentment | Stored anger, bitterness, envy, or humiliation. | Betrayal, rivalry, sabotage, breakup risk. |
| Jealousy | Fear of losing status, love, attention, power, or inheritance. | Romance conflict, noble drama, faction disputes. |
| Bond | Magical, emotional, familial, soul, tamed, or pact-based connection. | Shared abilities, loyalty, pain, commands, legacy effects. |
| Control | Forced obedience through power, law, fear, magic, ownership, runes, or domination. | Ethical risk, rebellion, legal consequence, backlash. |

#### Relationship States

| State | Meaning | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Stranger | No meaningful relationship. | Default state. |
| Acquaintance | Recognizes the player. | Basic dialogue and memory. |
| Friendly | Warm or helpful. | Small favors, better conversation. |
| Friend | Personal trust and care. | Private quests, support, social defense. |
| Best Friend | Deep platonic bond. | Sacrifice, loyalty, unique quests. |
| Rival | Competitive relationship. | Can be hostile, friendly, romantic, or respectful. |
| Enemy | Active hostility. | Attacks, sabotage, lawsuits, faction conflict. |
| Mentor | Teaches skills, values, or worldview. | Training, tests, guidance. |
| Student | Learns from player or NPC. | Legacy, teaching, responsibility. |
| Ally | Shared goals. | Quests, war, politics, contracts. |
| Guildmate | Shared guild identity. | Rank, duty, professional trust. |
| Factionmate | Shared faction identity. | Group loyalty, politics, internal drama. |
| Partner | Romantic relationship. | Courtship, dates, jealousy, shared future. |
| Spouse | Marriage or recognized life partnership. | Family, inheritance, housing, politics. |
| Soul Mate | Extremely rare deep compatibility or destiny bond. | Strong story effects, high emotional stakes. |
| Bonded | Magical, emotional, bloodline, pact, or life-bonded connection. | Shared consequences, abilities, or pain. |
| Family | Blood, adoption, marriage, household, clan, or chosen family. | Legacy, inheritance, protection, obligation. |
| Parent | Guardian or biological/adoptive parent. | Children, heirs, family quests. |
| Child | Minor family member, protected NPC, heir, or dependent. | PG-13 family care, education, protection. |
| Tamed | Animal, monster, slime, dragon, or beast-like being accepts care or command. | Companion systems, trust, breeding where appropriate. |
| Controlled | Forced obedience through magic, law, fear, ownership, or systems. | Moral risk, resistance buildup, backlash. |
| Slave | Coercive ownership status where a person is treated as property. | Serious moral, legal, social, rebellion, and rescue consequences. |

Slave and controlled states should be treated as dangerous and morally loaded. They can exist in the world as injustice, crime, tyranny, or dark magic, but they should create consequences, resistance, rescue quests, legal conflict, reputation damage, and faction reactions.

#### Romance And Courtship

Romance is available for adult eligible characters of all sexual orientations where both sides consent and the relationship is allowed by law, culture, or story context.

Romance can begin through:

- Friendship
- Shared quests
- Gifts
- Dates
- Saving someone
- Working together
- Faction life
- Guild duty
- Marriage arrangement
- Noble politics
- Soul mate events
- Rivalry
- Magical bond
- Long-term trust

Romance stages:

| Stage | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Interest | Attraction or curiosity begins. |
| Flirtation | Light romantic dialogue, gifts, tension, or attention. |
| Courtship | Intentional dating, visits, shared time, social checks. |
| Partnered | Recognized romantic relationship. |
| Committed | Long-term bond, shared plans, public or private promise. |
| Engaged | Marriage or life-bond contract pending. |
| Married | Legal, spiritual, cultural, or faction-recognized union. |
| Soul-Bound | Rare deeper bond through story, magic, reincarnation, or destiny. |
| Strained | Conflict, jealousy, neglect, betrayal, or incompatible goals. |
| Broken | Breakup, divorce, death, betrayal, disappearance, or annulment. |

Romance should affect:

- Dialogue
- Home life
- Gifts
- Jealousy
- Rivalries
- Marriage contracts
- Noble alliances
- Inheritance
- Faction diplomacy
- NPC loyalty
- Quest decisions
- Relocation
- Children and adoption
- Reputation
- Assassination, kidnapping, blackmail, or rescue risks

#### Social Needs And Mate Discovery

Players should track social needs alongside hunger and thirst.

Social systems:

- Low social fulfillment can create loneliness, stress, reduced morale, and weaker relationship growth.
- High social fulfillment can improve morale, charm opportunities, party trust, and romance discovery.
- Social ranks help players find friends, allies, dates, mentors, rivals, family paths, or soul mate possibilities.
- Certain abilities, such as Born Leader, improve social growth with allies.
- Certain reputations can attract or repel potential partners.

Mate discovery should depend on:

- Compatibility
- Orientation
- Age eligibility
- Consent
- Relationship history
- Reputation
- Values
- Race/culture acceptance
- Social rank
- Housing
- Stability
- Faction and family pressure
- Story events

#### Marriage, Contracts, And Political Bonds

Marriage can be personal, romantic, political, religious, financial, factional, or noble.

Marriage types:

| Type | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Love Marriage | Built on romance and mutual choice. |
| Political Marriage | Builds alliance, noble claim, succession, or peace. |
| Contract Marriage | Defines property, inheritance, duties, or social terms. |
| Religious Marriage | Recognized by church, deity, oath, or temple. |
| Faction Marriage | Strengthens faction ties or internal stability. |
| Secret Marriage | Hidden from law, family, faction, or enemies. |
| Soul Bond | Rare bond that may exceed normal law. |
| Dragon, Fae, Or Magical Pact Marriage | Unusual bond governed by supernatural rules. |

Marriage can affect:

- Shared housing
- Inheritance
- Family reputation
- Noble titles
- Land claims
- Taxes
- Social rank
- Faction diplomacy
- Succession
- Children
- Adoption
- Debt
- Contract Board obligations
- Divorce or annulment consequences

#### PG-13 Childbirth And Family Growth

Childbirth and family growth should be handled with care and kept PG-13.

Presentation rules:

- No explicit sexual scenes.
- No graphic childbirth depiction.
- Pregnancy, birth, adoption, and family planning are handled through dialogue, time skips, medical visits, household events, temple blessings, healer care, journal updates, and birth announcements.
- Brothel systems remain non-explicit and do not depict sexual acts.
- Child characters are never sexualized.
- Children are protected family/citizen NPCs and should be used for care, education, protection, inheritance, and emotional stakes, not adult content.

Family growth paths:

| Path | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Birth | Adult partners have a child through natural family progression. |
| Adoption | Player or NPC adopts a child, orphan, heir, or ward. |
| Step-Family | Marriage or partnership joins families. |
| Chosen Family | Emotional family without blood or law. |
| Magical Birth Event | Rare, story-driven, deity, fae, dragon, reincarnation, or relic-related child event. |
| Lineage Recognition | A hidden child, lost heir, or bloodline is discovered. |
| Guardian Role | Player becomes responsible for a minor without formal adoption yet. |

Childbirth flow:

1. Adult relationship reaches family-planning eligibility.
2. Both sides consent through dialogue or contract state.
3. Household, housing, finances, health, race compatibility, law, and social pressure are checked.
4. Pregnancy or family-growth state begins.
5. Healers, priests, family, and household NPCs may generate support quests.
6. Time passes using living server time.
7. Birth or adoption event resolves off-screen or through PG-13 scene framing.
8. Child name, lineage, traits, family record, inheritance state, and household needs are created.
9. Family reputation, laws, titles, and legacy hooks update.

#### Children, Heirs, And Protection

Children should matter without becoming ordinary combat pawns.

Child systems:

- Name
- Age and life stage
- Lineage
- Family ties
- Guardian
- Home
- Education
- Health
- Personality seed
- Mana well potential
- Race/sub-race inheritance
- Social needs
- Safety needs
- Future job/class interests
- Inheritance claims
- Relationship memory

Children can generate quests around:

- Education
- Illness
- Missing family
- Adoption
- Noble inheritance
- Bullying
- Apprenticeship
- Kidnapping threats
- Refugee safety
- Family disputes
- Heir legitimacy
- Mana well training

Child protection rules:

- Children cannot be romance targets.
- Children cannot be brothel participants.
- Children cannot be used for explicit adult content.
- Child harm should be handled carefully and sparingly, focused on rescue, prevention, protection, and consequence rather than spectacle.
- Players who exploit, enslave, abandon, or harm children should face severe reputation, legal, faction, religious, and moral consequences.

#### Heirs And Inheritance

Heirs can inherit more than property.

Inheritance can include:

- Land
- Homes
- Titles
- Noble claims
- Faction leadership
- Guild shops
- Debt
- Contracts
- Curses
- Relics
- Family weapons
